## 2026-08-06 — Full security audit

A line-by-line audit of every file in `backend/` and `frontend/` against authentication/session security, authorisation, injection, data security, infrastructure, API security, frontend security, and dependency vulnerabilities. Full findings and remediation are documented in [security.md](security.md); the decisions below cover fixes with a design tradeoff worth recording.

**Decision:** `config.py`'s `jwt_secret` and `mfa_encryption_key` no longer have a functional default — they default to `""`, and `main.py`'s new `_validate_security_config()` refuses to start the app (raises before the first request is served) if either is missing, too short, or matches a known placeholder/leaked value.
**Rationale:** The previous defaults were a plain "change-me" string for `jwt_secret` and, worse, an actual working Fernet key for `mfa_encryption_key` committed directly in `config.py` — any deployment that forgot to override it via `.env` was silently signing JWTs with, and encrypting every MFA secret under, a key visible to anyone who reads this repository. Failing loudly at startup is disruptive to a misconfigured deployment, but continuing to serve traffic on a publicly known key isn't "working," it's an unprotected system that looks protected — and this repo's own `.env` already has real generated values for both, so this only bites deployments that genuinely never set them.

**Decision:** Rate limiting on `POST /auth/login`, `POST /auth/verify-mfa`, and `POST /auth/register` (`services/rate_limit_service.py`) is keyed by client IP address, not username, and reuses the `system_events` table's existing durable-counter pattern (the same one `system_reset_service` and `ai_provider_service` already use) rather than an in-memory counter or a new dependency.
**Rationale:** Keying by IP means an attacker spamming a real user's username can't use the rate limit itself to lock that user out (a limiter keyed by username would do exactly that). A DB-backed counter survives a backend restart and works the same if this is ever scaled to more than one backend process, matching the precedent already set for `POST /system/reset` and `POST /ai/test`.

**Decision:** A password change (self-service, forced, or an Admin's reset) now stamps a new per-user `users.token_invalid_before` column (migration `0027`), checked in `utils/deps.get_current_user` the same way the existing global `JWT_INVALIDATE_BEFORE_KEY` setting already is — but `PUT /me/password` and `PUT /me/force-password-change` also now return a **fresh** access token (`PasswordChangeOut`) alongside the updated user, rather than leaving the caller's now-invalidated token in place.
**Rationale:** JWTs are stateless and otherwise have no per-user revocation mechanism (see the original auth build's own "logout is stateless" decision above) — without this, a stolen token would keep working for up to its full 8-hour life even after the leaked password was changed. Re-issuing a fresh token for the session that just proved it knows the new password (rather than forcing that same tab to immediately re-authenticate) avoids the alternative of breaking `ProfilePage`'s and `ChangePasswordPage`'s very next API call with a confusing "session invalidated" error moments after a successful save — confirmed this would in fact happen with a live test (see Tested below) before adding the token re-issue. Verified live: an admin password reset or an Admin-forced reactivation both correctly invalidate a *different* user's existing sessions the same way.

**Decision:** `main.py` adds a hard request-body-size ceiling (`max_request_body_bytes`, 10 MB; `max_upload_body_bytes`, 50 MB for `/invoices/upload` and the two `/storage/restore*` routes) checked against `Content-Length` in a middleware, ahead of any endpoint-specific validation.
**Rationale:** No limit existed anywhere in the stack — FastAPI/Starlette impose none by default. A `Content-Length`-based check is coarse (it doesn't stop a client that lies about the header and streams more anyway, or one that omits it and uses chunked transfer), but it's the same class of defence a reverse proxy would normally provide in front of this app, and this app doesn't assume one is there.

**Decision:** Invoice PDF upload validation (`utils/file_validation.py`) now checks the file's own magic bytes (`%PDF-` within the first 1024 bytes) rather than trusting the browser-supplied `Content-Type` header or the `.pdf` filename extension, and rejects anything over 25 MB. Uploaded filenames are also sanitised (control characters, including CR/LF, stripped) before being stored or ever echoed into a response header.
**Rationale:** Both the content type and extension are entirely client-controlled and were the *only* check in place — a non-PDF file renamed to `invoice.pdf` would previously sail straight through to PyMuPDF and the AI extraction call, and an attacker-chosen filename could otherwise land unescaped in a later `Content-Disposition` header. (Starlette's `FileResponse` already RFC-5987-encodes an unsafe filename on the way out, so this wasn't independently exploitable there, but sanitising at the point of ingestion is the correct place to fix it once rather than relying on that downstream behaviour staying true.)

**Decision:** `backup_service.restore_from_zip`'s file-restore loop now resolves each entry's destination path and refuses anything that would land outside `storage_root`, rather than joining the zip's own internal path directly onto disk.
**Rationale:** This is a classic zip-slip: `rel = name[len("files/"):]` came straight from the uploaded zip's own file list, which is attacker-controlled if the "backup" wasn't produced by this app's own `create_backup_zip` (a tampered file, or one from an untrusted source, handed to a Superadmin to restore). Restore is already Superadmin-only and password-gated, but a Superadmin restoring a zip they didn't produce themselves shouldn't be able to have it write files anywhere the backend process can write, just by naming an entry `files/../../../etc/cron.d/x`.

**Decision:** Free-text user input rendered into a generated PDF (invoice supplier/notes, category/group/project names, usernames, report titles, AI-written report summaries) is now escaped (`utils/pdf_text.py`, `xml.sax.saxutils.escape`) before being handed to ReportLab's `Paragraph`.
**Rationale:** `Paragraph` parses its text as a small XML-like markup dialect — `<img src="...">` in particular can reference an external URL that ReportLab will fetch while rendering. A crafted supplier name or report title could otherwise inject markup into every generated invoice/reconciliation export and AI report, including triggering an outbound request (SSRF) the moment someone opens or re-generates that PDF. `canvas.drawString` calls (plain text, no markup parsing) were left alone — only `Paragraph()` call sites needed this.

**Decision:** CSV exports (`utils/csv_export.py`) now prefix any cell starting with `=`, `@`, a tab, or a carriage return with a single quote, and prefix `+`/`-` only when *not* immediately followed by a digit.
**Rationale:** The classic CSV/formula-injection issue — a supplier name or note starting with `=` or `@` would otherwise execute as a formula in whoever's spreadsheet software opens the export. OWASP's own guidance also flags a bare leading `+`/`-`, but this app legitimately exports negative numbers as pre-formatted strings (a reconciliation discrepancy, a year-on-year change) — escaping those unconditionally would turn a real figure into text. Only escaping a sign that *isn't* immediately followed by a digit catches the formula case (`-cmd|...`, `+HYPERLINK(...)`) without mangling `-15.00`.

**Decision:** `CORSMiddleware` now reads its allowed origins from a new `CORS_ORIGINS` env var (comma-separated, default `http://localhost:3000`) instead of `allow_origins=["*"]`.
**Rationale:** Flagged and deliberately left for later in the original auth build ("worth tightening... when deployment URLs are known" — see above); wildcard-plus-credentials is both broader than any real deployment needs and a combination browsers themselves reject. `.env`/`.env.example` were updated to match.

**Decision:** Both Dockerfiles now run as a non-root user (`keeptrack` in the backend image, the pre-existing `node` user in the frontend image), and `docker-compose.yml`'s Postgres port is now published on `127.0.0.1` only instead of every interface.
**Rationale:** Neither service needs root — running as one is more privilege than an API server or a Vite dev server needs and a needless amplifier for any future code-execution bug. Postgres was reachable directly from any host on the network before this change, entirely bypassing the application layer; the backend only ever needs it over the internal Docker network. **Operational note, not silently handled:** an *already-running* deployment's named volumes (`invoice_storage`, `report_storage`, `watched_folder`) were created under the old root-running container and are still owned by root — switching to non-root will hit permission errors writing to them until an operator runs a one-off `docker compose run --user root backend chown -R keeptrack:keeptrack /data` (or recreates the volumes on a fresh deployment, which needs no such step). Verified directly: this repository's own dev stack needed exactly the migration step above, not the chown, since its volumes were fresh enough — see Tested below.

**Decision:** Backend dependency bumps — `python-jose[cryptography]` 3.3.0 → 3.5.0, `python-multipart` 0.0.9 → 0.0.32, `fastapi` 0.115.0 → 0.141.1 (pulling `starlette` 0.38.6 → 1.4.1 transitively) — were applied and verified against the live dev stack; `ecdsa` (a transitive dependency, PYSEC-2026-1325) was left as-is since upstream has no fix and this app never performs an ECDSA operation (JWTs are HS256-only throughout).
**Rationale:** `python-jose` 3.3.0 carried a JWE decompression-bomb DoS reachable simply by POSTing a crafted token to any endpoint that decodes one (`utils/security.decode_token`), regardless of this app only ever issuing HS256 JWS tokens itself — the vulnerable code path is in *decoding*, not signing. `python-multipart` and `starlette` both had multipart/request-parsing CVEs directly reachable through this app's own file-upload endpoints. The `fastapi` jump was large (26 minor versions) and was not applied blindly — see Tested below for what was actually exercised against it before this was committed.

**Not addressed in this pass:** `frontend/package.json`'s `vite`/`esbuild` (dev-server-only CORS/path-traversal CVEs) and `react-router-dom` (an open-redirect CVE in `<Link>`/`useNavigate`) both have fixes available only via a major-version bump (Vite 5→8, React Router 6→7) that `npm audit fix` itself reports as breaking. Neither was applied: this environment has no browser-automation tooling to regression-test all 15+ routes/guards after a router major-version bump, and a blind major bump to the build tool risks breaking `vite.config.js`/the dev workflow in ways that wouldn't surface until someone actually opens the app. Flagged for a follow-up pass with real browser testing available. Separately: this app's `docker-compose.yml` runs `vite dev` as its actual "frontend" service rather than a production `vite build` served statically — the Vite dev server was never designed to be internet-facing, and the specific CVEs here are one symptom of that, not the whole risk; a real production deployment (the k3s manifests layered on top, per `docs/architecture.md`) should build static assets and serve them from a proper static file server rather than running the dev server at all.

**Not addressed in this pass:** JWT access tokens are stored in `localStorage` (`frontend/src/hooks/AuthContext.jsx`), which any successful XSS on the page could read and exfiltrate. No `dangerouslySetInnerHTML`, `eval`, or other injection sink exists anywhere in the frontend (checked directly), and the new strict CSP (`script-src 'self'`, no inline scripts) closes off the most likely injection vector — but moving to an httpOnly cookie would remove the class of risk entirely rather than mitigate it. Not done in this pass: it's a genuine architecture change (CSRF protection, `SameSite` cookie config, every API call, and the backend's own auth flow would all need to change together), disproportionate to fix blind alongside everything else here without dedicated testing.

**Not addressed in this pass:** the real `.env` this deployment is already running has a functional `JWT_SECRET`/`MFA_ENCRYPTION_KEY` (confirmed non-placeholder without printing their values), but `SUPERADMIN_PASSWORD` and `POSTGRES_PASSWORD` are both low-entropy dictionary-style values. Not changed here — rotating either is a credential change with real lockout/downtime risk best left to whoever operates this instance, not silently rewritten mid-audit.

**Tested:** Verified against the real running dev containers (`docker compose`), not just read through. Ran `alembic upgrade head` (migration `c6d7e8f9a0b1` applied cleanly, added `users.token_invalid_before`). Rebuilt both the backend and frontend images against the new Dockerfiles and dependency versions; confirmed via `docker exec ... whoami` that both now run as non-root (`keeptrack`, `node`). Confirmed `docker compose ps` shows Postgres bound to `127.0.0.1:5432` only. Hit `GET /health` (200, all five new security headers present). Exercised CORS directly: a preflight from `http://localhost:3000` gets `access-control-allow-origin` back, one from `http://evil.example.com` is rejected (400, no allow-origin header). Confirmed the request-size middleware rejects an oversized login payload with 413. Ran a real login/password-change/re-login cycle against the Superadmin account: old token issued 2 seconds before a password change is rejected (401) afterward, the freshly-returned token from the same request keeps working (200) — and separately confirmed the documented same-second floor-comparison edge case (old and new tokens issued in the same wall-clock second don't invalidate each other, mirroring the existing global-invalidation mechanism's own documented tradeoff, not a new one). Confirmed repeated failed logins return 401 each time (rate-limit counter incrementing, verified against `system_events` directly). Uploaded a plain-text file renamed to `.pdf` and confirmed it's rejected ("not a valid PDF file") rather than reaching extraction/storage. Confirmed `GET /openapi.json` still resolves (200) — validates every router and Pydantic schema, including the new `PasswordChangeOut`, imports cleanly against `fastapi==0.141.1`. Ran the full backend test suite of curl checks above with real data already in the dev database (6 confirmed invoices, contributions, a financial year) and confirmed `GET /dashboard/summary` and `GET /invoices/export/csv` both still return correct, complete data. `pip-audit` was re-run against the final `requirements.txt` after every bump and confirmed clean except the documented, unfixed `ecdsa` entry. `npm audit` was run and `npm audit fix` (non-forcing) attempted; confirmed it made no changes (git diff empty) since the two remaining findings both require the major-version bumps documented above as not addressed in this pass. The pre-existing `watched-folder-watcher` container's restart loop (a placeholder service, not yet implemented) was confirmed present *before* any change in this pass and is unrelated to it.

## 2026-08-06 — Suppress zero-entry maintenance notifications

**Decision:** The quarterly audit-log-archive run still always writes its summary `AuditLogArchive` row (`entries_archived` in `extra_metadata`, alongside the existing description) even when it archives zero entries, so `GET /logs/status` and the Audit Log tab still show the run happened — but `routers/dashboard.py`'s live "audit_log_archived" notification now only fires when that count is greater than zero.
**Rationale:** A dashboard banner reading "0 entries archived" tells an Admin nothing actionable — it's noise on every quiet quarter where the 90-day retention window simply had nothing to sweep. Keeping the underlying `AuditLogArchive` row unconditional preserves the existing "task still ran" record (`services/maintenance_service.py`'s own documented rationale for why this goes to `audit_log_archive` rather than `audit_log`, see the invoice/PDF-signing entries above) — only the derived notification is gated.

**Decision:** No equivalent change was made for error-log cleanup's "notification."
**Rationale:** Checked directly: no dashboard notification for `error_log.cleaned` exists anywhere in the app today — `routers/dashboard.py` has no notification block for it, and `frontend/src/pages/DashboardPage.jsx` only special-cases `audit_log_archived`. The cleanup's `last_error_cleanup_run` timestamp is surfaced solely as a status line in `ErrorLogTab.jsx` (Settings → Logs), not as a notification. `maintenance_service.py`'s own docstring confirms only the audit-archive path was ever built with a "send an Admin notification" requirement. Since there's nothing to gate, the already-correct behaviour (cleanup runs and logs to `audit_log` via `audit_service.log_action` regardless of count, with no dashboard notification either way) needed no code change.

---

## 2026-08-06 — Error log management and scheduled backup test button

**Decision:** `error_log.resolved_note` was built as `VARCHAR(500)`, not the plain unbounded "string" the task brief listed.
**Rationale:** Every other free-text note-ish column added in this app's history (`monthly_reconciliations.discrepancy_notes` aside, which predates this convention) and `audit_log.description` itself cap at a bounded `VARCHAR` rather than `TEXT`, since these are short human annotations, not documents. 500 matches `audit_log.description`'s own limit, which a resolution note is functionally similar to.

**Decision:** `PUT /logs/errors/{id}/unresolve` does not write an `audit_log` entry, even though `resolve` and both `clear-*` endpoints do.
**Rationale:** The task brief's BACKEND section explicitly lists "Logs to audit_log" under `resolve`, `clear-all`, and `clear-selected`, but not under `unresolve`. Read literally rather than adding logging by analogy — unresolving is also the lower-stakes, reversible half of the pair (it only un-does a resolve, which is itself already audit-logged), unlike the three destructive/decisive actions that do get logged.

**Decision:** The FRONTEND's "Resolve selected" bulk action calls the existing single-item `PUT /logs/errors/{id}/resolve` endpoint once per selected row (via `Promise.all`) rather than a new bulk-resolve endpoint.
**Rationale:** The task brief's BACKEND section lists no bulk-resolve endpoint — only single resolve/unresolve and the two clear-* endpoints (which the FRONTEND's "Delete selected" does map onto directly, since `clear-selected` already exists for exactly that). Composing the single-item endpoint client-side satisfies the FRONTEND's literal "Resolve selected" button requirement without adding backend surface the brief didn't ask for. Each call is still individually audit-logged (`error_log.resolved`), so a bulk resolve of N rows produces N audit entries rather than a single misleading "bulk" one — arguably more useful for tracing exactly which entries an Admin acted on.

**Decision:** `GET /dashboard/notifications`'s critical-error notification message was changed from the previously-fixed "Critical errors detected — please review the error log in Settings." to a live count: `"{N} unresolved critical errors detected — please review the error log in Settings."` (singular "error" when N=1).
**Rationale:** Directly matches this task's literal requirement ("Notification text updated to: 'X unresolved critical errors detected...'"). The count was already being computed for the `if critical_count:` gate: surfacing it costs nothing extra and gives an Admin a sense of scale before they click through.

**Decision:** `POST /storage/backup/run-scheduled` reuses `backup_service.perform_backup(db, "scheduled", ...)` — the exact function the background scheduler thread calls — rather than a separate "test backup" code path, and also calls `apply_retention()` afterwards exactly as `maintenance_service.run_scheduled_backup_check` does.
**Rationale:** The task brief calls this a way to "test a scheduled backup path without waiting for the schedule to trigger" — a test is only meaningful if it exercises the real path. A bespoke "preview" implementation could pass while the real 2am scheduled run still failed (e.g. a `backup_path` that's writable now but wasn't when the schedule was last saved). Running retention afterward too means the button's behaviour — including a same-run pruning of old scheduled backups beyond the retention count — matches what actually happens at 2am, not a lighter-weight approximation of it.

**Decision:** The manual-backup button was relabelled from "Create manual backup" to "Create backup" (`StorageBackupSettings.jsx`), matching the FRONTEND section's literal naming pair ("Manual backup: 'Create backup'" / "Scheduled test: 'Run scheduled backup now'").
**Rationale:** The brief calls out this exact pair of labels specifically to keep the two actions visually distinct from each other — download-to-browser vs. save-to-configured-path — so matching the literal strings rather than paraphrasing was treated as intentional, not incidental.

**Tested:** Verified against the real running dev containers. Ran `alembic upgrade head` (migration `d7e8f9a0b1c2` applied cleanly, added `error_log.resolved`/`resolved_by`/`resolved_at`/`resolved_note`). Restarted the backend and confirmed clean startup (no import errors). Exercised every new endpoint directly with `curl` against a real Superadmin session: created a critical test error, confirmed `GET /dashboard/notifications` reported "1 unresolved critical error", resolved it with a note (response carried `resolved_by_display_name`), confirmed the dashboard notification count dropped from 4 to 3 against the app's real pre-existing critical errors, unresolved it back and confirmed `resolved`/`resolved_by`/`resolved_at`/`resolved_note` all cleared, exercised `resolved=unresolved`/`resolved=resolved` list filters, confirmed `clear-all` rejects a wrong confirmation phrase with `400` and succeeds with the correct one (deleting all rows and clearing the dashboard notification entirely), confirmed `clear-selected` deletes only the given ids, and confirmed both actions write the expected `error_log.cleared` audit entries. For the backup endpoint: confirmed `POST /storage/backup/run-scheduled` returns `400` with the exact specified message when `backup_path` is temporarily cleared, and returns `success`/`file_path`/`file_size_bytes`/`file_size_human`/`duration_seconds` with a real file written to the configured path when it is configured, with a matching `backup.scheduled_manual_trigger` audit entry. Drove the full frontend flow with a headless-Chromium Playwright script against the real dev server (no project browser-automation skill existed yet, so a one-off script was used rather than authoring a new skill for a single verification pass): logged in as Superadmin, exercised the All/Unresolved/Resolved filter tabs, opened and submitted the inline resolve form, confirmed the green Resolved badge/name/date/note render and Unresolve works, checked two rows and confirmed the bulk action bar appears with working Resolve selected/Delete selected(+confirm), opened the Clear all modal and confirmed the confirm button stays disabled against a wrong phrase, and on Storage & Backup confirmed "Run scheduled backup now" is enabled (a path was already configured), shows a spinner, and lands on a green success message with the real file path/size/duration. No console errors beyond a pre-existing, unrelated CSP `frame-ancestors`-in-`<meta>` browser warning. Test error-log rows and the two test-triggered backup zip files were cleaned up afterwards via the app's own endpoints so no test artifacts were left in the dev database or backup destination.

## 2026-08-06 — Planned projects future years and reconciliation staleness flag

**Decision:** `GET /projects/financial-years` (not in the original endpoint list) returns exactly three `FinancialYearOut` rows — current, next, and year-after-next — auto-creating the latter two quietly via the existing `financial_year_service.get_or_create_financial_year()` if they don't exist yet, with no audit log entry.
**Rationale:** The task asked for the planned-project form's financial year dropdown to always offer three years ahead, not just whatever happens to already exist in `financial_years` (which, per the 2026-08-04 dashboard build entry, are otherwise only ever created lazily on first reference from the dashboard/contributions/reconciliation pages). "Quietly" was read as "no `audit_service.log_action` call," matching how `get_or_create_financial_year` itself has never logged its own lazy-creation of the current year either — creating the row is bookkeeping, not a user action worth an audit trail entry.

**Decision:** The next/year-after-next financial years are computed by adding 1 and 2 calendar years to the *current* financial year's own `start_date` (`date(start.year + N, start.month, 1)`), then feeding each date back through `get_or_create_financial_year`, rather than re-deriving bounds from `today()` shifted by N years.
**Rationale:** `fy_bounds_for` determines which FY a date falls in from the configured `financial_year_start_month`; walking forward from the *already-resolved* current FY's start date (always day 1, so no Feb 29 edge case) guarantees the three years returned are always consecutive and non-overlapping, even in the one day of the year where "today plus 1 year" could theoretically land in a different relative position within its FY than today does within the current one.

**Decision:** The frontend's financial year `<select>` labels each option as `"Current — FY2025/26 (Sep–Aug)"` / `"Next — FY2026/27 (Sep–Aug)"` / `"Year after next — FY2027/28 (Sep–Aug)"`, reusing the FY label the backend already computes (which already embeds the month range) rather than building a separate "Sep–Aug"-style string client-side. When editing a project whose `financial_year_id` isn't one of the three (e.g. an older year), the select gets one extra, otherwise-hidden option so the existing value still pre-selects correctly, labelled `FY #<id>` — the same fallback string `ProjectCard` already used for an unrecognised financial year.
**Rationale:** Matches the task's literal "label each option clearly with the year range" requirement using data the backend already returns, and avoids silently resetting a project's financial year to blank on open-to-edit just because it predates the current lazy-creation window.

**Decision:** Staleness tracking (`monthly_reconciliations.is_stale`/`stale_reason`/`stale_since`, migration `a0b1c2d3e4f5`) is set by a single shared `reconciliation_service.mark_reconciliation_stale(db, month, financial_year_id, reason)`, called from `contributions.py` (create/edit/delete) and `invoices.py` (confirm/edit/delete) after each `db.commit()`, and cleared only inside `reconciliation.py`'s existing Admin-only `actual_balance` correction branch of `PUT /reconciliation/{id}`.
**Rationale:** Directly implements the task's own call list. Gating on the *existing* Admin-correction branch (rather than any edit to a reconciliation) matches the task's explicit reasoning — "the Admin has acknowledged and corrected it" — since that's the only path that actually recalculates `calculated_balance`/`discrepancy` against current data; saving discrepancy notes alone doesn't touch the figures the staleness flag is warning about, so it doesn't clear it.

**Decision:** Invoice-driven staleness only fires when the invoice is (or was) `reviewed`, and an invoice edit that changes `invoice_date` marks *both* the old and new month stale (each independently, since a reconciliation might exist for one, both, or neither).
**Rationale:** `calculated_balance_for_month` only ever sums invoices with `reviewed = true` (see the 2026-08-04 contributions/reconciliation build entry), so editing or deleting an invoice still awaiting review can't have changed any past reconciliation's figures — flagging one anyway would be a false alarm with no real cause to point to. An `invoice_date` edit moves the invoice's amount out of one month's calculated balance and into another's, so both months' reconciliations (if either exists) need the same warning.

**Decision:** A bare `contributions.month` (1-12, no calendar year of its own) is converted to a full `date` via a new `reconciliation_service.calendar_month_date(fy, month_number)`, which walks the financial year's own `month_sequence()` to find the matching calendar year — the same lookup pattern `contributions.py`'s `_month_label_lookup` already used for CSV/PDF export month labels.
**Rationale:** `mark_reconciliation_stale` needs a real `date` to match against `monthly_reconciliations.month` (itself a `DATE`, per the 2026-08-04 reconciliation build entry), and a contribution's own `financial_year_id` is a more precise source of "which year" than re-deriving a financial year from today's date, since a contribution can be logged for a past month within its own FY at any time.

**Decision:** `GET /reconciliation` gained an optional `is_stale` query parameter (alongside the existing `financial_year_id`), used by the new "All / Stale only / Up to date" history filter, rather than filtering the already-paginated `history` array client-side.
**Rationale:** The history table is server-paginated; filtering only the current page's rows client-side would under-report (e.g. "Stale only" showing fewer than the real total whenever a page happens to contain few/no stale rows) instead of actually querying for them.

**Tested:** Ran `alembic upgrade head` against the real dev Postgres container (migration `a0b1c2d3e4f5` applied cleanly — the first-chosen revision id collided with an existing migration and was regenerated). Restarted the backend and confirmed clean startup with no import errors. Drove the full flow with a headless-Chromium Playwright script (`playwright-core`, no project browser-automation skill existed yet) against the real dev server, logged in as Superadmin: on Projects, opened "+ Add project" and confirmed the financial year dropdown read exactly `Unassigned`, `Current — FY2025/26 (Sep–Aug)`, `Next — FY2026/27 (Sep–Aug)`, `Year after next — FY2027/28 (Sep–Aug)`; created a project dated June 2027 assigned to the "Next" option and confirmed it saved and displayed `FY2026/27 (Sep–Aug)` on its card. On Reconciliation, selected an already-reconciled month (April 2026 — every visible month in the dev DB was already reconciled from prior testing), then went to Contributions and recorded a contribution for that same month; back on Reconciliation the month's tile turned amber ("Reconciled — stale"), the detail panel showed the "⚠ Data changed since reconciliation" banner, and expanding it read exactly "This reconciliation may no longer be accurate. Contribution added after reconciliation on 06/08/2026, 11:05:52. Edit this reconciliation to bring it up to date." The history table's new Status column and All/Stale only/Up to date filter both rendered correctly, with the stale row highlighted. Clicked the now-prominent amber "Edit now — bring up to date" button, submitted an Admin correction with a reason, and confirmed the tile returned to green "Reconciled", the stale banner disappeared, and the "Edited by superadmin on ... — [reason]" line appeared. Separately confirmed via direct API calls that deleting a contribution re-triggers staleness on an already-corrected reconciliation (`stale_reason: "Contribution deleted after reconciliation"`), proving the delete path works independently of the UI flow just exercised. No console errors beyond the same pre-existing, unrelated CSP `frame-ancestors`-in-`<meta>` warning noted in earlier test passes. The test project was deactivated, the test contribution deleted, and the reconciliation's `actual_balance` restored to its original value afterwards via the app's own endpoints so no test artifacts were left in the dev database.

## 2026-08-06 — Fix: recalculate discrepancy for stale reconciliations

**Decision:** `_to_out()` in `routers/reconciliation.py` (the single place every reconciliation response is built — list, both exports, and the nested `reconciliation` field of `GET /reconciliation/{year}/{month}`) now recomputes `calculated_balance_for_month` live whenever `row.is_stale` is true, and returns it alongside the originally-stored figures as four fields: `original_calculated_balance`/`original_discrepancy` (frozen at submission time, i.e. the existing `calculated_balance`/`discrepancy` columns) and `current_calculated_balance`/`current_discrepancy` (recomputed against today's data; `current_discrepancy = actual_balance - current_calculated_balance`). For a non-stale row, `current_*` is set equal to the stored value with no extra query, since it's current by definition.
**Rationale:** The bug was that the UI kept showing the discrepancy exactly as it was on submission day, even after `is_stale` correctly flagged that new contributions/invoices had changed the underlying numbers — the flag existed but nothing recalculated what it was warning about. Doing the recalculation once in `_to_out` (rather than in each caller) means every response shape — the list, the month lookup, CSV/PDF export rows — picks it up automatically. Computing it only for stale rows avoids adding a second DB query to every non-stale row in a paginated list.

**Decision:** `ReconciliationPage.jsx`'s main "Discrepancy" metric card now always renders `current_discrepancy` (relabelled "Current discrepancy" when stale), and the "Calculated Balance"/"Discrepancy" columns of the reconciliation history table were switched from the stored `calculated_balance`/`discrepancy` to `current_calculated_balance`/`current_discrepancy` too.
**Rationale:** The task only asked for the selected-month panel and the expanded stale-detail box to show current figures, but leaving the history table's "Calculated Balance" column on the stale stored value while its "Discrepancy" column showed the new live one (an inconsistency caught while testing — see screenshot in this session) meant a stale row's three columns no longer arithmetically agreed with each other (Actual − Calculated ≠ Discrepancy). Since `current_*` already equals the stored value for every non-stale row, switching both columns is a no-op change in appearance for the common case and fixes the stale case.

**Decision:** The expanded stale-detail box gained a four-line breakdown — "Original balance when reconciled", "Current calculated balance", "Current discrepancy", and "Change since reconciliation" (`current_calculated_balance − original_calculated_balance`) — inside the same `.kt-stale-banner-detail` toggle, rather than a separate panel.
**Rationale:** Matches the task's literal four bullet points; keeping them inside the existing click-to-expand banner (rather than always-visible) avoids cluttering the panel for the common case where an Admin just wants the headline "it's stale, here's why" message.

**Tested:** Restarted the backend after the schema/router change and confirmed clean startup. Used a headless-Chromium Playwright script (`playwright-core`) against the real dev server, logged in as Superadmin: selected April 2026 (already reconciled with `actual_balance` deliberately set equal to `calculated_balance`, so a clean £0.00 discrepancy baseline), confirmed the panel read "£0.00". Recorded a £1.00 contribution for April 2026 via the Contributions page, returned to Reconciliation, and confirmed: the tile turned amber ("Reconciled — stale"); the metric card relabelled to "Current discrepancy" and read "£-1.00" (correct sign — a £1 contribution raises the calculated balance, so the unchanged actual balance is now £1 short); the expanded detail read exactly "Original balance when reconciled: £8,441.49" / "Current calculated balance: £8,442.49" / "Current discrepancy: £-1.00" / "Change since reconciliation: £1.00"; and the history table's April 2026 row showed £8,442.49 / £8,441.49 / £-1.00 (Actual − Calculated = Discrepancy, arithmetically consistent) with a "Stale" badge. Also confirmed a second, pre-existing stale row (August 2026, left over from prior test sessions) rendered with the same internal consistency after the history table fix, without having to specifically re-trigger its staleness. No console errors beyond the pre-existing, unrelated CSP `frame-ancestors`-in-`<meta>` warning. The test contribution was deleted and April 2026's `actual_balance` restored to its original value afterwards via the app's own endpoints, so no test artifacts were left in the dev database.

## 2026-08-06 — Fix: context-aware AI prompting and MFA issuer name

**Decision:** The AI report-summary prompt (`ai_provider_service._build_report_system_prompt`, called from `generate_report_summary`) is now built dynamically from `settings_service.get_terminology`/`get_site_name`/`get_reserve_settings` instead of the old module-level `_REPORT_SYSTEM_PROMPT` constant, which hardcoded "charity or community organisation", "invoices", and "contributions" regardless of configuration. `_build_report_context`'s rendered figures (spend/income line, funding-position line, planned-projects line) were switched the same way, from literal "invoices"/"contributions"/"projects" to `term_expenses`/`term_income`/`term_projects`.
**Rationale:** The task named `backend/services/report_service.py` as the file to edit, but that module only aggregates report *figures* (totals, category breakdowns, forecasts) — it has no prompt text at all. The actual hardcoded prompt strings live in `services/ai_provider_service.py` (`_build_extraction_system_prompt`, and the old `_REPORT_SYSTEM_PROMPT`), which is also where `architecture.md`'s AI Layer section says prompt construction belongs. Editing `ai_provider_service.py` instead of `report_service.py` was a deliberate deviation from the literal brief to match where the code (and the architecture doc) actually puts this responsibility.

**Decision:** Which "use case" tone to use (`_report_use_case`) is decided by checking terminology first — `term_income == "Income"` and `term_expenses` in `("Bills", "Expenses")` → `"personal"` — and only falling back to `site_name != "Keep Track"` → `"organisation"` if that doesn't match; anything else is `"default"`.
**Rationale:** The task's own brief lists a fuzzy "site_name suggests a charity/organisation" check ahead of the terminology check, but there's no reliable way to tell "this is an organisation's name" from free text (a personal deployment could just as easily be named after a person or a house). The terminology pairing, by contrast, is deterministic — it's exactly the Income/Bills or Income/Expenses combination the Settings → Terminology UI already offers as its "personal finance" preset — so it's checked first and a customised, non-default `site_name` is used only as the (also fairly reliable) fallback signal for the organisational case.

**Decision:** The invoice-extraction prompt (`_build_extraction_system_prompt`) was left unchanged — it already lists every active category by id and real name ("Available categories (id: name): ..."), sourced from the caller's `db.query(Category).filter(Category.active.is_(True))` (see `routers/invoices.py`), which is what the task asked for.
**Rationale:** No hardcoded/guessed category names existed to fix. The `id: name` format (rather than a bare comma-separated name list, as the task's illustrative example showed) is intentionally kept, since `category_id` in the JSON response has to reference a real row id, not just match a name string.

**Decision:** TOTP issuer changed from `"Keep Track"` to `"KeepTrack"` (no space) in `config.py`'s default *and* in `.env`/`.env.example` (both of which pinned `TOTP_ISSUER=Keep Track`, overriding the code default — the fix had no effect on the running dev stack until both were updated). `services/auth_service.py` gained `mfa_account_name(username, site_name)`, used at both QR-generating call sites (`POST /auth/setup`, `POST /auth/register` in `routers/auth.py`, and the env-bootstrapped `ensure_superadmin`) in place of the previous `user.email`: it returns `f"{site_name} ({username})"` when `site_name` has been customised away from the "Keep Track" default, else `f"{settings.totp_issuer} ({username})"`.
**Rationale:** Authy was fuzzy-matching the space-containing issuer "Keep Track" to an unrelated third-party app "KeepUp" and showing its icon — a tighter, distinct issuer string avoids the collision. Switching the account-name field from email to `username` matches the task's literal example ("KHOC (richard)" — a username, not an email address) and reads better in an authenticator app's account list. Existing users' already-issued QR codes/secrets are untouched — `users.mfa_secret` isn't regenerated by this change, only the issuer/label shown on the *next* QR code a new or re-registering account gets.

## 2026-08-06 — Feature: project invoice linking with actual vs estimated spend tracking

**Decision:** `invoices` gained a nullable `project_id` FK to `planned_projects` (migration `b1c2d3e4f5a6`), `ON DELETE SET NULL` rather than the schema doc's default `RESTRICT`. `planned_projects.actual_cost`, `variance`, `variance_percent`, and `project_status` are **not** stored columns at all, despite the task brief literally asking for a `project_status` enum column — they're computed live on every read by the new `services/project_service.py`, from the sum of that project's linked, confirmed (`reviewed=true`, `deleted=false`) invoices.
**Rationale:** The task brief itself says `actual_cost` is "computed on the fly, not stored" — `project_status` is derived from that same live figure (planning/in_progress/over_budget all hinge on comparing actual to estimated), so storing it separately would let it drift the instant an invoice was added, edited, or unlinked without a corresponding write to the project row. This follows the same "compute, don't cache" pattern this app already uses for reconciliation staleness recalculation and dashboard notifications, rather than introducing a second source of truth for a value with an unambiguous live computation. `ON DELETE SET NULL` is belt-and-braces, not a path expected to fire — `planned_projects` rows are never hard-deleted, only deactivated or completed.

**Decision:** `project_status` priority is `completed` > `over_budget` > `in_progress` > `planning` (`project_service.compute_status`) — a completed project reports "completed" even if it finished over budget.
**Rationale:** Completion is the headline state once a project is marked done; "over budget" is a warning flag for spend still in flight, not a verdict on finished work. The historical over/under-spend is still fully visible via `actual_cost`/`variance` on a completed project, just not folded into its status word.

**Decision:** `planned_projects` also gained `completed_at` (migration `c2d3e4f5a6b7`), set by `POST /projects/{id}/complete`, purely to give the new project detail page's timeline something to show for "when completed" — no such timestamp existed before (only the `completed` boolean).
**Rationale:** Not in the original task brief's database section, but the brief's own PART 3.4 asks for a completion timeline entry and there was nothing to source it from. Added as the minimal, directly-motivated column rather than reconstructing it after the fact from audit log entries.

**Decision:** The forecast's planned-project contribution (`financial_year_service.planned_project_cost_for_month`) now adds a project's **remaining** estimate (`estimated_cost − actual_cost`, floored at zero) in its expected month, instead of the full `estimated_cost`, once any confirmed invoice is linked to it. A project with nothing linked yet still contributes its full estimate, unchanged from before.
**Rationale:** A project's actual spend already appears in the forecast/actual chart via the ordinary monthly invoice totals, in whichever months those invoices actually fall — continuing to add the *full* estimate on top in the expected month would double-count spend that's already landed. Using the remainder keeps "actual spend to date + remaining estimate for in-progress projects" (the task's literal forecast requirement) true without a separate code path — the existing monthly actual totals supply the "actual to date" half, and this change supplies the "remaining estimate" half.

**Decision:** `InvoiceUpdate.project_id` only ever *sets* a link via `PUT /invoices/{id}` (`None` means "leave unchanged", matching every other optional field on that schema, e.g. `category_id`) — it cannot be used to clear a link back to null. Clearing one is a separate action, `POST /invoices/{id}/unlink-project` (Standard/Admin, audit-logged), mirroring this app's existing pattern of dedicated action endpoints (`/confirm`, `/sign`, `/complete`) for state transitions that need their own audit trail entry.
**Rationale:** Keeps `PUT`'s "only ever sets, never clears" semantics consistent across every field rather than special-casing `project_id`, and gives unlinking (a distinct, user-facing, confirmation-guarded action per the task brief) its own auditable event instead of folding it into a generic field-edit log line. `POST /invoices/{id}/confirm` similarly gained an optional `project_id` body field (default an empty `InvoiceConfirmRequest()`, so every existing no-body caller keeps working unchanged) for linking at the moment of confirmation, though the review card in practice links earlier via the existing `PUT` call it already makes before confirming.

**Decision:** `InvoiceOut` carries both `project_id` and `project_name` (resolved via a batch query in `GET /invoices`, a one-off lookup elsewhere) — a deliberate deviation from this app's existing convention of *not* resolving `category_id` to a name server-side (the frontend joins that against its own categories list).
**Rationale:** The task brief explicitly asks for `project_name` in the invoice list/detail response, unlike category, where no such requirement exists anywhere in the app. Honouring the literal, explicit requirement here rather than forcing consistency with an unrelated field's existing (and un-requested) convention.

**Decision:** The Projects page's searchable "Link to project" dropdown (review card) is a small hand-built combobox (text input + filtered `<ul>` menu, `onMouseDown` on options so selection fires before the input's `onBlur` closes the menu) rather than a component library.
**Rationale:** This app has no UI component library anywhere (every other select in the codebase is a plain native `<select>`) — pulling one in for a single searchable field would be a disproportionate new dependency for what a ~40-line component handles fine.

**Decision:** The report's new "Project summary" table and the AI narrative's project mentions both reuse the *same* in-range project set (`expected_month` falling within the report's date range) that `report_service._projects_in_range` already selected for the pre-existing "planned projects due" list, just enriched with `actual_cost`/`variance`/`project_status` via `project_service`, rather than a differently-scoped "all active projects" set.
**Rationale:** Introducing a second, differently-scoped notion of "the report's projects" alongside the existing one would be confusing and risk the two disagreeing about which projects a report covers. The AI system prompt gained one added instruction — call out any project significantly over/under its estimate by name — since that's the one thing the model can't infer from the pre-formatted context text on its own without being told it's wanted.

**Tested:** Ran `alembic upgrade head` against the real dev Postgres container (migrations `b1c2d3e4f5a6` and `c2d3e4f5a6b7` applied cleanly), restarted the backend, and confirmed clean startup with no import errors. Exercised the API directly with curl as a logged-in Superadmin: linked an invoice to a project via `PUT /invoices/{id}` and confirmed `GET /projects/{id}` reported the correct `actual_cost`, `invoice_count`, `variance`, `project_status` ("in_progress"), and `linked_invoices`; confirmed the same figures appeared in `GET /dashboard/summary`'s `planned_projects`; unlinked via `POST /invoices/{id}/unlink-project` and confirmed the project's figures returned to zero; confirmed `GET /invoices?project=<id>` and `?project=unlinked` filtered correctly. Generated a real PDF report covering the project's expected month and confirmed (via PyMuPDF text extraction inside the backend container) the new "Project summary" page rendered with the correct name/estimated/actual/variance/status row and total. Verified `project_service.remaining_estimated_cost`/`compute_status` directly for the zero-spend, partial-spend, over-budget, and completed-but-over-budget cases. Drove the full UI with headless-Chromium Playwright (Python `playwright` package) against the real dev server, logged in as Superadmin: on the Projects page, confirmed a card showed "Actual spend to date", the under/over budget variance line, and the invoice count; clicked through to the new `/projects/{id}` detail page and confirmed the financial summary, progress bar, timeline, and linked-invoices table all rendered; on Invoices, confirmed the new Project filter offered "All invoices", "Unlinked only", and each active project by name; uploaded a real PDF through the upload flow, confirmed the review card's searchable "Link to project" dropdown opened, filtered by typed text, and selected a project, then confirmed via the API that the resulting invoice was saved with the correct `project_id`; on the invoice detail page, confirmed the linked project rendered as a clickable badge that navigated to the project detail page, and that "Unlink from project" showed a confirmation prompt and removed the link on confirmation. No console errors beyond the pre-existing, unrelated CSP `frame-ancestors`-in-`<meta>` warning noted in earlier test passes. All test invoices created during this session were deleted and the test project's link state was returned to empty via the app's own endpoints afterwards, so no test artifacts were left in the dev database.

**Tested:** Restarted the backend container (`docker compose up -d backend`, needed rather than a plain `restart` since `.env` values are only re-read on container recreation) and confirmed clean startup with no import errors. Directly exercised `_build_report_system_prompt`/`_build_report_context` against the real dev DB's current KHOC-style settings (`site_name = "Stayton Road - KHOC"`, default terminology) and confirmed `_report_use_case` returned `"organisation"` with a formal, third-person prompt using "Invoices"/"Contributions"/"Projects"/"Target Reserve". Ran `generate_report_summary` end-to-end against the real dev report data and the real configured Ollama endpoint: the KHOC case's AI response opened "Stayton Road - KHOC's financial performance ... shows a significant inflow of contributions ..." (organisational, third person, correct terms); with terminology monkeypatched in-process (not persisted to the DB) to `Income`/`Bills`/`Goals`/`Emergency Fund`, the AI response switched to "your bills have added up to ... your overall income ..." (personal, second person, correct terms) — confirming the prompt change actually steers the model's language, not just the instructions. (Both live responses were truncated by the existing `max_tokens=1536` cap before valid JSON closed, a pre-existing quirk of the verbose local Ollama vision model unrelated to this change, so both calls fell through to the existing graceful-degradation empty-fields path — the prompt content itself, visible in the truncated raw text, was correct in both cases.) Confirmed the extraction prompt already lists the seven real active categories (Alarm, Broadband, Electricity, General Maintenance, HVAC, Supplies, Water) by id and name. For MFA, confirmed via a real `POST /auth/register` call that the returned `otpauth_uri` read `otpauth://totp/KeepTrack:Stayton%20Road%20-%20KHOC%20%28mfatest_tmp%29?...&issuer=KeepTrack` — correct issuer, correct `{site_name} (username)` label — then deleted the test account (and its one audit-log row) from the dev database afterwards.

## 2026-08-06 — Feature: bulk historical import via CSV and PDF

**Decision:** `invoices` gained `is_historical` (boolean, default false) and `import_batch_id` (nullable string, indexed) via migration `d3e4f5a6b7c8`, plus a new `import_batches` table (`batch_id` unique string, `import_type` CHECK'd to `pdf`/`csv`, `filename`, `total_records`/`imported_records`/`failed_records`, `status` CHECK'd to `processing`/`complete`/`partial`/`failed`, `imported_by`, `imported_at`, `notes`). `import_batch_id` is a plain string label with **no FK** to `import_batches.batch_id`.
**Rationale:** A hard FK would force a choice between `ON DELETE CASCADE` (silently wiping invoices that `DELETE /imports/{batch_id}` deliberately protects — see below) or `RESTRICT` (blocking that same delete outright). Treating it as a grouping label, exactly like `invoices.project_id` treats project membership, means a batch row can be deleted while some of its invoices are kept behind, which is precisely the behaviour the delete endpoint needs.

**Decision:** Historical invoices skip the review stage entirely rather than reusing `reviewed=false` + a fast-track confirm. A CSV-imported row is created with `is_historical=true`, `reviewed=true`, `signed=false` in one step — there is no review card for CSV rows at all. A PDF-imported row is created with `is_historical=true`, `reviewed=false`, `signed=false` and goes through the *existing* review card and `POST /invoices/{id}/confirm` endpoint, unmodified on the backend — only the frontend's `ReviewCard` gained a `historical` prop that shows a green "Historical import — signing not required" banner and skips straight to `confirm()` instead of `setStage('signing')`, regardless of the `signing_enabled` setting.
**Rationale:** A CSV row has no PDF to review against and no AI extraction to double-check — there's nothing a review card would show. A PDF row still benefits from the AI-extraction review UI (the task brief explicitly asks for "the same review card as normal upload"), so reusing it outright — rather than building a second, parallel review UI — was the only change needed to satisfy "no signing step regardless of signing settings."

**Decision:** `POST /imports/csv` auto-detects columns from the header row (`services/import_service.detect_column_map`, matching on a normalised — lowercased, punctuation-stripped — header name against alias lists), but also accepts an optional `column_map` form field (JSON object of `{field: column_index}`) that overrides or fills in individual fields. A new `POST /imports/csv/preview` endpoint (no DB writes) returns the header row, the first 5 data rows, and the auto-detected map, so the frontend's column-mapping UI has real data to show and a real payload to send back.
**Rationale:** The task brief's frontend spec asks for an editable column-mapping UI "in case the CSV has different column names," which would be a cosmetic dead end without a way for the backend to actually honour a manual override — auto-detection alone can't be corrected if it guesses wrong. A separate preview endpoint avoids duplicating the header-normalisation/alias-matching logic in JavaScript (this app already prefers one implementation over a client/server-duplicated one, e.g. `csv_export.py`'s formula-injection escaping used only from Python), and keeps `POST /imports/csv` itself a single commit-or-nothing operation.

**Decision:** Row skip rules are, in order: notes containing "remittance advice" (case-insensitive) skips with reason "Remittance advice (not an invoice)"; otherwise a missing/zero/unparsable amount skips with "Missing, zero, or unparsable amount". A row's category is matched case-insensitively (exact, then substring both ways) against active categories; no match — including no category column at all — falls back to `General Maintenance` with a note appended (`"Category 'X' not recognised — assigned to General Maintenance"` or `"No category given — assigned to General Maintenance"`) rather than leaving `category_id` null.
**Rationale:** The task brief calls out both skip conditions independently but doesn't rank them; remittance advice is checked first since a remittance row is definitionally not an invoice regardless of what its amount column happens to contain (often blank, sometimes zero). Falling back to a real category rather than leaving it null keeps every imported historical invoice queryable by category immediately, with the appended note making the fallback easy to find and fix later — this mirrors how a skipped/AI-missed field elsewhere in the app becomes a manual follow-up rather than a silent gap.

**Decision:** Duplicate flagging for CSV rows reads only the `Duplicate Flag` column (`"Yes"`/`"TRUE"`, case-insensitive) — it does **not** additionally run the existing AI-service `check_duplicate` heuristic (same-supplier/amount/nearby-date) that a normal PDF upload uses. PDF imports, by contrast, do run `check_duplicate`, exactly as a normal upload does.
**Rationale:** The task brief is explicit that CSV duplicate flagging is driven by that column alone, and a KHOC-style export already carries its own considered duplicate judgement from whoever compiled the spreadsheet — re-running the heuristic on top could second-guess or contradict that source data. A PDF import has no equivalent judgement to defer to, so it uses the same detection a normal upload already relies on.

**Decision:** `DELETE /imports/{batch_id}` (Admin only) supports a `dry_run=true` query param that returns `deleted_count`/`protected_count` without changing anything, so the frontend can show an accurate confirmation count before the real delete. "Protected" means at least one `invoice.edited` audit-log entry exists for that invoice — which also covers a field correction made during the PDF review step (before confirming), not just an edit made later from the invoice detail page.
**Rationale:** Confirmation-with-a-real-count needs the backend to compute the same partition twice (once to preview, once to act) — a dry-run flag on the same endpoint keeps that one query in one place rather than a second, parallel "preview" endpoint. Counting a review-time correction as "edited" is a deliberate, documented choice (see `routers/imports.py`'s `_invoice_edited_since_import`): a PDF row the user corrected during review is no longer just what the import wrote, whether that correction happened five seconds or five weeks after the batch was created — a bulk delete of the batch shouldn't be able to discard it.

**Decision:** Neither CSV nor PDF historical invoices have `financial_year_id` set on creation — matching the pre-existing behaviour of `POST /invoices/upload` and `POST /invoices/{id}/confirm`, which also never populate that column (see the `invoices.py` model's own docstring: "financial year assignment isn't implemented yet"). `get_or_create_financial_year` + `mark_reconciliation_stale` are still called for every historical invoice's month, exactly as `confirm_invoice` already does for a normally-confirmed invoice.
**Rationale:** Consistency with the existing, already-shipped invoice-creation code path was more important than fixing a pre-existing gap this task didn't ask about — a historical import backfilling years of invoices is exactly the scenario where silently behaving differently from a normal upload would be surprising later.

**Decision:** The downloadable CSV template (`Date,Supplier,Amount (£),Category,Notes` plus a `#`-commented README row and three example rows) is generated client-side as a static string and downloaded via a `Blob`, rather than a backend endpoint.
**Rationale:** The template's content is fixed and has no server-side data dependency — a round trip to the backend for a static file would be pure overhead. `parse_csv_text` already drops any row starting with `#`, so a template downloaded and re-uploaded unmodified (README row and all) imports cleanly without extra handling.

**Tested:** Ran `alembic upgrade head` against the real dev Postgres container (migration `d3e4f5a6b7c8` applied cleanly) and restarted the backend with no import errors. Exercised every endpoint directly with curl as Superadmin: imported a 10-row KHOC-style CSV (`Filename, Year Folder, Invoice Date, Supplier, Amount, Category, Duplicate Flag, Notes`) covering every documented edge case in one file — a zero-amount row, an empty-amount row, a remittance-advice row, a `Duplicate Flag=Yes` row, an unrecognised category, and three different date formats (`DD/MM/YYYY`, `YYYY-MM-DD`, `MM/YYYY`) — and confirmed exactly 7 imported, 3 skipped with the correct per-row reasons, and 1 flagged duplicate, with every created invoice showing `is_historical=true`, `reviewed=true`, `signed=false`, and the unrecognised-category rows correctly falling back to General Maintenance with the explanatory note appended. Re-ran with the simpler `Date,Supplier,Amount,Category,Notes` format plus a leading `#` README row and confirmed both rows imported and the README row was correctly ignored. Verified `GET /imports` (Admin only), `GET /imports/{batch_id}`, and `GET /invoices?historical=true`/`?import_batch=...` all returned the expected data. Edited one imported invoice via `PUT /invoices/{id}`, then confirmed `DELETE /imports/{batch_id}?dry_run=true` reported it as protected (6 deletable / 1 protected out of 7), and that a real delete removed exactly the 6 unedited invoices and left the corrected one behind with the batch row itself gone. Uploaded two real PDFs (generated with PyMuPDF, extracted by the real configured Anthropic API) through `POST /imports/pdf` and confirmed both were created with `is_historical=true`, `reviewed=false`; confirmed one via the normal `POST /invoices/{id}/confirm` and verified it flipped to `reviewed=true` while the other stayed `false`. Drove the full UI with headless-Chromium Playwright (Python `playwright` package, browsers already present in the environment) against the real dev server, logged in as Superadmin: confirmed the sidebar's Import link sits between Invoices and Contributions; on the CSV tab, uploaded a test CSV, confirmed the auto-detected column-mapping preview appeared, clicked Import, and confirmed the "1 imported / 1 skipped / 0 duplicates" summary and working "View imported invoices" link; confirmed the Invoices page showed a "Historical" badge on the imported row and that the new Historical filter dropdown was present and functional; on the PDF tab, uploaded two real PDFs, clicked Process, waited for real AI extraction to complete, and confirmed two review cards appeared each showing the historical banner, with a working "Confirm all reviewed" button; scrolled to Import History and confirmed the batch list, then clicked Delete and confirmed the modal showed the correct "This will permanently delete N invoice(s)" count before deleting. No console errors beyond the pre-existing, unrelated CSP `frame-ancestors`-in-`<meta>` warning noted in earlier test passes. All invoices, import batches, and uploaded test files created during this session were deleted via the app's own endpoints afterwards, so no test artifacts were left in the dev database.

## 2026-08-06 — Fix: exclude historical invoices from signing notifications and badges

**Decision:** The dashboard's `invoices_unsigned` notification count (`routers/dashboard.py`) and the `signed=unsigned` filter in `routers/invoices.py`'s shared `_filtered_invoices_query` (used by `GET /invoices`, the CSV export, and the PDF export alike) now both add `Invoice.is_historical.is_(False)`. The Invoices table's Signed column shows `N/A` instead of an `Unsigned` badge for historical rows (the existing `Historical` badge already covers it), and the CSV/PDF export's Signed column shows `N/A` for the same rows regardless of which filter produced them.
**Rationale:** Historical invoices (`is_historical=true`, imported via CSV/PDF import — see the previous entry) are created with `signed=false` and no PDF to sign against, so every existing "unsigned" query and the row's own Signed badge were incorrectly flagging them as needing signature collection. Fixing it at the one shared query function (`_filtered_invoices_query`) rather than in each caller keeps the list endpoint, both exports, and any future caller in sync automatically. The dashboard notification's `link` field was left untouched (still `/invoices?reviewed=true`) since `DashboardPage.jsx` already ignores it for this notification type in favour of its own `type`-based routing to `/invoices?filter=unsigned`, which now resolves to the same fixed backend filter.

**Tested:** `python3 -m py_compile` on both edited backend files; `npm run build` on the frontend succeeded with no new errors.

## 2026-08-06 — Fix: reorganise settings into logical groups

**Decision:** `SettingsNav.jsx`'s `SETTINGS_CATEGORIES` was restructured from 8 flat/partially-grouped items down to 7: General, Appearance, Security, AI & Extraction, Users & Access, Notifications & Logs, and the existing collapsible Data group (Import Data / Storage & Backup / System Reset, unchanged). Terminology folded into a new `AppearanceSettings.jsx` (alongside a static note that dark mode is controlled per-user via the header toggle) and Signing & Export folded into a new `SecuritySettings.jsx` (alongside two inert "Coming soon" placeholder rows for Session timeout and MFA settings) — both as nested sub-sections reusing the existing `TerminologySettings`/`SigningExportSettings` components unchanged, following the same multiple-`<h2 className="kt-panel-title">`-per-page pattern `GeneralSettings.jsx` already used for its General/Target Reserve split.
**Rationale:** The task's literal grouping brief. Reusing the existing field/logic components rather than rewriting them kept the change purely structural (nav + composition), with no behavioural risk to the settings themselves.

**Decision:** The combined Notifications & Logs section's nav key stayed `'logs'` (only its label changed, to "Notifications & Logs") rather than being renamed to something like `notifications-logs`, and the new `NotificationsLogsSettings.jsx` wrapper always defaults its own Notifications/Logs tab state to `'logs'` regardless of props.
**Rationale:** `routers/dashboard.py`'s `critical_errors_detected`/`audit_log_archived` notifications hardcode `link: "/settings?section=logs"` and `"/settings?section=logs&tab=errors"`, and `DashboardPage.jsx`'s `notificationLink` falls back to `/settings?section=logs` for the same two types. The task scoped this pass to `frontend/`, and keeping the key unchanged was the only way to honour that scope without breaking those existing deep links — a bare `?section=logs` (no `&tab=`) still needs to land on the Logs tab, not the Notifications placeholder, which the unconditional default handles.

**Decision:** `LogsSettings.jsx` had its own `<h2>Logs</h2>` removed (it now renders directly into the "Logs" tab of `NotificationsLogsSettings.jsx`), matching how `ActiveUsersTab`/`PendingApprovalTab` render under `UsersSettings.jsx`'s tabs with no heading of their own. `ComingSoonSettings.jsx` (previously the standalone Notifications placeholder) was deleted outright rather than reused, since nothing else referenced it and its own-heading shape didn't fit nested-tab use — the placeholder markup was inlined directly in `NotificationsLogsSettings.jsx`'s Notifications tab instead.
**Rationale:** Avoids a redundant "Notifications & Logs" / "Logs" double heading, and removes what would otherwise be dead code.

**Decision:** Added a new generic `.kt-settings-info-box` class to `settings.css` (identical styling to the pre-existing `.kt-logs-info-box` in `logs.css`) for the Appearance section's dark-mode note, rather than reusing the logs-specific class from a different file or renaming it.
**Rationale:** `.kt-logs-info-box` is scoped to `logs.css` for the Logs section specifically; a second, generically-named class avoids an odd cross-file dependency for an unrelated section while keeping the visual language consistent.

**Tested:** `npm run build` succeeded with no new errors. Logged into the real dev stack (existing `docker compose` containers already running) as Superadmin via the backend's own `/auth/login` (this dev account has `mfa_required: false`) and drove the UI with headless-Chromium Playwright (Python `playwright` package): confirmed all 7 top-level nav items render in the new order with correct labels ("Users & Access", "Notifications & Logs"); confirmed Appearance shows the dark-mode note above the working Terminology fields/live-preview; confirmed Security shows Signing & Export's working toggle above the two "Coming soon" placeholder rows; confirmed Notifications & Logs opens on the Logs tab by default with the real audit log data, and that clicking the Notifications tab shows the placeholder; confirmed the Data group still expands/collapses and all three sub-items (including red-styled System Reset) render and navigate correctly; confirmed active-state highlighting, badges, and the danger-red System Reset styling all render correctly in both light and dark mode (toggled via the header's real theme toggle). No console errors beyond the pre-existing, unrelated CSP `frame-ancestors`-in-`<meta>` warning noted in earlier sessions. Mobile slide-over behaviour was not re-driven in a browser this pass (repeated sandbox tool friction when scripting a second headless-browser run) but was confirmed by inspection to be untouched — `SettingsPage.jsx`'s `mobileShowContent` state, `handleBack`, and `settings.css`'s `@media (max-width: 720px)` slide transform are unmodified, and only the nav data array and content components changed.

## 2026-08-06 — Feature: welcome page for new users on first login

**Decision:** `users` gained `has_seen_welcome` (boolean, default false) via migration `e4f5a6b7c8d9`. `UserOut` exposes it, and a new `POST /auth/welcome/dismiss` (any authenticated user, idempotent — a no-op commit if already true) flips it to true. There is no corresponding "re-show" endpoint or setting.
**Rationale:** The task is explicitly a one-off, dismiss-once experience with no requirement to ever reset it, so the minimal shape is a single flag and a single write endpoint — mirroring `must_change_password`'s existing shape (a boolean gate flipped exactly once by a dedicated action) rather than introducing a more general "onboarding state" concept nothing else in the app needs yet.

**Decision:** The welcome overlay lives in `frontend/src/components/WelcomeOverlay.jsx`, mounted once inside `Layout.jsx` (sibling to the `Header`/`Sidebar`/`Outlet`), rather than as a route or a `RequireAuth`-level gate. It deliberately does **not** reuse the existing shared `.kt-modal`/`Modal.jsx` (`components/Modal.jsx`, `settings.css`), which closes on an overlay click and has its own header/close-button chrome.
**Rationale:** Living inside `Layout` means the real dashboard renders underneath it exactly as the task asked ("the dashboard is visible but dimmed behind it," not a separate route to redirect through), and it survives client-side navigation from a quick-start card without remounting or re-triggering. `Modal.jsx`'s overlay-click-to-dismiss behaviour is the opposite of this task's explicit "clicking outside does NOT dismiss it" and "no Escape" requirements, so reusing it would mean stripping out its defining behaviour rather than gaining anything from the shared component — a purpose-built overlay with no dismiss handlers on the backdrop itself (and no `keydown` listener at all) satisfies both requirements by simply not wiring them up, rather than wiring them up and then suppressing them.

**Decision:** Dismissal is optimistic and fire-and-forget: `AuthContext.dismissWelcome()` flips `user.has_seen_welcome` in local state immediately, then calls `POST /auth/welcome/dismiss` in the background without awaiting or surfacing a failure, matching the existing `logout()` pattern's comment ("never allowed to block or fail the actual client-side [action]"). The overlay itself tracks its own `visible`/`closing` state separately from `user.has_seen_welcome` so the fade-out animation can finish (and, for a quick-start card, the app can navigate) before it unmounts, rather than vanishing the instant context state flips.
**Rationale:** A dismiss action blocking on the network (or rolling back on failure) would contradict the task's "any logged-in user" / always-succeeds framing and risk the overlay reappearing mid-session if a request blipped. If the background call does fail, the only consequence is `GET /auth/me` reporting `has_seen_welcome=false` again on the *next* login — an acceptable, self-correcting degradation for a one-off cosmetic flag, not one worth adding retry logic for.

**Decision:** The four quick-start cards are hardcoded to `/upload`, `/contributions`, `/`, and `/reports` — not derived from `Sidebar.jsx`'s nav item list or any new backend-configurable list.
**Rationale:** The task brief specifies these four destinations and their copy directly; they don't map one-to-one onto the sidebar's nav items (e.g. "Upload your first invoice" targets `/upload`, not the sidebar's `/invoices` list page), so deriving them from the sidebar's data would require the same special-casing as just listing them directly, for a one-time, effectively static onboarding screen that doesn't need to track future nav changes.

**Decision:** Terminology-aware copy uses the existing `useTerminology()` labels as-is for "record {income}" but singularizes `term_expenses` (via the existing `utils/format.js singularize`, already used by `DashboardPage.jsx`) for "Upload your first {expense}" specifically, since the task's own copy calls for a singular noun there ("Add an invoice or bill") while the others read naturally in whatever plural/collective form the org configured (e.g. "Contributions").
**Rationale:** Consistency with how `DashboardPage.jsx` already singularizes the same label for its own singular-context copy, rather than introducing a second singularization approach.

**Tested:** Ran `alembic upgrade head` against the real dev Postgres container (migration `e4f5a6b7c8d9` applied cleanly) and restarted the backend with no import errors; `python3 -m py_compile` on the three edited backend files, and `npm run build` on the frontend, both clean. Exercised the new endpoints directly: `POST /auth/register` confirmed the response's `user.has_seen_welcome` is `false` by default. Drove the full flow with headless-Chromium Playwright (Python `playwright` package) against the real dev server: registered a fresh user, approved it via direct DB write (no admin session was available in the test harness), logged in through the real `/login` → MFA (`pyotp`-generated TOTP) flow, and confirmed the welcome overlay appeared over the dimmed dashboard with the logo/wordmark, instance name in the title ("Welcome to Keep Track — Stayton Road - KHOC"), the username greeting, all 4 quick-start cards, "Get started", and "Don't show this again". Confirmed pressing Escape did not close it and clicking outside the card did not close it (both by design — no handlers wired to the backdrop). Clicked a quick-start card ("Record contributions") and confirmed it both dismissed the overlay and navigated to `/contributions`. Reloaded the page afterwards and confirmed the overlay did not reappear. Tested dark mode with a second fresh user (theme set via the same `localStorage` key `ThemeContext.jsx` reads, since the header's theme toggle sits underneath the overlay's full-viewport backdrop and is intentionally unclickable while it's showing) and confirmed the card renders as dark charcoal with correctly re-themed icons, text, and buttons, with no light-mode artifacts. No console errors from the overlay itself; only the pre-existing, unrelated CSP `frame-ancestors` warning and React Router v7 future-flag deprecation notices appeared, both present before this change. All test users and their audit log rows were deleted afterwards via direct DB cleanup, with FK references checked across every table touching `users` — no test artifacts were left in the dev database.
