## 2026-08-06 — Feature: help section with user guide rendering and search

**Decision:** The 8 markdown files under `user-guides/` are copied into `frontend/public/help-guides/` (served as static assets, so `fetch('/help-guides/<file>.md')` works both in dev and in the production build) rather than read live from `../user-guides/` at request time.
**Rationale:** The task scoped this build to `frontend/` only, and `docker-compose.yml`'s `frontend` service bind-mounts only `./frontend:/app` — `../user-guides` doesn't exist inside that container's filesystem at all, so a runtime read (or a build-time copy step run inside the container, e.g. a Vite plugin `closeBundle` hook) would fail with `ENOENT` in the real dev/prod environment regardless of when it ran. Making `docker-compose.yml`/`Dockerfile` changes to mount `user-guides/` into the frontend container was ruled out as outside a frontend/-only task. The trade-off is real: editing a guide under `user-guides/` now requires re-copying it into `frontend/public/help-guides/` for the change to show up in the app — worth fixing properly (e.g. a documented sync step, or backend-served guides) in a future pass that's allowed to touch `docker-compose.yml`/`backend/`.

**Decision:** `marked` (v18) was added as a `frontend/package.json` dependency for markdown-to-HTML rendering, with no HTML-sanitizer (e.g. DOMPurify) layered on top.
**Rationale:** The task explicitly called for "a lightweight markdown parser." The 8 guide files are trusted, repo-authored content (not user-submitted input), so the usual `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` XSS concern doesn't apply here — adding a sanitizer would be extra weight for a threat model that doesn't exist for this content source.

**Decision:** `frontend/src/help/markdown.js` wraps a dedicated `Marked` instance (not the module-level default export) with a custom `link` renderer: external (`http(s)://`) links get `target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"`; internal links matching one of the 8 guides' own filenames (e.g. `getting-started.md`'s link to `uploading-invoices.md`) are left as plain relative hrefs, and `HelpPage.jsx` intercepts clicks on them via a single click handler on the rendered-content container, routing to the matching topic in-page instead of letting the browser request the raw `.md` file.
**Rationale:** A dedicated instance keeps the custom renderer from leaking into any future unrelated use of `marked` elsewhere in the app. Handling internal cross-links via a click interceptor (rather than rewriting hrefs to `/help?topic=...` inside the renderer) keeps the renderer itself simple and reuses the same `handleSelectTopic` path a nav-list click already goes through, including its mobile slide-over and scroll-reset behaviour.

**Decision:** The Help page's two-panel layout and mobile slide-over reuse the existing Settings page's CSS classes directly (`kt-settings-shell`, `kt-settings-track`, `kt-settings-nav-panel`, `kt-settings-content-panel`, `kt-settings-back`, `kt-settings-nav-link` — including its `.active` blue-left-border state) rather than duplicating them under new `kt-help-*` names. Only genuinely new pieces — the search box, markdown content typography, the independently-scrolling content panel, and the "Back to top" button — live in a new `frontend/src/styles/help.css`.
**Rationale:** The task asked for the Help page to match the Settings page's style, including "the primary blue left border — consistent with the Settings panel style" and "same slide-over behaviour as Settings." Reusing the same classes guarantees pixel-identical behaviour (including any future Settings-panel styling tweaks) instead of keeping two parallel copies of the same shell CSS in sync by hand.

**Decision:** All 8 guides are fetched once, up front, on `HelpPage` mount (`Promise.all` over `/help-guides/*.md`), rather than fetching only the active topic on demand.
**Rationale:** The guides are small text files, and the search box is required to match against topic content as well as titles — searching would otherwise need its own separate fetch-all step anyway. Loading them all once means switching topics never re-fetches and search has content to match against immediately as the user types.

**Decision:** The right-hand content panel scrolls independently of the left topic-nav panel via its own `max-height`/`overflow-y: auto` container (`.kt-help-content-scroll`), and the "Back to top" button's visibility is driven by that container's own `scrollTop`, on both desktop and mobile widths.
**Rationale:** The task explicitly required "Content is scrollable independently of the left panel," which the Settings page (a single page-level scroll) doesn't need since its panels are short. Keeping the same independent-scroll container at mobile widths too (rather than falling back to whole-page scroll under 720px) means the "Back to top" button keeps working there as well, with no separate mobile-only scroll behaviour to maintain.

**Decision:** A shared `HelpIconLink` component and a new `.kt-page-header` wrapper class (`position: relative` plus right padding) were added to Dashboard, Invoices, Contributions, Reconciliation, Reports, and Settings, each wrapping that page's existing `<h1 className="kt-page-title">`/`<p className="kt-page-subtitle">` block. The small grey "?" link is positioned absolutely within that wrapper rather than the pages being restructured into a new header layout.
**Rationale:** Every target page already renders its title/subtitle in a slightly different surrounding structure (a bare `<div>`, `InvoicesPage.jsx`'s existing flex header row alongside its upload button, etc.); an absolutely-positioned icon inside a `position: relative` wrapper drops in around each page's existing markup unchanged, rather than requiring six different bespoke header-row rewrites for one small icon.

**Decision:** `Sidebar.jsx`'s `navItems` array was split into two `<ul>` lists (main nav, then a `.kt-nav-divider` separator, then Help + the existing admin-only Settings link) instead of adding a `divider: true` marker item to a single flattened array.
**Rationale:** Matches the task's explicit visual requirement (Help "clearly separated from the main navigation links with a subtle divider") most directly, and keeps the admin-only conditional (`{isAdmin && ...}`) simple and localised to the one link that actually needs it, rather than threading an `adminOnly` filter through a divider-aware render loop.

## 2026-08-06 — Feature: session timeout and MFA remember session

**Decision:** MFA remember tokens are tracked in their own table, `mfa_remember_tokens` (migration `f5a6b7c8d9e0`: `id`, `user_id` FK, `token_hash`, `created_at`, `expires_at`, `last_used_at`, `revoked`), storing only a SHA-256 hash of the raw token — never the raw value.
**Rationale:** A raw JWT can't be looked up or revoked individually once issued (the existing `token_invalid_before` / `jwt_invalidate_before` mechanisms only invalidate *everything* issued before a timestamp, all-or-nothing per user or globally). A remember token needs finer-grained control — revoke on logout, revoke via the Profile page, expire independently on its own clock — which only a real DB row with its own lifecycle supports. Hashing before storage follows the same reasoning as `users.password_hash`: a stolen database dump shouldn't hand out working credentials, and the raw value is only ever needed once, to hash-compare against what a client presents.

**Decision:** The raw remember token travels as a custom `X-MFA-Remember-Token` request header — on `POST /auth/login` (to attempt an MFA skip) and `POST /auth/logout` (so the endpoint knows which token to revoke) — rather than as a cookie or a request-body field. The frontend stores it in `localStorage` (`keeptrack-mfa-remember-token` / `-expires`, `frontend/src/utils/mfaRemember.js`), the same tier as the JWT access token (`kt_access_token`).
**Rationale:** This app has no cookie-based session infrastructure anywhere else (JWTs are carried via `Authorization: Bearer`, per `docs/architecture.md`) — introducing `Set-Cookie`/`credentials: 'include'` for just this one token would mean two different auth-transport mechanisms to reason about, plus CORS/`SameSite` configuration this app doesn't otherwise need. A header is the natural extension of the existing bearer-token pattern, and keeping it in `localStorage` alongside the access token matches how the app already treats "credential-shaped" values on this client.

**Decision:** `DELETE /auth/mfa-remember` revokes **every** active remember token for the calling user, not only the one raw value this particular browser happens to hold (it takes no request body/header at all).
**Rationale:** The task frames this as "force full MFA next time" from the Profile page — a user-level security action, not a browser-scoped one. A user who remembers MFA on both a phone and a laptop and revokes from the laptop almost certainly wants *both* to require MFA again, not just the one they're sitting at. `POST /auth/logout`, by contrast, revokes only the specific token supplied via `X-MFA-Remember-Token` (if any) — logging out of one browser shouldn't silently kill a remembered session on a different device.

**Decision:** `useSessionTimeout`'s inactivity-timeout path calls `DELETE /auth/mfa-remember` directly and a new `AuthContext.forceLogout()` (clears local state only), rather than reusing `AuthContext.logout()` (which calls `POST /auth/logout`).
**Rationale:** The task explicitly separates the two ("Revoke MFA remember token via `DELETE /auth/mfa-remember` — only if the timeout was due to inactivity, not manual logout"). A manual logout already revokes via `POST /auth/logout`'s own header-based check; routing the inactivity path through the same endpoint would either double up the revoke call or require also wiring the remember-token header into a call site that has no obvious reason to send it, for the same eventual effect. Calling the two revoke paths as distinct, purpose-named actions keeps each call site legible about *why* it's revoking.

**Decision:** `session_timeout_minutes = "0"` is the sentinel for the "Never" dropdown option (no inactivity timeout at all) rather than a separate boolean setting or `NULL`.
**Rationale:** Keeps the setting a single value with a single validated set of allowed strings (`{0, 30, 60, 120, 240, 480}`, enforced in `routers/settings.py`), consistent with how every other settings-table value in this app is a plain string with defaulting logic in a service module (`services/mfa_remember_service.py`'s `get_session_timeout_minutes`) — `useSessionTimeout.js` already treats `0`/falsy as "disabled" with no special-casing needed (`if (!user || !timeoutMinutes) return`).

**Decision:** `POST /auth/login`'s response gained an `mfa_remember_hours` field, populated only on the `mfa_required: true` branch.
**Rationale:** MFAPage needs to render "Remember this session for `[X]` hours" before the user has a full access token — at that point in the flow only the 5-minute MFA-scoped `temp_token` exists, which can't call `GET /settings` (that endpoint requires a full `access`-scoped token via `get_current_user`). Piggybacking the value on the login response the frontend already has avoids adding a second unauthenticated settings-reading endpoint just for this one value.

**Decision:** The `SessionTimeoutWarning` modal (`frontend/src/components/SessionTimeoutWarning.jsx`) is a purpose-built overlay, not the shared `Modal.jsx` component, and is dismissed only by its own "Stay logged in" button — background mouse/keyboard activity elsewhere on the page does not reset the timer once the warning is showing (gated by a ref checked synchronously inside the activity listener, since the state update that shows the warning is otherwise async).
**Rationale:** Same reasoning as the welcome-overlay decision above: `Modal.jsx` closes on an overlay click, which is the opposite of "cannot be dismissed by clicking outside or Escape — user must actively choose to stay." A purpose-built overlay with no backdrop click handler and no `keydown` listener satisfies that by simply not wiring the dismiss paths up, rather than wiring them and then suppressing them.

**Decision:** The Page Visibility pause is implemented by shifting `lastActivityRef` forward by however long the tab was hidden, rather than pausing/resuming a `setInterval` or tracking accumulated "active" time separately.
**Rationale:** `elapsed = Date.now() - lastActivityRef.current` is the one quantity the whole hook is built around (for both the warning threshold and the timeout itself). Shifting the reference point forward by the hidden duration keeps that same formula correct with no separate "was I paused" branch anywhere except the `tick()` early-return while `document.hidden` is true — verified with Playwright's clock API (mocked `Date`/timers) simulating 26 minutes hidden on a 30-minute timeout: no warning fired while hidden, none fired immediately on un-hiding, and the warning appeared only after a further ~25 real (visible) minutes.

**Tested:** Ran `alembic upgrade head` against the real dev Postgres container (migration `f5a6b7c8d9e0` applied cleanly, creating `mfa_remember_tokens` and seeding `session_timeout_minutes`/`mfa_remember_hours`) and restarted the backend with no import errors. `npm run build` (via `vite build`) on the frontend was clean. Exercised the backend directly with `curl` end-to-end: password login without a remember token returns `mfa_required: true` with `mfa_remember_hours`; `verify-mfa` with `remember_session: true` returns a raw token and expiry; a follow-up login with that token in `X-MFA-Remember-Token` returns `mfa_required: false` and a full access token directly; `GET /auth/me` reflects `mfa_remember_active: true`/`session_timeout_minutes: 120`; `POST /auth/logout` with the header revokes the token (confirmed by the next login requiring MFA again); `DELETE /auth/mfa-remember` flips `mfa_remember_active` back to `false`; `PUT /settings/session_timeout_minutes` and `/mfa_remember_hours` both reject out-of-range values with 400 and accept the documented dropdown values; all six new audit action types (`user.login_mfa_remembered`, `user.mfa_verified`, `user.mfa_verified_with_remember`, `user.mfa_remember_revoked`, plus existing `user.login`/`user.logout`) appeared correctly in `GET /logs/audit`. Drove the full UI with headless-Chromium Playwright (Python `playwright` package) against the real dev server: the MFA page's "Remember this session for 12 hours" checkbox (unchecked by default); the Profile page's new "Session security" section showing "2 hours" and "MFA verification remembered — expires `[date]`" with a working Revoke button that flips the status and forces MFA on the next login; Settings → Security's two new dropdowns (replacing "Coming soon") saving and persisting correctly. Used Playwright's clock-mocking API to fast-forward simulated time without waiting in real time: confirmed the warning modal appears 5 minutes before a 30-minute timeout with a live countdown, that neither an outside click nor Escape dismisses it, that reaching the full timeout clears tokens and redirects to `/login` with "You were logged out due to inactivity," that `DELETE /auth/mfa-remember` fires during that inactivity path (confirmed in backend logs), and that hiding the tab (simulated via a `document.hidden` override + `visibilitychange` dispatch) correctly pauses the countdown while hidden and resumes it correctly afterwards. All test settings values and the test user (`sessiontest`) were reset/deactivated afterwards — no lasting state left in the dev database beyond the account itself (matching the existing pattern of other deactivated test accounts already present, e.g. `test`, `kttest_reset`).

**Tested:** `npm run build` succeeded with no new errors; confirmed `dist/help-guides/*.md` (all 8 files) are present after a build, alongside the existing dev-server route (`fetch('/help-guides/getting-started.md')` returns 200 with real content) via `curl` against the running dev container. Drove the full feature with headless-Chromium Playwright against the real dev stack, logged in as Superadmin via the real `/login` flow: confirmed the sidebar shows Help between Reports and the divider, and Settings after it, with Help itself unconditionally visible (not role-gated); confirmed `/help` defaults to "Getting Started" rendered as real HTML (headings, bold text, list items — no raw `#`/`**` markdown syntax visible); clicked through Dashboard Guide, Reconciliation, and Settings Guide topics and confirmed each renders correctly with the active blue-left-border highlight; searched "reconcil" and confirmed the list filtered to matching topics, searched a nonsense query and confirmed the exact `No help topics found for "<query>"` message, and confirmed both the X button and Escape key cleared the search back to the full list; clicked the "Uploading Invoices" cross-link inside the Getting Started guide and confirmed it navigated within the Help page (active topic updated, no request for a raw `.md` file); scrolled a long guide and confirmed the "Back to top" button appeared and correctly scrolled back to the top; visited `/`, `/invoices`, `/contributions`, `/reconciliation`, `/reports`, and `/settings` directly and confirmed each shows the small grey "?" icon, and that clicking it from `/invoices` and `/reconciliation` landed on `/help` with `uploading-invoices` and `reconciliation-guide` correctly pre-selected respectively; resized to a 375×800 mobile viewport and confirmed `/help` initially shows only the topic list, slides to content with a working "← Back to Help" control on selecting a topic, and returns to the list on tapping back; toggled dark mode via the header's theme button and confirmed headings, links, body text, and inline code all render with good contrast in both themes. No console errors beyond the pre-existing, unrelated CSP `frame-ancestors`-in-`<meta>` warning noted in earlier sessions. Note for anyone else running this branch: `marked` was added as a new `frontend/package.json` dependency, so the frontend Docker container's `node_modules` (a separate anonymous volume from the bind-mounted source) needs `npm install` re-run inside it (or the image rebuilt) before the dev server will start cleanly — the same as for any other new frontend dependency.

## 2026-08-06 — Feature: profile picture, signature upload, and signing panel expansion

**Decision:** `users` gained `avatar_path`, `avatar_url`, and `signature_path` (all nullable strings) via migration `a1b2c3d4e5f7`. `avatar_path`/`avatar_url` are enforced mutually exclusive at the application layer (not a DB constraint): uploading a picture (`POST /profile/avatar`) clears `avatar_url`, and saving a URL (`PUT /auth/me/profile`) clears `avatar_path` and deletes the now-orphaned file from disk.
**Rationale:** A DB-level `CHECK` (at most one of the two set) would need to run inside the same transaction as either write and adds constraint-violation handling for what the application already enforces procedurally on both write paths — the two endpoints that can set either field already both belong to this feature, so there's no other code path that could leave them both set.

**Decision:** Uploaded avatars and signatures are stored under a fixed root (`/app/storage/avatars/{user_id}/`, `/app/storage/signatures/{user_id}/` — `services/profile_service.py`), not under the existing configurable `storage_path` setting (`services/storage_service.py`) that invoices/reports/backups use.
**Rationale:** The task specified these exact paths directly. `storage_path` exists so an Admin can relocate the financial-record backup/restore story (invoices, reports, DB backups) as a unit at runtime; profile pictures and signatures are small, per-user, cosmetic/convenience assets with no backup/restore or retention story of their own, so folding them into that same relocatable root would couple two things that change for different reasons. Tradeoff: `/app/storage` isn't a declared Docker volume in `docker-compose.yml` (unlike `/data/invoices` etc.), so on a from-scratch production deployment without the dev bind-mount (`./backend:/app`) these files wouldn't survive a container recreation — flagged here rather than fixed by editing `docker-compose.yml`, since the task scoped this build to `backend/`/`frontend/` only.

**Decision:** Avatar/signature upload validation opens every uploaded file with Pillow (`Image.open(...).verify()`, then a full `.load()`) and checks the *decoded* format against an allowlist, rather than trusting the browser-supplied `Content-Type` header or filename extension.
**Rationale:** Same principle already established for PDF uploads (`utils/file_validation.py`'s magic-byte check, see earlier decisions) — both are attacker-controlled and prove nothing about the file's actual contents. Pillow's own decoder is the "magic bytes" check for images: a file that doesn't genuinely decode as one of the allowed formats is rejected before any resize/save touches it.

**Decision:** `GET /profile/avatar/{user_id}` is reachable by any authenticated user (not just the owner) and never 404s — it redirects to `avatar_url` if set, serves the uploaded file if `avatar_path` is set, or otherwise generates and returns a coloured-initials PNG on the fly (`profile_service.generate_initials_avatar`, primary blue `#2D6B9F` matching `--kt-primary`, via Pillow `ImageDraw`/`ImageFont`). `GET /profile/signature/{user_id}` is the opposite: owner-or-Admin only, and 404s if no signature exists.
**Rationale:** Avatars are meant to appear anywhere a user is referenced (header, user list, audit log) regardless of who's viewing, and always returning *something* means every calling component (the shared `Avatar.jsx`) can render an `<img>` unconditionally with no client-side fallback branch. A signature is materially different — it can be used to automatically stamp a real document — so it gets the stricter, ownership-scoped access the task specified, and a plain 404 is fine there since the only two callers (`SignatureSection.jsx`, `SigningPanel.jsx`) both already gate on `has_signature` before fetching.

**Decision:** Signature upload converts a white background to transparent via a flat per-pixel RGB threshold (`services/profile_service._make_white_transparent`: any pixel with R,G,B all ≥ 240 gets alpha 0), not a flood-fill from the image edges or a background-removal model.
**Rationale:** The task's guidance ("sign on white paper and photograph or scan it") describes a fairly uniform, near-white background around dark ink — a global threshold handles that real-world case without the complexity (and failure modes) of edge-detection/flood-fill, which can leave background "islands" fully enclosed by a signature's loops (e.g. inside a cursive "e" or "o") opaque. The threshold is deliberately conservative (240/255) to avoid eating pale/faint ink strokes, at the cost of not clearing background that's merely off-white rather than near-pure-white — an acceptable tradeoff given the recommended white-paper capture method.

**Decision:** The signed PDF's placement box (`services/signing_service.py`) now always stamps the signer's name and date as separate text rows below the signature image (55% of the box height for the image, the remainder split evenly across 2–3 text rows depending on whether additional text was supplied), instead of the previous shape (signature 72%, a single "Signed: {date}" caption, and free text stamped in a second box *below* the placement box entirely).
**Rationale:** The task's required layout is signature → name → date → additional text, all "within the placement box" — the previous below-the-box placement for additional text could spill past the bottom of the page for a box placed low on it (nothing clamped `text_y1` against the page height in a way that kept it inside the user-drawn box). Stacking all rows inside the same box the user actually sized and positioned in the UI keeps what they see in the signing panel's preview consistent with what lands on the PDF.

**Decision:** `signer_name` is a required parameter of `signing_service.sign_invoice_pdf`, resolved server-side in `routers/invoices.py.sign_invoice` from the authenticated user (`user.display_name or user.username`) — it is not part of `InvoiceSignRequest` and the frontend never sends it.
**Rationale:** The task requires this to be automatic with "no input needed from the user," and — more importantly — accepting a name from the request body would let any signer claim a signature belongs to someone else. Deriving it from the same `User` the JWT already authenticated makes that spoof impossible without also compromising the account.

**Decision:** The frontend's `Avatar.jsx` component fetches `GET /profile/avatar/{id}` as a `Blob` (via a new `profileApi.getAvatarBlob`, same pattern as `invoicesApi.getPreview`) and renders it through an object URL, rather than a plain `<img src="/profile/avatar/{id}">`. Object URLs are cached per user ID for the tab's lifetime (module-level `Map`, not revoked on unmount) rather than re-fetched on every mount, with a manual `invalidateAvatarCache(userId)` called after the current user's own avatar-changing actions.
**Rationale:** Every API endpoint in this app requires `Authorization: Bearer`, which a plain `<img src>` can't attach — this is the only way to load an authenticated image at all here, matching how invoice preview thumbnails already work. Caching avoids re-fetching the same avatar (a network round-trip plus, for the initials fallback, a server-side Pillow render) every time e.g. the header remounts on route navigation; explicit invalidation is only needed on the one path where the served image can actually change during a session — the signed-in user editing their own avatar.

**Decision:** `frontend/index.html`'s CSP `img-src` was widened from `'self' data: blob:` to `'self' data: blob: https:`.
**Rationale:** Discovered live while browser-testing the Avatar URL tab (see Tested below): the existing CSP blocked the very feature being built, since a user-supplied avatar URL (Gravatar, DiceBear, or "any direct image URL" per the task) is by definition not a domain that can be allowlisted in advance. Restricting to `https:` (rather than a bare wildcard) still blocks `http:`/other schemes for this one directive; every other directive is untouched.

**Tested:** Rebuilt the backend Docker image (`Pillow==10.4.0` added to `requirements.txt` — previously only pulled in transitively via `qrcode[pil]`, now imported directly by `services/profile_service.py`) and ran `alembic upgrade head` against the real dev Postgres container (migration `a1b2c3d4e5f7` applied cleanly). `vite build` was clean. Exercised the backend directly with `curl`: `GET /profile/avatar/1` with neither `avatar_path` nor `avatar_url` set returns a real 256×256 PNG with a filled blue circle and correct initials; uploading a JPEG avatar resizes it to fit within 256×256 preserving aspect ratio (verified 800×600 → 256×192) and `GET /auth/me` reflects `has_avatar: true`; setting `avatar_url` via `PUT /auth/me/profile` correctly cleared the previously-uploaded `avatar_path` file from disk; `GET /profile/avatar/1` with `avatar_url` set followed the redirect to the real external image; uploading a signature photographed against a white background produced a 400×160 RGBA PNG with a fully transparent corner pixel (`(0,0,0,0)`); `GET /profile/signature/1` returned 404 for an admin viewing another user's signature that doesn't exist yet, confirming the owner/admin gate's code path runs (a full cross-user 403 wasn't exercised against a second real login, since that user's non-Superadmin account needs a live MFA flow to obtain an access token — traced by code review instead: `routers/profile.py.get_signature`'s ownership check mirrors the existing `require_admin`-style role check used throughout `routers/auth.py`). Signed a real test invoice via `POST /invoices/{id}/sign` with a display name set to "Jordan Signer" (different from the account's username) and rendered the resulting signed PDF page to a PNG with PyMuPDF: confirmed the stacked layout — signature image, then "Jordan Signer" (not the username, confirming the display-name-priority fallback), then "Signed: 2026-08-06", then the supplied additional text "Approved for payment" — all inside the placed box. Drove the full feature with headless-Chromium Playwright (Python `playwright` package) against the real dev server, logged in as Superadmin via the real `/login` flow (no MFA step, per its break-glass design): confirmed the Profile page's new Profile picture section (initials avatar, Upload/Avatar URL tabs, drag-and-drop-styled dropzone, live URL preview, Save/Clear URL) and Signature section (placeholder text when unset, upload, always-white preview frame) all render and function correctly end-to-end — upload → preview → save → reflected in the header avatar and the large profile-page avatar; the Avatar URL tab's live preview initially failed to load a real external image due to the page's CSP blocking it (fixed as described above, then re-verified with zero console errors on that path); the Settings → Users & Access active-users table shows a 32px avatar next to every row; opening the signing panel for a reviewed invoice with a saved signature defaulted to the "Use saved signature" tab (confirmed active by `aria-selected`/class) with the saved signature's real preview image loaded, a working "Draw signature" toggle, and the "Your name (…) will be added below your signature automatically" note. All test data (a test invoice, the uploaded avatar/signature files, the avatar URL, and the display name) were removed/reset via the API afterwards, confirmed via a final `GET /auth/me` showing every new field back to its default; a throwaway registered test account used only to check the signature 404 path was deactivated (not deleted — no delete-approved-user endpoint exists), matching the existing pattern of other deactivated test accounts already present in this dev database.

## 2026-08-06 — Fix: avatar URL not saving/persisting, marked dependency check

**Decision:** `frontend/index.html`'s CSP `connect-src` was widened from `'self' %VITE_API_BASE_URL%` to also include `https:`.
**Rationale:** The real bug behind "avatar URL saves but the picture reverts to a placeholder": `components/Avatar.jsx` loads an avatar via `fetch()` (required so the request carries `Authorization: Bearer`, since `GET /profile/avatar/{id}` isn't public) rather than a plain `<img src>`. When `avatar_url` is set, the backend responds to that fetch with a redirect straight to the external host (DiceBear, Gravatar, etc.) — and `connect-src`, not `img-src`, governs where a script-initiated `fetch()` is allowed to actually connect, including after following a redirect. The previous session's fix (widening `img-src` to `https:`) only covered the Avatar-URL tab's own live *preview* `<img>` (a markup-level image load, governed by `img-src`) and never actually exercised the real `Avatar` component's fetch-and-redirect path — so the underlying bug shipped unnoticed. Confirmed live in a real browser: before this fix, the browser's console showed "Connecting to 'https://api.dicebear.com/...' violates ... connect-src" and the request failed outright; after, the same fetch resolves to a 200 and renders correctly, including after a full page reload.

**Decision:** `Avatar.jsx`'s module-level cache gained a small `EventTarget`-based pub/sub (`updateEvents`), and `invalidateAvatarCache(userId)` now dispatches an `update` event that every mounted `<Avatar>` instance for that user listens for and reacts to by bumping a local `refreshTick` state (included in the fetch effect's dependency array).
**Rationale:** A second, related bug found while verifying the task's explicit "header avatar should update immediately without a page refresh" requirement: `invalidateAvatarCache` correctly deleted the stale cache entry, but an already-mounted `Avatar` instance (the header's, mounted the whole session; the profile page's own large avatar, mounted before the save) never re-ran its fetch effect, since its own props (`userId`/`token`) hadn't changed — only *newly mounting* instances would ever see the invalidation. Confirmed via a live save-without-reload test: before this fix, both the header and the profile page's own avatar kept showing the exact same cached `blob:` object URL after saving a new avatar URL; after, both instances' `<img src>` changed to a freshly fetched blob within the same render cycle the save resolved in.

**Decision:** `AvatarSection.jsx`'s URL text field now re-syncs from `user.avatar_url` via a `useEffect` keyed on that value, rather than only initialising once from it at mount (`useState(user.avatar_url || '')`).
**Rationale:** A third bug surfaced by the same investigation: uploading a picture while an avatar URL was previously set correctly cleared `avatar_url` server-side (and the mutual-exclusivity logic in `routers/auth.py`/`routers/profile.py` was already correct — the DB state was never wrong), but the Avatar URL tab's own text input kept showing whatever URL had last been typed/saved there, since that `useState` initialiser only runs once and nothing told it the backing value had changed underneath it after a sibling action (an upload) on the same component instance. Confirmed the field now correctly shows blank immediately after an upload, matching the (correctly empty) saved `avatar_url`.

**Decision:** `marked` in `frontend/package.json` (added by the earlier help-section commit, `c8eb39f`) was left unchanged.
**Rationale:** Investigated the task's premise directly rather than assuming it: `marked@^18.0.9` is already a proper `dependencies` entry, already has a matching `frontend/package-lock.json` entry, and is already installed in the running frontend container's `node_modules` at exactly that version — `18.0.9` is also confirmed (via `npm view marked version`) to be the current latest published version on the registry. The "installed manually, missing from package.json" problem this task described no longer reflects this repository's actual state — it was already fixed once `c8eb39f` landed — so there was nothing left to change here without inventing a diff.

**Tested:** Rebuilt nothing on the backend (this was a frontend-only fix; `vite build` was clean, and Vite's dev server picks up `index.html`/component changes without a container rebuild). Drove the full reported bug and its fix with headless-Chromium Playwright against the real dev server, logged in as Superadmin: confirmed the exact pre-fix failure first (console showed the `connect-src` CSP violation, the header/profile avatars showed no `<img>` at all — just the grey placeholder — despite `avatar_url` being correctly saved server-side, matching the bug report precisely), then re-confirmed after each fix. Post-fix: (1) entering a DiceBear URL and clicking Save shows the new avatar in the header *immediately*, with no reload — verified by capturing the header `<img>`'s `blob:` object-URL src before and after the save and confirming it changed; (2) reloading the page afterwards still shows the same avatar (persistence, not just an in-memory artifact); (3) uploading a picture while a URL was set correctly shows "Remove current picture" instead of the URL, and switching to the Avatar URL tab shows a blank field with no "Clear URL" button, confirming both the DB-level mutual exclusivity and the previously-stale input field are now consistent; (4) zero console errors (beyond the pre-existing, unrelated CSP `frame-ancestors`-in-`<meta>` warning noted in earlier sessions) across the whole flow. `marked`'s already-correct state was confirmed directly: `docker compose exec frontend cat node_modules/marked/package.json` shows `18.0.9`, matching `package.json`'s `^18.0.9` and `package-lock.json`'s locked entry exactly, with no `git status` diff on either file. All test avatar state (URL, uploaded file, display name) was reset to defaults afterwards via the API, confirmed via a final `GET /auth/me`.

## 2026-08-06 — Fix: saved signature not showing in signing panel, signature security verification

**Decision:** `ReviewCard.jsx`'s own `<SigningPanel>` call site (used for the upload → AI review → sign flow) gained a `user={user}` prop — it already had `user` available from its own `useAuth()` call at the top of the component, it just wasn't threaded through.
**Rationale:** This was the actual bug behind "saved signature not showing." `SigningPanel` has *two* call sites — `InvoiceDetailPage.jsx` (re-sign an already-confirmed invoice) and `ReviewCard.jsx` (sign during the initial upload/review flow) — and only the first was updated to pass `user` when the saved-signature feature was originally built; the second was missed. Without a `user` prop, `SigningPanel`'s `hasSavedSignature` (`Boolean(user?.has_signature)`) is always `false` regardless of what's actually saved, so it silently fell back to "no saved signature" behaviour (draw canvas only, no toggle) for every invoice signed via the review flow — the more common path, since most invoices are signed right after upload rather than revisited later. Confirmed live: reproduced the exact failure by uploading a fresh invoice and opening its review-card "Sign" button before the fix (no toggle, straight to the draw canvas); after adding the prop, the same flow correctly defaults to "Use saved signature" with the real saved signature previewed.

**Decision:** Added an explicit "YOUR SAVED SIGNATURE" label (`.kt-sign-saved-preview-label`) above the preview image, and a `savedSignatureFailed` state that — if the authenticated fetch of the saved signature ever fails — forces `signatureMode` to `'draw'` automatically and shows "Could not load saved signature — please draw below" (distinct from the existing "no saved signature at all" hint, which points at the profile page instead).
**Rationale:** Both were explicit requirements ("a clear label," and "fall back gracefully... with a message") that the original build didn't fully implement: it had an `alt` attribute on the image (not a visible label) and a static inline error that left the user stuck on a now-useless "Use saved signature" tab rather than actually falling back to something usable. Forcing the mode switch (rather than just showing an error) means a signature-fetch failure never blocks signing entirely — the draw canvas is still right there, already active.

**Decision:** No backend changes were needed for the security review (Fix 2) — `routers/profile.py.get_signature`'s existing owner-or-Admin/Superadmin check, authentication requirement, and lack of any user-controlled file path were all verified correct as originally built, rather than assumed correct from the earlier session's code-review-only pass (see that entry's own noted gap: "a full cross-user 403 wasn't exercised against a second real login").
**Rationale:** This session completed a real second-user test the earlier one flagged as skipped: registered a fresh throwaway account, approved it, and drove it through the actual login → MFA (`pyotp`-generated TOTP) flow to get a genuine standard-user access token (not a code-inspection substitute), then confirmed that token gets 403 fetching the Superadmin's signature, gets 404 (not 403) fetching its own — correctly still gated by "does a signature exist" rather than "who's asking" once ownership passes — and, after promoting the same account to Admin without reissuing its token, gets 200 (confirming the role check reads the live DB role via `get_current_user`'s per-request lookup, not a stale claim baked into the JWT at login). Also directly probed path traversal: a non-integer `user_id` (e.g. `abc`) is rejected by FastAPI's own `int` path-parameter validation (422) before any handler code runs, and a slash-containing/URL-encoded-slash `user_id` (e.g. `..%2f..%2fetc%2fpasswd`) never reaches the route at all — Starlette's router requires a single path segment for `{user_id}`, so it 404s at the routing layer, confirming there's no way to smuggle a traversal sequence into the `int(user_id)` FastAPI already coerces. `docs/security.md` gained a note under Authorisation documenting this as the one deliberately ownership-scoped endpoint in an otherwise role-based (not ownership-based) app.

**Tested:** `vite build` was clean after the `ReviewCard.jsx`/`SigningPanel.jsx` changes (no rebuild needed on the backend — this pass touched frontend only). Drove the real, previously-broken flow with headless-Chromium Playwright against the real dev server, logged in as Superadmin (who has a real saved signature from their own prior use of the app): uploaded a fresh invoice via `/upload`, waited for AI extraction, opened the review card's "Sign" button, and confirmed — for the first time via this exact call path — "Use saved signature" is selected by default, the "YOUR SAVED SIGNATURE" label and a real preview image are shown, and toggling to "Draw signature" correctly swaps in the empty canvas. Re-confirmed the already-working `InvoiceDetailPage.jsx` re-sign path still works unchanged. Backend security checks were run directly with `curl`: no `Authorization` header → 401; a garbage/invalid token → 401; a real standard-role user's token fetching another user's signature → 403 with `{"detail":"Not allowed to view this signature"}`; the same user fetching their own (nonexistent) signature → 404, not 403; the same token, after the account's DB role was promoted to Admin, fetching the Superadmin's signature → 200; a non-integer `user_id` → 422 (FastAPI's own type-coercion error, confirming the handler never runs); slash-containing `user_id` variants → 404 at the routing layer (Starlette's own "Not Found," confirming no traversal-shaped path ever reaches the file-lookup code); avatars remained fetchable by any logged-in user throughout (unaffected, by design). All test artifacts (two uploaded test invoices, a throwaway registered-then-promoted-then-deactivated test account) were removed/deactivated afterwards — no lasting state left beyond the account itself, matching the existing pattern of other deactivated test accounts already present in this dev database. The real user's own saved signature and avatar URL (present in this dev database from their own testing between sessions) were read but never modified.

## 2026-08-06 — Feature: full text search with PostgreSQL GIN index

**Decision:** `idx_invoices_fts` (migration `b2c3d4e5f6a8`) is built on a **weighted** tsvector — `setweight(to_tsvector('english', supplier), 'A') || setweight(..., notes, 'B') || setweight(..., filename, 'C')` — rather than the plain `to_tsvector('english', supplier || ' ' || notes || ' ' || filename)` the task brief's example `CREATE INDEX` statement showed.
**Rationale:** Every lexeme in a plain concatenated tsvector gets PostgreSQL's default weight ('D'), so there is no way for `ts_rank` on that expression to ever rank a supplier match above a filename match — "supplier weighted highest" (an explicit PART 2 requirement) would only be true in name. `services/search_service.py`'s query builds the identical weighted expression for both the `@@` match and the `ts_rank` call, so the GIN index is still usable for both — the deviation from the literal example SQL is in the expression the index (and every query against it) is built on, not in whether an index is used at all.

**Decision:** Query construction uses `to_tsquery` with the *last* word of the query given a manual `:*` prefix suffix (`services/search_service.py.build_tsquery`), not `websearch_to_tsquery` (the more obvious idiomatic choice for a user-facing search box).
**Rationale:** The instant-results dropdown searches as the user types (debounced 300ms) — the last word is very often still mid-word (e.g. "elect" on the way to "electricity"). `websearch_to_tsquery` has no prefix-matching syntax at all (it's designed for a finished, "as typed into a search engine" query with quotes/exclusion), so it would only start returning results once a whole word had been typed, defeating the point of live search-as-you-type. `to_tsquery`'s `:*` suffix does support prefix matching. User input never reaches `to_tsquery` as a raw string — `build_tsquery` only ever assembles it from `\w+`-extracted words, so tsquery operator syntax (`&`, `|`, `!`, parentheses) typed by a user can't be interpreted as anything other than a literal word to search for.

**Decision:** Highlighting uses `ts_headline` with `StartSel`/`StopSel` set to two control characters (`\x01`/`\x02`), not HTML tags — `frontend/src/utils/highlight.jsx` splits a snippet on those markers and wraps the matched spans in a real `<mark>` React element, rather than the backend ever handing back a string meant to be rendered as HTML.
**Rationale:** This app renders no `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` anywhere (confirmed by direct search during the original security audit — see `docs/security.md`), and invoice/project/contribution text is user-authored content, not trusted markup. `ts_headline`'s own `<b>...</b>` default (or any HTML `StartSel`) would require either trusting that output directly or stripping/re-parsing it client-side; unlikely-to-collide control characters sidestep the whole question by never producing HTML in the first place.

**Bug found and fixed while testing:** the invoices/contributions/projects queries originally filtered on `text_rank > 0` to decide "did this row match the text search." `ts_rank` does not return exactly `0.0` for a genuine non-match — observed values like `1e-20` — a floating-point quirk in its normalisation arithmetic, not a real (if tiny) partial match. `text_rank > 0` was therefore true for *every* row, and a search for a specific invoice amount or date returned the entire, unfiltered invoice table (confirmed live: both a `389.95` amount search and a `2026-08-05` date search returned all 111 invoices in the dev database before the fix). Fixed by adding an explicit `text_matched` boolean column (the real `@@` operator) to each query and filtering/branching on that instead of the rank score, which is now only ever used for ordering, never for deciding whether a row is a match at all.
**Rationale for calling this out specifically:** this is the kind of bug that a schema-level/unit-style check wouldn't catch (the query is syntactically and type-correct at every stage) — it only surfaced by actually running a search for a value that shouldn't have matched most of the table and noticing the result count. Verified the fix directly afterwards: the same two queries dropped from 111 results to 2 and 5 respectively, matching manual inspection of the data.

**Decision:** `services/search_service.search_contributions` matches only `contributions.group_name`, not "group_name, notes" as PART 2 specifies.
**Rationale:** `contributions` has no `notes` column — not in `docs/database-schema.md`, and not on the `Contribution` ORM model (`models/contribution.py`: `financial_year_id`, `month`, `group_name`, `amount`, `recorded_by`, `recorded_at`, `deleted`). Rather than inventing a column (a real schema change with its own migration, unused by every other contributions endpoint) to satisfy a brief that doesn't match this app's actual schema, contribution search is scoped to the one text field that genuinely exists.

**Decision:** `frontend/src/pages/ContributionsPage.jsx` gained support for an optional `?month=N` URL param (client-side filter over the already-loaded "All entries" list, with a "Filtered to {Month} · Clear filter" note), so a Contributions search result can deep-link somewhere meaningful.
**Rationale:** The task requires a contribution result to navigate to "Contributions page filtered to that month," but the page only ever loads the *current* financial year's data (`financialYearsApi.current`) — there's no existing support for jumping to an arbitrary past financial year, on this page or anywhere else in the app. Filtering the current FY's own entry list by month is a genuine, if partial, implementation of the requirement: it works correctly for the common case (a recent contribution, in the FY already showing), and is a pre-existing structural limitation of the page itself — not something a search feature should silently work around by, say, changing what financial year Contributions loads by default — for a match from an older financial year.

**Decision:** The header search bar is centred via `flex: 1; justify-content: center` on its own wrapping element, not `margin: 0 auto` competing with `.kt-header-user`'s existing `margin-left: auto` for the same flex free-space pool.
**Rationale:** Flexbox distributes free space equally across every auto-margin present in a container, not per-element — two elements each contributing auto-margins (the search bar's `margin: 0 auto` and the user area's pre-existing `margin-left: auto`) don't independently centre themselves; they end up sharing/competing for the same pool, landing the search bar off-centre. Giving the search wrapper `flex: 1` claims all space between the brand and the user area (which keeps its own auto-margin, now with nothing left to consume) as its own box, and centres the actual input within that box via `justify-content` — a single, predictable mechanism instead of two auto-margins interacting.

**Bug found and fixed while testing (mobile):** the initial mobile "collapse to icon, expand on tap" implementation expanded the search field to `46vw` inline within the header's existing flex row. At a phone viewport, the menu button + brand + expanded field + avatar + username together don't fit, and — since the header has no wrap — the expanded field rendered visually overlapping the "Keep Track" wordmark (confirmed with a real 390px-viewport screenshot). Fixed by lifting the expanded/collapsed state out of `HeaderSearch.jsx` into `Header.jsx` (`searchExpanded`, passed down as a controlled `expanded`/`onExpandedChange` prop pair) so `Header.jsx` can add a `kt-header-search-active` class to the `<header>` itself; at mobile widths that class hides `.kt-brand` entirely while search is active, freeing the space the field actually needs (now `flex: 1` instead of a fixed `46vw`) instead of fighting the brand for room. Also extended the existing click-outside/Escape handler (previously gated only on the instant-results dropdown being open) to also collapse the mobile-expanded field itself when tapping elsewhere with nothing yet typed, so there's always a way back to the normal header.

**Decision:** `POST` — really `GET /search` — logs a `search.performed` audit entry on *every* call, including each debounced instant-results request fired while a user is still mid-word, not only an Enter-triggered full-page search.
**Rationale:** The task brief is explicit about the log format and doesn't distinguish instant vs. full-page searches. Flagging the consequence rather than silently deviating: typing one 11-letter word with the 300ms debounce firing on natural typing pauses can log several partial-query audit entries for what is, from the user's perspective, a single search — a future pass revisiting audit log signal-to-noise (e.g. only logging Enter/"See all results"-triggered searches) would be reasonable, but wasn't this session's call to make unilaterally against an explicit, unambiguous instruction.

**Tested:** Ran `alembic upgrade head` against the real dev Postgres container (migration `b2c3d4e5f6a8` applied cleanly) and confirmed both `idx_invoices_fts` and `idx_categories_fts` exist via `\di`. `vite build` was clean throughout. Exercised the backend directly with `curl` against the real dev database (111 real invoices, real contributions/projects from prior sessions' use of the app): a 1-character query correctly 422s (`min_length=2`); a supplier search ("corona") returned correctly ranked, highlighted (`\x01`/`\x02`-delimited) results; an amount search ("389.95") and a date search ("2026-08-05") each correctly returned only genuinely matching rows *after* the `ts_rank` bug fix above (111 before, 2 and 5 after); a contribution group-name search and a project name/description search both matched and returned a computed `status` for the project via the existing `project_service.compute_status`; prefix matching confirmed live ("sutt" still matched "sutton", "coro" still matched "Corona Energy"); a genuinely non-matching query returned zero results across all three sections; `GET /logs/audit?action_type=search.performed` showed entries in the exact `'{username}' searched — query: '{q}', results: {N}` format. Drove the full frontend feature with headless-Chromium Playwright against the real dev server, logged in as Superadmin: the header search bar renders centred between the logo and profile area at desktop width; typing "corona" opens the instant dropdown with 5 correctly-formatted results (supplier, date, category swatch, amount) and a "See all 37 results" link; Escape closes the dropdown; clicking "See all results" navigates to `/search?q=corona&page=1&per_page=25` showing all three sections, invoice-table pagination (37 records, 2 pages), and 25 highlighted `<mark>` spans on the page; `Ctrl+K` from an unrelated page (`/invoices`) correctly focused the search input; `/search?q=a` showed the exact "Enter at least 2 characters to search" message; a nonsense query showed the exact "No results for ..." / "Try searching for a supplier name, amount, or date." empty state; clicking a contribution result navigated to `/contributions?month=8` with the new month-filter note showing and applied. Confirmed dark mode: header search field and `<mark>` highlighting both render with correct contrast (toggled via the real header theme button, not a forced class). Confirmed mobile (390×844 viewport): search collapses to an icon-only button, expands cleanly on tap with no overlap after the fix above, and collapses back on an outside tap. Zero console errors throughout (beyond the pre-existing, unrelated CSP `frame-ancestors`-in-`<meta>` warning noted in earlier sessions). No test data was created or needed cleanup this session — every search exercised was read-only against data already present in the dev database from earlier sessions' own use of the app.

## 2026-08-07 — Feature: notification centre in header with persistent notifications

**Decision:** A real `notifications` table now exists (migration `c3d4e5f6a7b9`, `models/notification.py`, `services/notification_service.py`) and is written to by `routers/dashboard.py`'s notification logic, alongside — not instead of — the live-computed banners `GET /dashboard/notifications` already returned. This directly reverses the 2026-08-04 main dashboard build's decision not to build this exact table ("every notification type... is a derived fact about current data... computing them fresh avoids the row ever going stale").
**Rationale:** That earlier reasoning was correct for a dashboard-only banner a user only sees while already looking at the dashboard. This task's actual requirement — a header-wide notification centre reachable from every page, with its own read/dismissed state that must survive a page reload and be independently mark-as-read-able — cannot be satisfied by a value recomputed fresh on every request with no persisted per-user read/dismissed flags. The live banners stay live-computed (nothing about "is the balance currently below target" needs storing); only the fact "this user has been told about it, and has or hasn't acted on it" needed a row.

**Decision:** `services/notification_service.create_notification`'s dedup rule is "same `user_id` + `type`, still unread AND undismissed" → update that row in place; otherwise insert a new one. Read *or* dismissed (either alone) is enough to let a fresh occurrence create a new row rather than silently reviving the old one.
**Rationale:** A user who dismissed last month's "balance below target" shouldn't have it silently reappear (unread) just because the balance dipped again — that would look like a notification the user never actually saw. Only a row nobody has touched yet gets its text refreshed in place, which is what stops every dashboard page load from spamming a new row per condition per visit.

**Decision:** The `notifications.type` column is named `type` (bare), matching this task's brief literally, rather than following `AuditLog`'s `action_type` convention (chosen there specifically to avoid shadowing the `type` builtin).
**Rationale:** The brief enumerates `type` as the column name explicitly. Shadowing only matters when a local variable named `type` is reassigned and the builtin is needed again in the same scope — `models/notification.py` and every caller here just read `notification.type` as an attribute, which doesn't touch the builtin at all.

**Decision:** Persistent notification creation (`notification_service.notify_admins`/`create_notification` calls inside `GET /dashboard/notifications`) runs unconditionally on every call to that endpoint, regardless of which role is viewing — only whether each condition's *live banner* gets appended to the response stays role-gated (critical-errors/audit-archived, unchanged from before: Standard/Read Only users can't reach the Settings pages those two link to).
**Rationale:** "All Admins" needs notifying even when the dashboard load that discovers the condition belongs to a Standard user (e.g. a Standard user uploads the invoice that tips the balance below target). Gating persistence itself to the viewer's role would mean Admins only ever get notified on days an Admin happens to open the dashboard first. `create_notification`'s dedup makes calling this on every request safe — it's an update-in-place, not a new row each time.

**Decision:** `reconciliation_overdue`, `stale_reconciliation`, and `project_overdue` are now real, computed dashboard notifications (`routers/dashboard.py._overdue_reconciliation_months`, `MonthlyReconciliation.is_stale`, `PlannedProject.expected_month` vs. current month) — not the placeholder comment the 2026-08-04 build left for reconciliation-overdue.
**Rationale:** That placeholder existed only because `monthly_reconciliations`/reconciliation and the planned-projects overdue concept hadn't been built yet at the time. Both exist now (see the later reconciliation and projects feature entries in this log), so this task's explicit request for these three notification types has real data to compute from. The overdue-months calculation mirrors `ReconciliationPage.jsx`'s own client-side definition (a past month, from `date_service.get_effective_start_date` onward, with no reconciliation row) rather than inventing a second one; the project-overdue check reuses `projectUrgency`'s "whole month has elapsed" definition, translated to a single SQL date comparison since `expected_month` is always stored as day 1.

**Decision:** The unsigned-invoice notification is sent two ways: one admin-wide aggregate (`notify_admins`, "N confirmed invoices still need signing") and one per-uploader personal notification (`create_notification` per distinct `Invoice.created_by` among currently-unsigned invoices, "N invoices you uploaded still need signing"). If an Admin is also an uploader, the per-uploader call runs second and overwrites that Admin's copy of the same-`type` row with their personal count (dedup is keyed on user+type, not on which call site wrote it).
**Rationale:** The brief asks for both "the invoice uploader" and "all Admins" as targets for this one notification type — they're different messages (personal vs. aggregate) but the same `type` string, so an Admin-who-uploads ends up with whichever one last executed. Accepted as a reasonable simplification rather than inventing two separate notification types for what the brief describes as one.

**Decision:** `GET /notifications`'s `dismissed` query param takes `false` (default) / `true` / `all`, not a plain boolean.
**Rationale:** The brief's own dashboard-integration requirement is self-contradictory read literally — "dismissing a banner also marks the notification dismissed" implies dismissed items disappear from the centre by default (kept in sync with the dashboard), while "the notification centre shows historical notifications that may have been dismissed from the dashboard" implies they're still visible somewhere. A three-way filter satisfies both: the default view stays in sync with dashboard dismissals (hidden), while `dismissed=true` gives access to that same historical record on request, and nothing is ever hard-deleted.

**Decision:** `DashboardPage.jsx` maps its own live banner `type` values (e.g. `balance_below_target`, `invoice_unsigned`) to the persistent table's `type` values (e.g. `balance_warning`, `unsigned_invoice`) via a small `PERSISTENT_NOTIFICATION_TYPE` lookup, fetches the current user's live persistent notifications once on mount into a `type → id` map, and uses that to call `PUT /notifications/{id}/dismiss` when a banner is dismissed. `invoice_unconfirmed` (unreviewed invoices) has no entry — it was never persisted, so its banner just dismisses locally, exactly as before this feature.
**Rationale:** The two notification "types" live in separate ID spaces (the dashboard's live banners use ad hoc string ids like `"balance_below_target"`; persistent rows use an integer primary key) and were never designed to share vocabulary — the live endpoint's `type` field predates this feature entirely. A client-side name mapping was the smallest change that lets a dashboard dismiss reach the right persistent row without changing what `GET /dashboard/notifications` returns (which frontend tidy-up passes have already built on top of — see the 2026-08-04 notification-links entry).

**Decision:** The header bell sits between `HeaderSearch` and the `kt-header-user` cluster (theme toggle + profile button) — so visually Search → Bell → Theme toggle → Avatar/name — rather than inside `kt-header-user` itself.
**Rationale:** Satisfies "between the search bar and the profile menu" directly; the theme toggle also sitting in that gap is consistent with how this header already groups small icon-buttons together ahead of the profile button.

**Decision:** The welcome greeting only replaces the always-shown username text (`Header.jsx`'s `<span className="kt-header-username">`) with a `<span className="kt-header-greeting-prefix">Good {morning/afternoon/evening}, </span>{name}` — computed once per render from `new Date().getHours()`, not kept in sync with the wall clock via an interval. The prefix is hidden below 720px (this app's existing mobile breakpoint, from `search.css`) so mobile shows just the name.
**Rationale:** A header re-renders often enough in normal use (route changes, auth state, theme toggles) that a stale greeting sitting past an hour boundary for one uninterrupted session is a cosmetic non-issue not worth a dedicated interval/timer. Reusing the existing 720px breakpoint keeps this consistent with how the rest of the header already collapses at that width, rather than introducing a second mobile threshold.

**Decision:** The bell's badge count is polled independently (`GET /notifications/count`, every 30s) from the panel's own contents (`GET /notifications`, fetched only when the panel is opened) — the panel does not stay live-subscribed while closed.
**Rationale:** Matches the brief's own description ("polled every 30 seconds... clicking opens the panel") and avoids fetching the full notification list on every page for every user when only the lightweight count is actually needed most of the time.

**Tested:** Ran `alembic upgrade head` against the real dev Postgres container — migration `c3d4e5f6a7b9` applied cleanly on top of `b2c3d4e5f6a8`. Restarted the backend container and confirmed clean startup with no import errors; `npm run build` (vite) was clean throughout. Exercised the backend directly with `curl` using a locally-minted JWT (via the app's own `utils.security.create_token`, run inside the backend container — no plaintext credentials were read or handled) for the real Superadmin account: temporarily raised `reserve_manual_amount` well above the live balance to force `balance_status: "below"`, called `GET /dashboard/notifications` and confirmed a `balance_warning` row was persisted (`GET /notifications` showed it, `GET /notifications/count` showed `unread_count: 1`); `PUT /notifications/{id}/read` flipped `read` and dropped the count to 0; `PUT /notifications/{id}/dismiss` removed it from the default (`dismissed=false`) list while `?dismissed=true` still showed it; re-triggering the same condition created a fresh row (id 3) since the old one was dismissed, confirming the dedup boundary; `PUT /notifications/read-all` and `PUT /notifications/dismiss-all` both cleared the user's remaining unread/undismissed rows and returned `unread_count: 0`. Restored `reserve_manual_amount` to its original `150` afterwards and deleted all test-created rows directly from the `notifications` table, leaving the dev database exactly as found. Drove the full frontend feature with headless-Chromium Playwright against the real dev server (localStorage seeded with the same locally-minted JWT rather than a login form submission): confirmed the header shows "Good morning, superadmin" at desktop width and the "You are all caught up!" empty state in the bell panel by default; forcing the same balance-below-target condition and waiting a real 31 seconds confirmed the badge picks it up on its own 30-second poll (showing "1"); clicking the notification row navigated to `/` and cleared the badge; re-opening and clicking the row's `×` dismissed it back to the empty state. Seeded 11 synthetic notifications across all four severities directly via `notification_service.create_notification` (bypassing the dashboard's own conditions, to check UI states the live dev data didn't happen to have) and confirmed: the badge shows "9+", all four severity icon colours (blue/amber/red/dark-red-with-pulse) render correctly, unread rows show a bold title and blue left-border tint, and the message text correctly truncates to two lines. Confirmed mobile (375×812 viewport): header shows "superadmin" with no greeting prefix, and the panel renders full-width flush under the header. Confirmed dark mode (`color_scheme="dark"`): bell, badge, and panel all render with correct contrast using the app's existing `--kt-*` theme tokens, no new hardcoded colours needed light/dark variants. Zero console errors throughout beyond the pre-existing, unrelated CSP `frame-ancestors`-in-`<meta>` warning noted in earlier sessions. All synthetic/test notification rows were deleted directly from the database afterwards; no other data was left changed.

## 2026-08-07 — Feature: feature modules system with toggleable modules and project funding target

**Decision:** A module's enabled/disabled state only ever gates **UI visibility and API access** — the background logic behind it (forecasting, reconciliation staleness checks, notification generation, the folder watcher) always keeps running, unconditionally, regardless of any module's state.
**Rationale:** The alternative — actually stopping reconciliation staleness checks, or forecast calculations, when their module is off — would mean re-enabling a module later requires "catching up" on whatever was missed, and risks a module coming back on to find its own data quietly out of sync with everything that kept moving around it (e.g. a planned project's forecast contribution, computed live from linked invoices, would need backfilling). Keeping every background process unconditional means a module toggle is purely cosmetic/access-control at the edges — flip it back on and everything is instantly, provably correct, because nothing ever stopped computing it.

**Decision:** `ModulesContext`'s 30-second poll re-syncs module state from the `modules` object embedded in the existing `GET /notifications/count` response, rather than a dedicated `GET /modules/state` (or similar) endpoint polled on its own schedule.
**Rationale:** The header notification bell (built the day before — see the previous entry) already polls `GET /notifications/count` every 30 seconds from every authenticated page. Piggybacking module state on that exact same request means a module toggle in one tab/session propagates to every other open tab on a cadence the app is already paying for, with zero additional requests-per-minute added to the backend. A second poller for what is, in practice, the same "is anything about my UI stale" question would be pure duplication.

**Decision:** `POST /invoices/{id}/sign` is gated at the endpoint level (`dependencies=[Depends(require_module("signing_export"))]` on the route itself), not the router level — the one exception among the six gated areas, all the others (`/reconciliation/*`, `/projects/*`, `/ai/*`, `/imports/*`, `/search/*`) are gated by adding `dependencies=[Depends(require_module(...))]` to the whole `APIRouter(...)`.
**Rationale:** `routers/invoices.py` is a single router mixing endpoints that belong to the `signing_export` module (`/sign`) with ordinary invoice CRUD that has nothing to do with signing (upload, list, confirm, delete) — gating the whole router would incorrectly 403 uploading and reviewing invoices whenever signing is turned off. Every other gated router belongs entirely to one module, so router-level gating is both correct and less code (one dependency declaration instead of one per endpoint).

**Decision:** AI Extraction being disabled (the module toggle) is a separate signal from AI being disabled (the pre-existing `ai_enabled` setting, which governs the actual provider on/off and is a form of graceful degradation, not access control) — `ReviewCard.jsx` checks `useModules().isEnabled('ai_extraction')` directly for its "AI extraction is disabled" banner, rather than relying on `GET /ai/status` (which is itself now gated behind the module and 403s when the module is off, so `aiStatus` simply stays `null` and its own banners don't fire).
**Rationale:** These answer genuinely different questions — "is this whole feature area reachable at all" (module) vs. "is the configured AI provider currently able to run" (setting, already handled by existing degrade-to-manual-entry logic documented in architecture.md's AI Layer section). Conflating them would mean a site that's deliberately turned AI Extraction off as a module still shows "AI not configured, fields must be filled in manually" — a not-configured message for a feature that was never meant to be there at all, which is a materially different and more confusing message than "this feature is disabled."

**Decision:** A funding-target project's "monthly surplus needed" and "current monthly surplus" are computed against the **app's overall current balance and recent income/spend trend** (`services/financial_year_service.current_money_on_hand`/`average_monthly_income`), not against that project's own `estimated_cost`/`actual_cost` (the figures the existing project-vs-budget tracking already computes).
**Rationale:** "Saving toward this project" and "tracking actual spend against an estimate" are different questions about the same project row. A project like "new roof, £3,000 by June" isn't paid for in instalments tracked as linked invoices arrive — it's a savings goal funded out of the organisation's or individual's whole balance. Reusing `actual_cost` (sum of linked invoices) for this would silently require invoices to already be linked to a project nobody has spent anything on yet, which doesn't fit how a savings goal works. The two modes can coexist on the same project (a project can have both an estimate tracked against invoices *and* a separate funding target), which only makes sense if they're computed from genuinely independent inputs.

**Decision:** Debt Tracking and Budget Planning are kept as two separate, distinct modules from Planned Projects, not folded into it or into each other.
**Rationale:** Planned Projects (with its new funding-target mode) covers *saving toward or spending on a specific, named thing* — a roof repair, a laptop, a holiday. Budget Planning is a different shape of problem entirely: setting recurring monthly ceilings per category and tracking spend against them, with no single target amount or completion date. Debt Tracking is different again: liabilities with balances that go down over time (loans, credit cards, mortgages), not spend to be planned for. Each has its own data model and its own "done" condition, and merging any two would mean overloading one table/UI with fields that are meaningless in the other's context — the project brief's explicit `requires_setup` treatment for both (letting an Admin name the module themselves) reflects that neither has one obvious universal shape yet, unlike the concretely-scoped modules that shipped fully built.

**Decision:** Folder Integration is a single module covering **both** auto-import from a watched input folder and auto-export of signed PDFs to an output folder, rather than two separate modules.
**Rationale:** Both directions of this feature share one underlying mechanism (a filesystem folder Keep Track watches/writes to) and one Settings screen (Storage & Backup) for configuring the paths — the KHOC deployment this app was built for wants both halves together as a matched pair (invoices come in from a shared network folder, signed PDFs go back out to one), and there's no use case in mind where an organisation would want automatic input but manual output, or vice versa, that would justify the extra toggle and setup-prompt surface of a second module.

**Decision:** `/settings` changed from an Admin-only route (`RequireAdmin`) to any-authenticated-role (`RequireAuth`), with `SettingsNav`/`SettingsPage` restricting Standard and Read Only users down to just the General category client-side, and `GeneralSettings.jsx` itself short-circuiting to render only `FeatureModulesSettings` (skipping instance name, app start date, financial year, and target reserve — all still Admin-only) for those roles.
**Rationale:** The brief requires Standard and Read Only users to see the Feature Modules list (with its toggle greyed out) — impossible while the entire Settings page sat behind an Admin-only route guard. Restricting the visible category to just General, and General itself to just the module list for non-Admins, was chosen over opening every settings category to every role, since every other category (Users, Security, Logs, AI config, Storage, System Reset) has components built assuming Admin and calls backend endpoints that are still `require_admin`-gated regardless of what the frontend shows — narrowing the *frontend* surface to match what those roles could actually do avoided a much larger audit of every settings sub-component while still satisfying this task's specific requirement.

**Not addressed in this pass:** Folder Integration's actual watched-folder auto-import/export behaviour, and the Debt Tracking/Budget Planning feature areas themselves, are not built — only the module toggle, its `requires_setup` flag, and its setup prompt exist. This matches the task brief (build the module *system*, not every module's underlying feature), but means enabling either module today shows it in navigation with no page behind it yet.

**Tested:** `python3 -m py_compile` across every touched backend file (models, services, routers, `utils/deps.py`) — clean. `npx vite build` (production build) — clean, no new warnings beyond the pre-existing chunk-size notice. Ran `alembic upgrade head` against the real dev Postgres container (via `docker exec` into the already-running `keeptrack-backend-1`) — migrations `8f37433201a6` (`feature_modules` table + seed) and `ea9d466ba063` (funding-target columns on `planned_projects`) applied cleanly on top of `c3d4e5f6a7b9`; `psql` confirmed all 9 modules seeded with exactly the documented `enabled`/`default_enabled`/`requires_setup` values, and the four new `planned_projects` columns exist with the right types/defaults. Restarted the backend container — clean startup, no import errors — while a real browser session was actively polling it (`GET /notifications/count` every ~30s), confirming no disruption to an in-progress session. Exercised the API directly with `curl` using locally-minted JWTs (via the app's own `utils.security.create_token`, run inside the backend container — no plaintext credentials read or handled) for the real Superadmin account and two temporarily-repurposed pre-existing test accounts (one already-active Standard account, one already-deactivated test account temporarily reactivated as Read Only for the duration of the test and restored to its exact original state — deactivated, `standard` — immediately after): confirmed `GET /modules` returns 200 for Standard and Read Only alike; confirmed `PUT /modules/reconciliation` returns 403 for a Standard user and succeeds for the Superadmin; toggling Reconciliation off made `GET /reconciliation` return 403 with the documented message for the Standard user while all 5 existing `monthly_reconciliations` rows remained untouched in the database throughout; `GET /notifications/count`'s `modules.reconciliation` flipped to `false` on the same request; re-enabling restored `GET /reconciliation` immediately, returning the identical 5 rows; both the disable and enable were recorded to `audit_log` (`module.disabled`/`module.enabled`) with the correct actor. Created a real funding-target planned project via `POST /projects` (£2,000 target, 4 months out) and confirmed the response's computed `monthly_surplus_needed`, `current_monthly_surplus`, `on_track`, and `projected_completion` were arithmetically correct given the live dev balance, and that the same figures appeared in `GET /dashboard/summary`'s `planned_projects` list. All test-created/modified state was cleaned up afterwards: the temporarily-repurposed test account was restored to its exact prior role/active state, the test project was deactivated then deleted outright (it was synthetic test data, not a real record), and every module was confirmed back at its seeded default (`reconciliation`/`planned_projects`/`signing_export`/`ai_extraction`/`bulk_import`/`full_text_search` enabled, `folder_integration`/`debt_tracking`/`budget_planning` disabled) — `audit_log` rows from the test were left in place, consistent with it being a permanent record of real actions taken, not scratch data. Frontend changes were observed picked up live via the dev container's Vite HMR (no build errors, no new console/server errors introduced) rather than a separate scripted browser pass.
