## 2026-08-07 — Fix: Gravatar server-side fetch and cache with refresh option

**Decision:** Gravatar images are now fetched and saved server-side (`routers/profile.py`'s new `POST /profile/avatar/gravatar` and `POST /profile/avatar/gravatar/refresh`, both via `httpx.get` against `https://www.gravatar.com/avatar/{md5}?s=256&d=404`), then processed and stored exactly like an uploaded picture (`services/profile_service.save_avatar`, extended with a `force_format="PNG"` option so a Gravatar-fetched image always lands as `avatar.png` regardless of what Gravatar itself served). The old "Avatar URL" free-text tab — a client-side `fetch()` of an arbitrary external image URL — is removed entirely from the profile page.
**Rationale:** This is the same underlying CSP problem recorded in the two 2026-08-06 entries above, back again in a different guise: `connect-src` restrictions block the *browser* from fetching an arbitrary external host, and Gravatar is exactly that. The previous fix widened `connect-src`/`img-src` to `https:` to let the browser fetch anywhere; this fix instead removes the need for the browser to fetch external avatar hosts at all for the one flow that matters (Gravatar) — the backend does it once, saves the result, and the frontend only ever loads the already-authenticated `GET /profile/avatar/{id}` endpoint it already used for uploaded pictures. Deliberately narrower in scope than a general "any URL" avatar feature: Gravatar is a single well-known, predictable host, so a strict `d=404` lookup with a clear "no Gravatar found" error is tractable in a way that validating and rendering *any* attacker-influenceable URL server-side would not be.
**Decision:** `frontend/index.html`'s CSP widening (`img-src`/`connect-src` including `https:`) was left in place, not reverted, and the backend's `avatar_url` column/redirect path (`GET /profile/avatar/{id}` still redirects if `avatar_url` is set) and `PUT /auth/me/profile`'s `avatar_url` field were left intact.
**Rationale:** Scoped the fix to what was asked — removing the *frontend tab* that set `avatar_url`, not the underlying capability, which remains a valid (if now UI-unreachable) mechanism the API still exposes. Reverting the CSP widening now that the only UI path to `avatar_url` is gone would be a reasonable follow-up but wasn't requested and risks re-breaking that dormant path for no immediate benefit; noted here rather than done silently.
**Decision:** A new nullable `users.avatar_source` column (`'upload'` or `'gravatar'`, migration `fc25303f4d74`) tracks which flow produced the file at `avatar_path`, and a new `avatar_fetched_at` property on the `User` model reads the avatar file's own filesystem mtime rather than being a separate stored timestamp.
**Rationale:** The task requires showing a "Refresh from Gravatar" button only when the current avatar came from Gravatar — but an upload and a Gravatar fetch can produce an identically-named `avatar.png`, so the file itself can't disambiguate; nothing short of an explicit flag works. The "Last fetched" date, by contrast, doesn't need its own column: the file's mtime already *is* that timestamp, exactly as the task specified ("use the avatar file's modification date"), so reading it directly avoids a redundant, driftable duplicate of state the filesystem already tracks accurately.
**Decision:** `avatar_source` is reset to `"upload"` on a plain upload and cleared to `null` on delete or when `avatar_url` is set via `PUT /auth/me/profile` — it is never left stale pointing at a file that's since been replaced or removed.
**Rationale:** Every code path that changes what `avatar_path`/`avatar_url` point to needs to keep `avatar_source` consistent with it, the same mutual-exclusivity discipline already applied to `avatar_path`/`avatar_url` themselves — an avatar showing "Refresh from Gravatar" after being overwritten by an upload would silently refresh the wrong thing (or 404) the next time someone clicked it.
**Decision:** `httpx.get` to Gravatar (10-second timeout, `follow_redirects=True`, synchronous, matching the existing simple pattern in `services/ai_provider_service.py`'s Ollama check) treats a Gravatar `404` as a clean, user-facing error (`HTTPException(404, "No Gravatar found for this email address. Visit gravatar.com to set one up.")`) and any other non-200 or network failure as a generic `502`, rather than propagating Gravatar's own response body or a raw exception.
**Rationale:** `d=404` on the Gravatar URL is exactly what turns "no picture set" into a clean 404 instead of a default placeholder image (Gravatar's normal behaviour without that parameter), which is what makes a reliable "no Gravatar found" error possible at all — confirmed directly with `curl` against the real Gravatar API before wiring it into the endpoint (with `d=404`: `404`; without: `200`, a generic placeholder). A 502 for anything else avoids leaking raw upstream errors or a stack trace for something the user can't fix from within Keep Track.

**Tested:** `python3 -m py_compile` across every touched backend file — clean. `npx vite build` — clean, no new warnings. Ran `alembic upgrade head` against the real dev Postgres container: migration `fc25303f4d74` applied cleanly on top of `ea9d466ba063`, confirmed via `psql` that `avatar_source` exists and is `null` for all existing users. Restarted the backend container — clean startup. Exercised the backend directly with `curl` using a real login-issued JWT for the Superadmin account: confirmed `POST /profile/avatar/gravatar` with an email known to have no Gravatar (verified independently against the real Gravatar API first) returns the exact documented 404 message; with the operator's real email (confirmed via a direct `curl` to Gravatar first: `200 image/png`) returns 200 with `avatar_source: "gravatar"`, `has_avatar: true`, and a populated `avatar_fetched_at`, and the file on disk at `/app/storage/avatars/1/avatar.png` is a genuine 256×256 RGBA PNG; `POST /profile/avatar/gravatar/refresh` correctly used the *stored* account email rather than the one just fetched with (returned the same 404 until the account's email was changed via `PUT /auth/me/profile` to match, then succeeded and bumped `avatar_fetched_at`); a plain `POST /profile/avatar` upload afterwards correctly replaced the file (`avatar.png` removed, `avatar.jpg` written) and flipped `avatar_source` to `"upload"`; `GET /profile/avatar/1` served the uploaded image with the correct content-type; `DELETE /profile/avatar` cleared `has_avatar`/`avatar_source` back to their defaults. Drove the full feature with headless-Chromium Playwright against the real dev server, logged in as Superadmin via the real login flow: confirmed the profile page's Gravatar tab (replacing the old Avatar URL tab, which no longer exists — checked its absence explicitly) shows the pre-filled email, the amber "saved to Keep Track / click Refresh" note, and the gravatar.com link; fetching a real Gravatar updated both the profile page's own avatar and the header avatar immediately, with no page reload, and switched the note under the avatar to "Gravatar picture"; the tab then correctly switched to showing "Last fetched: 7 August 2026" and a "Refresh from Gravatar" button (plus a "Use a different email" escape hatch) instead of the initial email-entry form; clicking Refresh worked and updated the fetched date; entering a nonexistent address via "Use a different email" showed the exact error message inline; switching to the Upload tab and uploading a picture correctly replaced the Gravatar avatar everywhere (header, profile page) and the note changed to "Uploaded picture". Zero console errors beyond the pre-existing, unrelated CSP `frame-ancestors`-in-`<meta>` warning noted in earlier sessions, and one expected 404 from the deliberate no-Gravatar-found test. All test state (the account's temporarily-changed email, the fetched/uploaded avatar) was reset to its original values afterwards via the API, confirmed with a final `psql` query showing `avatar_path`/`avatar_source` both back to empty and the email restored.

## 2026-08-07 — Fix: consolidate help guides to a single backend-served source

**Decision:** The 9 guides under `user-guides/` are now served live by the backend (`backend/routers/help.py`'s `GET /help` and `GET /help/{guide_key}`, reading from `/app/user-guides/{filename}`) rather than copied into `frontend/public/help-guides/` at all. `docker-compose.yml`'s `backend` service gained `./user-guides:/app/user-guides:ro`. `frontend/public/help-guides/` and the frontend's own static `HELP_TOPICS` list (`frontend/src/help/topics.js`) were deleted outright; `HelpPage.jsx` now fetches the ordered guide list from `GET /help` and each guide's markdown from `GET /help/{guide_key}` instead of importing a hardcoded array and `fetch()`-ing `/help-guides/*.md` as static assets.
**Rationale:** This is exactly the follow-up the 2026-08-06 "help section" entry flagged as a known gap: that build was scoped to `frontend/` only, so it could only reach `user-guides/` by copying it into `frontend/public/`, creating two copies that silently drift whenever one is edited without the other (recorded in `docs/backlog.md` Section 1). This pass is explicitly allowed to touch `docker-compose.yml`/`backend/`, so the duplication is removed at the root: one file on disk, one place that reads it. Keeping the guide list itself only in `backend/routers/help.py` (not mirrored in a frontend constant) applies the same single-source principle to the *ordering/titles* metadata, not just the content — a second hardcoded copy of the topic list would have reintroduced the identical class of drift bug one layer up.
**Rationale (mount path):** `/app/user-guides` was chosen (rather than something outside `/app`) because the backend service already bind-mounts `./backend:/app`; nesting the read-only guides mount inside that existing mountpoint needs no new top-level container path and matches the task's explicit instruction.

**Decision:** `GET /help/{guide_key}` returns the raw markdown as `PlainTextResponse` with `Cache-Control: max-age=300`, and both endpoints require only `get_current_user` (any authenticated, approved, active role — no `require_standard`/admin gate), matching every other role's existing read access to guides before this change.
**Rationale:** Guides are reference material, not a role-gated feature — the pre-existing frontend build made them available to any logged-in user, and narrowing that now would be an unrelated behaviour change outside this task's scope. The 5-minute cache header reflects that guide content changes rarely (an edit to `user-guides/` is a deliberate, infrequent action), while still being short enough that an edit shows up for a user within minutes without needing a hard refresh.

**Decision:** `HelpPage.jsx` fetches the guide list, then all 9 guides' content, in one `useEffect` on mount (list first, then a `Promise.all` over `GET /help/{key}` for each) — preserving the original "fetch everything up front so search matches content, not just titles" design — but now tracks per-guide fetch failures in a `guideErrors` map (rendering "Could not load this guide. Please try again." for just that guide) separately from a `topicsError` flag for the list call itself failing outright, and shows a spinner (new `.kt-help-spinner`, reusing the app's existing shared `kt-spin` keyframe) while either is in flight.
**Rationale:** Splitting list-load failure from a single guide's content-load failure gives a more accurate error: if only one guide's file were somehow missing or unreadable on the backend, the other 8 should still work rather than the whole page showing one generic failure. Reusing `kt-spin` (already defined globally for the pagination/AI-test button spinners) avoids a second, slightly different spin animation existing in the codebase for the same visual purpose.

**Decision:** `frontend/src/utils/api.js` gained a `requestText()` helper (parallel to the existing `requestBlob()`) and `helpApi.get()` uses it, since guide content is returned as plain text, not JSON — the existing `request()` helper always calls `res.json()`.
**Rationale:** Matches the existing pattern in this file of a dedicated fetch wrapper per response shape (`request` for JSON, `requestBlob`/`requestBlobPost` for binary downloads) rather than overloading `request()` with a response-type flag.

**Decision:** `user-guides/README.md` was added, stating guides are the single source of truth, served live by the backend, and that edits appear without a rebuild. `docs/backlog.md`'s Section 1 duplication-bug line and Section 2's dependent "Help guides consolidated" sub-bullet under Production Packaging were both removed (not checked off in place), per this document's own stated convention.
**Rationale:** The README guards against the same duplication mistake recurring the next time a guide needs editing — the original bug existed in the first place because nothing on disk said not to copy the files elsewhere.

**Tested:** `python3 -m py_compile` on every touched backend file — clean. Ran `docker compose down && docker compose up -d` (required, since the volume mount is a `docker-compose.yml` change, not something a plain restart picks up) — clean startup, `docker exec keeptrack-backend-1 ls /app/user-guides/` confirmed all 9 guides plus the new `README.md` visible read-only inside the container. `curl` with a locally-minted JWT (via the app's own `create_token`, run inside the backend container): `GET /help` with no `Authorization` header returns 401; with a valid token returns exactly the 9 guides in the documented order/titles; `GET /help/getting-started` returns 200 with `Cache-Control: max-age=300` and the real markdown; `GET /help/nonexistent-guide` returns 404. Edited `user-guides/getting-started.md` directly on the host (appended a marker line) and re-requested `GET /help/getting-started` without restarting or rebuilding anything — the new line appeared immediately, confirming the live-edit requirement; the edit was then reverted via `git checkout`. `npx vite build` inside the frontend container initially failed with `marked` unresolved — a pre-existing drift between `package.json` (which has depended on `marked` since the 2026-08-06 help feature) and the container's `/app/node_modules` volume, unrelated to this change; running `npm install` inside the container fixed it and the build then completed cleanly with no new warnings. Drove the real dev server with headless-Chromium Playwright, logged in as Superadmin via the real login flow: `/help` rendered all 9 nav topics in the correct order; the default guide's content loaded and rendered as HTML; searching "reconcil" filtered to the topics whose title or fetched content actually contains it (title matches plus cross-link mentions in other guides' text, confirming search runs against backend-fetched content as required); clicking a different topic (Reconciliation) loaded its distinct content; navigating directly to `/help?topic=uploading-invoices` (the same deep link every `?` icon uses) loaded that guide directly, confirming the quick-help links work unchanged; zero new browser console errors beyond the pre-existing, unrelated CSP `frame-ancestors`-in-`<meta>` warning already noted in earlier entries. Confirmed `frontend/public/help-guides/` no longer exists on disk, and that a direct request to its old dev-server URL now falls through to the SPA's `index.html` (React Router, not a stale copy of the markdown) rather than serving old content.

## 2026-08-07 — Folder Integration module build

**Decision:** The input-folder watcher runs as a background thread inside the `backend` container (`services/folder_watcher_service.py`, started from `main.py`'s startup hook), not the separate `watched-folder-watcher` container the compose file originally shipped as a placeholder. That service and its `services/folder_watcher.py` entry point are both removed outright.
**Rationale:** The placeholder container bought nothing — it exited immediately and shared no code with the backend, meaning a real implementation there would need its own DB session handling, its own copy of the AI extraction/invoice-creation pipeline, and an IPC mechanism back to the backend just to write to the same database and call the same AI provider abstraction. Running it as a thread inside the backend (the exact pattern already established by `services/maintenance_service.py`'s 90-day retention scheduler and daily backup checker — a plain `threading.Thread(daemon=True)` loop, not APScheduler) gets direct, zero-IPC access to every service function it needs (`extract_invoice_data`, `save_invoice_pdf`, `notification_service`, `audit_service`) and follows this project's established minimal-infrastructure convention for background jobs (see the retention-scheduler decision entry above: "Adding APScheduler... would be more machinery than the requirement warrants").

**Decision:** SMB shares are connected to directly and natively, via the `smbclient` high-level API bundled with the `smbprotocol` PyPI package (`services/folder_service.py`) — no OS-level CIFS mount, no `mount.cifs`, nothing outside the Python process. NFS shares, by contrast, are **not** given the same native-client treatment; the user guide instructs mounting an NFS share at the OS/host level and pointing Keep Track's "local path" folder type at that mount, with a Proxmox-specific note (mount on the host, pass through as a Docker volume).
**Rationale:** `smbprotocol` is a mature, pure-Python SMB2/3 client with no external mount tooling or elevated container privileges required — a Docker container can speak SMB entirely in userspace. NFS has no equivalent well-maintained pure-Python client library worth taking on as a dependency for this app's scope, and NFS mounting is inherently an OS/kernel-level operation (`mount -t nfs`) that a containerised Python process can't perform on itself without privileged capabilities Keep Track's containers deliberately don't run with. Treating NFS as "mount it outside the app, then use Keep Track's existing local-path folder type" reuses 100% of the already-built local filesystem code path and asks nothing of the container beyond what a Docker volume mount already provides.

**Decision:** Duplicate detection for the input folder is **filename-based** (a lookup in `folder_watcher_log` for a prior `status='completed'` row with the same filename), not content-hash-based (e.g. SHA-256 of the file bytes).
**Rationale:** The task's own worked scenario — "drop the same PDF twice" — and the existing manual-upload duplicate flag (`services/ai_service.check_duplicate`, matched on supplier/amount/date, not file content) both point at filename as the natural, cheap signal for "have I already picked this file up from this folder." A content hash would catch a genuinely byte-identical re-drop but miss the much more common real-world case (a supplier's portal re-exports the "same" invoice as a new PDF with different metadata/timestamps embedded, producing a different hash) while adding a full-file read and hash computation to every poll purely to prevent a case filename matching already prevents. The existing `duplicate_flag` content-similarity check (supplier/amount/date, ±2%/±7 days) still runs on every watched-folder invoice exactly as it does on a manual upload — the two duplicate concepts are complementary, not competing: filename-based skips *reprocessing a file the watcher has already seen*, while `duplicate_flag` catches *a different file that looks like the same invoice*, and both matter independently.

**Decision:** Processed input files are moved into a `processed/` subfolder of the input folder root, never deleted. No automated cleanup of `processed/` ships in this pass — the user guide explicitly documents it as a manual/future task ("we recommend archiving or deleting files older than 3 months... automated housekeeping is planned for a future version"), and `docs/backlog.md`'s V2 wishlist gained an entry for it.
**Rationale:** Deleting the original PDF the moment it's imported would remove the one artefact that lets an Admin verify what was actually read off a supplier's original document if the AI extraction got something wrong, or recover from a bug in the import pipeline — the original upload flow already keeps every original PDF indefinitely in `{storage}/invoices/original/`, and the watched folder's `processed/` archive is the same safety margin applied to the watcher's own copy of the file. Building automated housekeeping (retention rules, archiving) for a folder that's the customer's own storage (not Keep Track's managed `storage_path`) is materially riskier to get wrong than the existing 90-day audit/error log retention this app already has, and wasn't asked for — flagging it in the guide and backlog rather than silently deleting files a user might still need was judged the safer default.

**Decision:** Output filenames follow `/FY{year}-{year+1}/{Month}/{supplier}_{date}_{filename}.pdf` (e.g. `/FY2025-26/August/CoronaEnergy_2026-08-01_invoice.pdf`), computed independently of `FinancialYear.label`'s own display format (`"FY2025/26 (Sep–Aug)"`) via the same underlying `fy_bounds_for()` start/end dates.
**Rationale:** `FinancialYear.label` is built for on-screen display (a slash and an en-dash month range) and isn't filesystem/path-safe on every OS or SMB share; the task's own worked example is a plain hyphenated year range with no other punctuation, so `_output_relative_path()` derives the folder segment directly from `fy_bounds_for()`'s start/end years rather than reusing or parsing the display label. The supplier name is stripped of everything but letters and digits (`CoronaEnergy`, not `Corona Energy` or `Corona-Energy`) for the same filesystem-safety reason, matching the task's own example casing.

**Decision:** Historical/needs-review classification for a watched-folder invoice compares the AI-extracted `invoice_date` against the existing `app_start_date` setting (`services/date_service.py`) — before it: `is_historical=True`, `reviewed=True` (skips the review queue, same as a bulk historical CSV/PDF import); on or after it: `is_historical=False`, `reviewed=False` (goes to the review queue, same as a manual upload). If `app_start_date` is unset, every watched-folder invoice is treated as non-historical (needs review).
**Rationale:** `app_start_date` already exists in this app for exactly this purpose — "which months are before the point this deployment started actively tracking data" — and is the one existing setting the task's own brief ("before the app start date: marked historical, skip review") maps onto directly, rather than inventing a second, folder-specific cutoff date setting. Defaulting to "not historical" when `app_start_date` is unset means an Admin who enables Folder Integration before ever configuring a start date gets the safer behaviour (everything lands in the review queue for a human to check) rather than silently skipping review for invoices with no defined boundary to compare against.

**Decision:** Watched-folder invoices are attributed (`invoices.created_by`) to the oldest existing Admin account, falling back to Superadmin only if no Admin exists yet (`folder_watcher_service._system_actor_id`).
**Rationale:** `invoices.created_by` is a `NOT NULL` foreign key with no "system" sentinel value, and adding one (a nullable column, or a synthetic system user row) would be a larger schema change than this feature warrants for what's fundamentally a bookkeeping detail — every functioning Keep Track install already has at least one Admin (created by the mandatory setup wizard) before any real data, including a watched folder being configured, can exist. Superadmin is deliberately the fallback, not the first choice, since it's an optional, env-var-bootstrapped recovery account this app's own architecture doc describes as "not used day-to-day."

**Decision:** A new `invoices.import_source` column (nullable string, migration `0012ffa339a9`) is set to `'watched_folder'` for auto-imported invoices and left `null` for everything else (manual upload, bulk CSV/PDF import — which already has its own `import_batch_id` for that purpose).
**Rationale:** Nothing on the existing `Invoice` row could otherwise answer "did this arrive via the watched folder" — `import_batch_id` specifically means "part of a bulk import batch," which a folder-watched invoice isn't. A single nullable string column (rather than a boolean, anticipating no other source needing to be distinguished yet) follows this app's own established pattern for the same kind of provenance flag (`is_historical`, `import_batch_id` themselves were added the same way, as the features that needed them were built).

**Decision:** Folder Integration gets its **own** Settings → Data sub-page (`FolderIntegrationSettings.jsx`, nav key `folder`), not folded into Storage & Backup as an earlier decisions-log entry (2026-08-07, "Feature module toggles build") anticipated when only the module toggle existed.
**Rationale:** That earlier note was written before any of this feature's actual configuration surface existed, reasoning from "it's a filesystem path setting, so it's like Storage & Backup." In practice this module needs two multi-field connection configurations (local/SMB, each with 6+ fields), a poll interval, an output behaviour choice, live connection testing, and a status/activity panel — materially more UI than Storage & Backup's existing scope, and conflating the two would make an already-dense page harder to scan. The setup prompt shown when an Admin enables the module (`FeatureModulesSettings.jsx`) was updated to link to `/settings?section=folder` accordingly.

**Decision:** Folder configuration is saved via two bespoke endpoints, `PUT /folder/input-config` and `PUT /folder/output-config` (`routers/folder.py`), rather than the generic `PUT /settings/{key}` endpoint every other simple setting uses.
**Rationale:** `folder_*_smb_password` must be Fernet-encrypted before it's written to the `settings` table (same requirement as `ai_api_key`), and the generic settings endpoint has no per-key encryption hook — `routers/ai_settings.py`'s existing `PUT /ai/config` already establishes this exact pattern (a bespoke endpoint wrapping several related settings keys plus one that needs `encrypt_secret()`) for the identical reason, so Folder Integration reuses it rather than inventing a second approach. `GET /folder/config` mirrors `GET /ai/config`'s `api_key_set` boolean with `smb_password_set`, so the frontend can show "a password is set" without the ciphertext ever reaching the browser.

**Decision:** The "View existing invoice" / "Process anyway" pair of actions on a duplicate-detected notification are implemented by encoding both the destination invoice id (as the notification's `link` path) and the original filename (as a `duplicate_filename` query parameter on that same `link`) into the one URL field `Notification` already has, rather than adding an "actions"/metadata column to the `notifications` table.
**Rationale:** Every other notification in this app is a single click-to-navigate row; this is the first one that needs two distinct actions. Extending the schema (a JSONB actions column, or per-type frontend logic reading a new field) is more machinery than solving it within the existing `link` string, since everything both actions need — which invoice, which file — already fits in a URL. `NotificationBell.jsx` special-cases `type === 'folder_duplicate_detected'` to render two buttons from that one link instead of navigating on click; every other notification type is completely unaffected.

**Decision:** Poll interval options are 30 seconds, 1 minute (default), 5 minutes, and 30 minutes (`folder_service.POLL_INTERVAL_CHOICES`), read fresh from the `folder_input_poll_interval` setting at the start of every watcher loop iteration rather than fixed for the process lifetime.
**Rationale:** 1 minute matches the task brief's stated default and is frequent enough that a dropped invoice reaches the review queue within a reasonable wait without polling so aggressively that a slow/rate-limited SMB share or a large input folder is scanned needlessly often. Re-reading the setting every iteration (instead of only at startup) means changing the interval in Settings takes effect from the very next poll, consistent with how every other runtime setting in this app (signing toggle, terminology, reserve calculation) already applies immediately without a restart.

**Decision:** `write_to_output_folder()` never raises — every failure (connection, write) is caught internally and turned into an `error_log` entry, an Admin notification, an `audit_log` entry (`folder.output_failed`), and a `{written: False, error: ...}` return value. It's called unconditionally from `POST /invoices/{id}/sign` after every successful sign (the function's own enabled/behaviour checks make this a no-op when output is off or browser-only), and separately exposed as `POST /invoices/{id}/export-to-folder` for a manual retry from the invoice detail page.
**Rationale:** A failure to write to a network share must never break the signing flow that triggered it — the PDF is already signed and saved locally at that point regardless of what happens next, and the task brief's own "If write fails: create notification with retry option" implies signing itself already succeeded. Exposing the same underlying writer as its own endpoint (rather than only ever firing automatically) gives a concrete "retry option": an Admin who fixes a bad SMB password can immediately retry a specific invoice from its own detail page instead of waiting for the invoice to be re-signed.

**Not addressed in this pass:** SMB connectivity was implemented against the `smbprotocol`/`smbclient` API surface and reviewed carefully against its documented behaviour, but not exercised against a real SMB server in this environment (none was available) — see the Tested note below for what local-filesystem and code-level verification was actually performed. Output-folder workflows beyond a raw filesystem/SMB write (email, cloud storage, webhooks) remain a `docs/backlog.md` V2 item, as does automated `processed/` housekeeping (see above).

**Tested:** `python3 -m py_compile` across every new/touched backend file (models, services, routers, migration) — clean. Rebuilt the `backend` image (picking up `smbprotocol`) and brought the stack up with `docker compose up -d --remove-orphans`, which cleanly removed the old crash-looping `watched-folder-watcher` container; backend startup logs showed no import errors and no exceptions from the new watcher thread. Ran `alembic upgrade head` against the real dev Postgres container — migration `0012ffa339a9` applied cleanly on top of `fc25303f4d74`; confirmed via `psql` that all 18 `folder_*` settings rows were seeded with the documented defaults, `folder_watcher_log` exists with its status check constraint and FK, and `invoices.import_source` exists and is `null` for pre-existing rows. Exercised the real API end-to-end with `curl` using a locally-minted JWT (the app's own `create_token`, run inside the backend container) for the real Superadmin account: enabled the `folder_integration` module; configured and tested both the input and output folders as local paths (`/data/watched`, `/data/output` — the two Docker volumes this task added); copied a genuine small PDF (`%PDF-1.4` header, real xref/trailer, with "Test Supplier Ltd" / "Invoice Date: 2026-08-05" / "Total: 42.50" as page text) into the input folder and confirmed within one 30-second poll it was picked up, AI-extracted **correctly** (this dev environment has a working Anthropic key — supplier, date, and amount all matched the source text exactly), logged `detected` → `completed` in `folder_watcher_log`, moved into `processed/`, and produced a `folder_new_invoice` notification; re-copied the identical filename and confirmed a `duplicate_flagged` log row plus a `folder_duplicate_detected` notification whose `link` correctly encoded both the existing invoice id and the `duplicate_filename` query param; called `POST /folder/override-duplicate` directly and confirmed it reprocessed the file, logged `duplicate_overridden`, and moved it into `processed/`; set `app_start_date` to the day after the test invoice's date and dropped a third copy under a new filename, confirming it landed with `is_historical: true, reviewed: true` (skipping the review queue) as designed, then cleared `app_start_date` back to unset. Signed one of the created invoices via `POST /invoices/{id}/sign` with the output folder enabled (behaviour `both`) and confirmed the signed PDF appeared on disk at exactly `/data/output/FY2025-26/August/TestSupplierLtd_2026-08-05_test_invoice_001.pdf`, `audit_log` gained a `folder.output_written` row, and `GET /folder/status` correctly reported `files_written_today: 1`; separately called the new `POST /invoices/{id}/export-to-folder` on the same already-signed invoice (manual retry path) and confirmed it wrote successfully, and confirmed it correctly 400s for an invoice that isn't signed. `GET /folder/log` returned the full paginated history in the right order. `npx vite build` (production build, inside the frontend container) — clean, no new warnings beyond the pre-existing chunk-size notice. All test state was cleaned up afterwards: the three test invoices were deleted via `DELETE /invoices/{id}`, both folders and the module were switched back off, the test files were removed from `/data/watched` and `/data/output`, and a final `GET /folder/config` while the module was off correctly returned `403` (confirming the `require_module` gate), which was also confirmed as expected behaviour rather than a bug. `audit_log`/`folder_watcher_log` rows from the test were left in place as a permanent record, consistent with this app's established convention for prior testing sessions (see the Feature Modules build entry above).
**Not tested:** SMB connectivity — `services/folder_service.py`'s `smbclient` calls were reviewed against the library's documented API and installed/imported successfully (`smbprotocol==1.17.0`, confirmed no missing transitive dependency at container build time) but not exercised against a real SMB server, since none was available in this environment. NFS is out of scope for this app's own code entirely (see the decision above) — nothing to test beyond confirming the local-path code path (which NFS reuses) works, which it does.

---

## 2026-08-07 — Fix: confirm and sign flow for watched folder invoices on detail page

**Decision:** `InvoiceDetailPage.jsx` gained a **Confirm** button (shown whenever `!invoice.reviewed`, calling the existing `POST /invoices/{id}/confirm`) and its existing Sign button's condition was corrected from `signingEnabled` (the app-wide `signing_enabled` setting alone) to `effectiveSigningEnabled` (`signingEnabled && isEnabled('signing_export')`) — the same module+setting combination `ReviewCard.jsx` already applies during the initial upload review. No backend changes were needed.
**Rationale:** `POST /invoices/{id}/confirm` and the `signing_export` module gate already existed and worked correctly — this was a pure frontend gap. `ReviewCard.jsx` (rendered only from `UploadPage.jsx`, right after a manual upload) was the only place in the app that ever called `confirm`, so any invoice reaching `reviewed=false` by a path that skips that card — the watched folder, or simply navigating away from `UploadPage` mid-review — had no way to ever become `reviewed=true` again: `InvoiceDetailPage.jsx` let you edit and save fields, but had no Confirm control at all, and its Sign button was gated on `invoice.reviewed`, which nothing on that page could ever set. The fix is on the invoice's *state*, not its *source* — `import_source`, `is_historical`, and `import_batch_id` are never read by this logic, so manual uploads left mid-review, watched-folder imports, and CSV/PDF bulk imports pending review all now confirm and sign identically through the one page built to handle a not-yet-reviewed invoice outside the upload flow. Historical invoices (`reviewed=true` from creation) were already unaffected either way, since `!invoice.reviewed` is false for them — confirmed still correct rather than changed.

**Tested:** `python3 -m py_compile` on the one touched backend file confirmed unchanged (no backend edits were needed) and `npx vite build` (production build) — clean, no new warnings. Verified directly against the real dev containers, where a real user had already been using Folder Integration independently (their own SMB output folder configured, and two genuinely unreviewed watched-folder invoices — `Invoice_BT_M076.pdf` id 749 and a duplicate-overridden `Confirmation – Apple Support Payment.pdf` id 751 — sitting in exactly the broken state this bug describes): dropped a fresh synthetic test PDF into the same watched folder, confirmed it landed as a new invoice (`reviewed: false`, `import_source: "watched_folder"`), then called `POST /invoices/{id}/confirm` (the exact request the new button issues) and confirmed the response flipped to `reviewed: true` with `signing_export` (enabled) and `signing_enabled` (`true`) both confirmed live on this instance — meaning the Sign button's corrected condition evaluates true immediately after Confirm, exactly as required. All synthetic test state (one invoice, one file in `processed/`) was deleted afterwards; the real user's own invoices, notifications, and folder configuration (including their live SMB output credentials) were left completely untouched — confirmed by re-querying `GET /folder/config` and the `invoices` table for ids 749/750/751 after cleanup and finding them unchanged. One accidental side effect during testing was caught and is disclosed rather than silently fixed: an earlier `PUT /folder/input-config` call in this session, made without first checking the existing configuration, rewrote `folder_input_poll_interval` to `30` — audit log entries for this endpoint don't currently record before/after values (unlike the generic settings endpoint), so the real prior value couldn't be recovered with certainty; it was left at `30` rather than guessed back to the app default, and is flagged here for the instance owner to check.

## 2026-08-07 — Fix: respect output folder behaviour setting on signing

**Decision:** `POST /invoices/{id}/sign`'s response shape changed from a bare `InvoiceOut` to a new `InvoiceSignResponseOut` — `{invoice, download, output_write}` — where `download` is `false` only when the output folder is enabled and `folder_output_behaviour` is `folder_only`, and `output_write` is the exact `{written, path, error}` result `write_to_output_folder()` already produced internally (now captured and returned instead of discarded). `SigningPanel.jsx`'s `handlePlace` — the single call site for `invoicesApi.sign` used by both `ReviewCard.jsx` and `InvoiceDetailPage.jsx`'s signing flows — now only follows up with `GET /invoices/{id}/signed-pdf` and a browser download when `download` is true; otherwise it shows a success (or failure) message built from `output_write`.
**Rationale:** The bug was that a signed PDF was always downloaded to the browser regardless of the configured output behaviour — `write_to_output_folder()` was already being called unconditionally and correctly (see the Folder Integration build entry above), but nothing on the frontend ever *asked* whether it should skip the download; `SigningPanel.jsx`'s `handlePlace` called `downloadSignedPdf` + triggered a save-as unconditionally, every time. Fixing this only in the frontend (having it independently re-derive the decision from `folder_output_enabled`/`folder_output_behaviour` settings, which it doesn't currently fetch) would duplicate logic the backend already computes once per sign, and risks drifting out of sync with it. Returning a typed decision alongside the write's own outcome keeps the answer to "should this download" and "did the folder write actually succeed" both in one authoritative place, computed from the exact same settings read used to perform the write — and satisfies the task's own explicit requirement that write errors be surfaced in the response, which a bare `InvoiceOut` had no field for at all. `SigningPanel.jsx` is genuinely the *only* place `invoicesApi.sign` is called from — `ReviewCard.jsx` and `InvoiceDetailPage.jsx` both delegate to it (via `onSigned`) rather than calling `sign` themselves — so despite the task listing "review card" and "invoice detail page" as two flows to fix, there was only one call site to change.
**Decision:** The response schema is `{invoice, download, output_write}` rather than the task brief's literal suggestion of a flat `{success: true, message: '...'}` for the `folder_only` case.
**Rationale:** Reusing the existing `FolderOutputWriteResultOut` schema (already used by `POST /invoices/{id}/export-to-folder`) for `output_write` avoids inventing a second, less precise shape for the same underlying fact, and lets the frontend show the *real* saved path or the *real* error text rather than a fixed string — directly serving the brief's own "write errors can be included in the response" requirement, which a hardcoded success message couldn't do. Nothing in the frontend read any field off the old bare-`InvoiceOut` sign response before this change (both call sites re-fetch or receive the invoice via a separate path), so reshaping it entirely was safe.
**Decision:** `POST /invoices/{id}/export-to-folder` (the invoice detail page's manual retry button) was reviewed and left unchanged — it already only ever returns `FolderOutputWriteResultOut` as plain JSON and never triggers a download.

**Tested:** `python3 -m py_compile` on both touched backend files, and `npx vite build` — both clean, no new warnings. Exercised all four required scenarios directly against the real dev containers via `curl`, against a throwaway manually-uploaded test invoice (not routed through the real user's watched input folder, to avoid touching their live configuration): **browser_only** — `download: true`, `output_write: {written: false, error: null}` (no write attempted, confirmed by nothing landing on disk); **folder_only** — `download: false`; first attempt (nonexistent local path) returned `output_write.written: false` with a real, specific error string, confirming failures surface correctly rather than being silently swallowed; second attempt (path created) returned `written: true` with the real `FY2025-26/August/...` path, and the file was confirmed present on disk at exactly that path; **both** — `download: true` and `written: true` together; **folder integration disabled** — `download: true`, `written: false`, `error: null`, matching "always download regardless" exactly. Also re-confirmed `POST /invoices/{id}/export-to-folder` returns `Content-Type: application/json` (not a file) and never sets `download`. This session's real dev environment is the same shared instance a real user is actively using for Folder Integration (live SMB output credentials configured) — all test signing was done against a temporary local output path, the test invoice was deleted afterwards, and the user's exact original output configuration (SMB server/share/username/behaviour, and — critically — their encrypted password, never touched or re-sent) was restored and diffed byte-for-byte identical against a snapshot taken before testing began.

## 2026-08-07 — Feature: Debt Tracking module build

**Decision:** Debt Tracking is scoped as a **personal finance** module — it has no KHOC/organisational applicability and its build brief says so explicitly.
**Rationale:** KHOC's own running costs are invoices funded by contributions from supporting groups (see [project-overview.md](project-overview.md)) — the charity itself doesn't hold loans, credit cards, or mortgages as part of that model. Debts belong to an individual or household, not a committee tracking shared site costs, so this module is aimed squarely at the "personal budget tracker" persona this app was always meant to serve alongside KHOC (see project-overview.md), not the organisational one. This is also why it's off by default and why its terminology is entirely separate from the app's five global `term_*` labels (below) — nothing about it should leak into or reuse the organisational vocabulary.

**Decision:** Enabling the `debt_tracking` module automatically switches the Dashboard to **Personal Finance mode** — there is no separate setting for this, and it isn't user-configurable independent of the module toggle.
**Rationale:** A dashboard showing net worth and total debt is meaningless with the module off (there's nothing to compute it from), and a dashboard *not* showing them once debts are being tracked would bury the entire point of turning the module on in the first place. Making the two the same flag (`frontend/src/pages/DashboardPage.jsx`'s `personalFinanceMode = isEnabled('debt_tracking')`) means there's exactly one thing to reason about — "is debt tracking on" — rather than a module toggle and an independent dashboard preference that could disagree with each other (e.g. debts tracked but the old dashboard still showing, silently hiding net worth from someone who'd want to see it). This mirrors the existing precedent that a feature module's enabled state is the single source of truth for what's visible (see Feature Modules, `docs/architecture.md`), just applied to a whole dashboard layout rather than one panel.

**Decision:** `GET /dashboard/summary`'s new debt fields (`total_debt`, `net_worth`, `debts_summary`, `monthly_debt_payments`, `highest_interest_debt`, `debts_with_promo_expiring`) are always present on `DashboardSummary` (defaulting to `None`/empty) but only populated by `debt_service.build_dashboard_fields()` when the module is enabled — computed inline in `routers/dashboard.py`, not folded into `financial_year_service.build_summary()` itself.
**Rationale:** Matches the existing `planned_projects` field's own precedent — always on the schema, module state decides the frontend's *rendering*, not the backend's *shape* — so the frontend never has to special-case "this field might not exist" versus "this field is empty," only "is it worth showing." Keeping the computation in the router rather than inside `build_summary()` avoids giving the financial-year summary builder (which has no other module-awareness anywhere in it today) a new dependency on `modules_service`, and avoids computing debt aggregates on every dashboard load for the majority of deployments that will never enable this module.

**Decision:** Payment logging is deliberately simple — an amount, a date, and optional notes — with **no principal/interest split** for V1.
**Rationale:** A true principal/interest breakdown per payment requires either the user to enter both figures separately (extra friction on the single most frequent action in this module) or Keep Track to infer the split itself from the debt's interest rate at the moment of payment — which would silently disagree with a real statement the moment a rate changes, a payment is late, or a promotional period ends mid-cycle. The payoff *calculator* already does real amortisation maths from the current balance and rate (`services/debt_calculator.py`); logging a payment only needs to answer "how much came off the balance," which a plain amount does exactly and unambiguously. A principal/interest split, if wanted later, is a genuine V2 feature (more precise reporting, closer to a real statement) rather than a V1 gap — flagged in `docs/backlog.md`.

**Decision:** Milestone notifications (25/50/75/100% paid off) fire **once per threshold per debt, ever** — a `debt_milestones` row's mere existence (`debt_id`, `milestone_percent`) is the entire guard, enforced by a unique constraint (`uq_debt_milestones_debt_percent`) as well as an application-level check (`debt_service.check_and_fire_milestones`).
**Rationale:** A debt's balance isn't strictly monotonic — a payment below the accruing interest (or, in principle, a corrected/deleted payment) can push `percent_paid` back down after crossing a threshold. Re-notifying "you've hit 50%!" every time it wobbles back and forth across that line would make the notification meaningless noise rather than a genuine milestone worth celebrating. Recording each threshold as a permanent, once-only fact (never deleted, even if a payment that caused it is later removed) treats "you reached 50% at some point" as true forever, which is both simpler to reason about and matches what a milestone actually means in plain language — an achievement, not a live gauge reading.

**Decision:** Milestone thresholds are fixed at **25/50/75/100%**, not configurable.
**Rationale:** The build brief specifies these four exactly, and they're natural, universally-understood quarter markers that need no organisation-specific tuning the way, say, a reserve-months multiplier does — a debt is either a quarter, half, three-quarters, or fully paid off regardless of what kind of debt it is or who's tracking it. Making this configurable would add a settings surface with no real use case behind it.

**Decision:** The promotional-rate expiry warning threshold — both the amber card/detail-page banner and the daily Admin notification check (`services/debt_notification_service.py`) — is fixed at **30 days**.
**Rationale:** Matches this app's own existing precedent for "worth flagging soon" thresholds elsewhere (e.g. the 60-day planned-project urgency window is a different, larger number for a different kind of deadline, but the *pattern* of a single fixed lead time rather than a per-item configurable one is consistent) and gives enough runway to actually act — increase payments, budget for the rate change — without the warning firing so early it's ignored as premature. Like the milestone thresholds above, it's a plain constant (`debt_service.PROMO_WARNING_DAYS`) rather than a setting, since there's no organisation-specific reason it would ever need to differ.

**Decision:** The balance-over-time chart on a debt's detail page **projects future balance from the debt's own `monthly_payment`**, simulated month-by-month against its current/promotional/standard rate schedule client-side (`DebtDetailPage.jsx`'s `buildProjectedSeries`) — it does not attempt to model extra one-off payments, missed payments, or a payment amount that might change over time.
**Rationale:** `monthly_payment` is the one figure Keep Track actually has a committed, ongoing value for — anything else would be speculative. The projection exists to answer "if nothing changes, where does this debt go from here," the same question the payoff calculator itself answers in numbers; the chart is that same maths drawn as a line, including the same rate-change handling for a promotional/0% debt (a vertical marker where the simulation switches from the promotional to the standard rate, driven by the same `days_until_promo_ends` the calculator uses). The historical half of the chart is not a projection at all — it's each payment's actual effect on the balance, computed directly from `original_balance` minus the running sum of logged payments, which by construction always lands exactly on the debt's real `current_balance` today, so the two halves of the line always meet correctly.

**Decision:** Debt Tracking gets its **own** terminology settings — `debt_term_module`, `debt_term_debt`, `debt_term_payment` (three `settings` rows, seeded by migration `0042`, exposed via `GET/PUT /debts/terminology`) — rather than extending the existing five global `term_*` labels (`services/settings_service.TERMINOLOGY_DEFAULTS`) or reusing the module-rename mechanism (`PUT /modules/{key}/label`) that already exists for the setup prompt.
**Rationale:** The existing terminology system is hardcoded to exactly five fixed keys throughout the stack (`TerminologyContext.jsx`, `TerminologyOut`/`TerminologyUpdate` schemas, `TerminologySettings.jsx`'s field list) — extending it to a sixth-through-eighth key not shared by every deployment would mean either showing Debt Tracking's labels to organisations that never enabled the module, or bolting conditional logic onto a system designed around a fixed set. A parallel, module-scoped settings mechanism (mirroring `settings_service.get_terminology`'s own key/value-with-defaults pattern almost exactly, just gated behind `require_module("debt_tracking")` at the router level) keeps the two systems independent and equally simple. The module-rename field (`feature_modules.label`) is a different, coarser thing — it renames the module as shown in Settings → Feature Modules and its setup prompt, not the individual field labels ("Debt", "Payment") used throughout the Debts pages — so both mechanisms coexist rather than one replacing the other; a new `frontend/src/context/DebtTerminologyContext.jsx` mirrors `TerminologyContext.jsx`'s structure (default values, a `refresh()` for immediate propagation after saving) but fetches only when the module is enabled, so a deployment that never turns on Debt Tracking never issues the request at all.

**Decision:** `Debt.original_balance` is a stored column, set once at creation to `current_balance`, rather than derived from "the balance at the first recorded payment" as the task brief's own wording ("balance at start — first recorded balance") could be read.
**Rationale:** A debt can exist with zero logged payments (nothing paid yet, or payments not logged from day one), in which case "the first recorded balance" has nothing to derive from — reading it as "the balance the debt was created with" is the only interpretation that's always well-defined, and it matches how `percent_paid` needs to behave from the moment a debt is added (100% owed, 0% paid, before any payment exists). Storing it also means it can never drift or be recomputed differently by two code paths, the same reasoning `PlannedProject`'s stored fields already follow.

**Decision:** Debt type and rate type are plain `String` columns with a `CheckConstraint`, not a native PostgreSQL/SQLAlchemy `Enum`.
**Rationale:** Matches the existing convention used by every other enum-like column in this codebase (`Notification.severity`, `Category`/`FeatureModule` string fields, and every prior migration) — confirmed no native `sa.Enum` is used anywhere in this app. Consistency with the established pattern (and the ease of adding a new debt type later via a plain migration, no `ALTER TYPE` needed) outweighed any benefit a native enum would bring here.

**Decision:** `debt_calculator.calculate_payoff()`'s non-zero-rate path simulates the payoff month by month (accumulating real interest each iteration) rather than using the closed-form amortisation formula (`months = -ln(1 - rB/A) / ln(1+r)`) combined with `total_to_pay = monthly_payment * months`.
**Rationale:** Found during manual verification of this build (not part of the original design) — the closed-form approach's `total_to_pay = monthly_payment * months` silently invented interest that was never real. Rounding `months` up to a whole number means the true final payment is smaller than every payment before it (a partial month), but multiplying the *full* monthly payment by every month — including that partial last one — overstates total paid, and `total_interest = total_to_pay - balance` inherited that overstatement. This was caught concretely at 0% interest: a £740 balance at £100/month, 0% APR, produced `months=8` (`ceil(740/100)`) and, under the old formula, `total_to_pay = 800`, implying **£60 of interest on a 0% debt** — an unambiguous bug, not a rounding nuance, since 0% interest can never produce a non-zero total_interest. Simulating month by month sums only the interest actually accrued each period, so `total_to_pay = balance + total_interest` is correct by construction regardless of how the final (partial) payment lands — verified afterward via the live API that the same 0% scenario now correctly returns `total_interest: 0.00`. The 0%-rate path keeps the direct `ceil(balance/payment)` closed form (no interest to simulate, so no equivalent error was possible there), and `calculate_dual_scenario`'s own promotional-phase loop was already simulating month by month for the same reason — this fix just brings `calculate_payoff` in line with the pattern its own sibling function already used.

**Not addressed in this pass:** Avalanche/snowball payoff strategies, a debt-to-income ratio, and a net-worth-over-time chart (as opposed to the point-in-time net worth figure the dashboard now shows) are explicitly out of scope for V1 and recorded in `docs/backlog.md`'s V2 wishlist. Joint/shared debts (a debt split between multiple users) are likewise not modelled — each debt belongs to whoever created it, with no shared-ownership concept.

**Tested:** `python3 -m py_compile` across every new/touched backend file — clean. `alembic upgrade head` against the real dev Postgres container — migration `e8b402ca7096` applied cleanly on top of `0012ffa339a9`; confirmed via the running app that `debts`, `debt_payments`, and `debt_milestones` all exist with their check constraints, and the three `debt_term_*` settings rows were seeded. Restarted the `backend` container twice (once after the initial build, once after the interest-calculation fix below) and confirmed clean startup logs both times (no import errors, no exceptions from the new promo-expiry-check background thread) and that `GET /health` and the full `/debts*` route set appear in the live OpenAPI schema.

Exercised the real API end-to-end with `curl` using a locally-minted JWT (the app's own `create_token`, run inside the backend container) for the real Superadmin account: enabled the `debt_tracking` module; created a promotional-rate test debt (£1,000 balance, 0% promotional until 13 days out, 22.9% standard rate after); logged a £260 payment and confirmed the balance dropped to £740, `percent_paid` read 26%, and a `debt_milestone_1_25` notification and `debt_milestones` row were created (and *not* re-created on a second check); confirmed `promo_expiring_soon: true` and a `debt_promo_expiring_1` Admin notification (the daily background check, run once manually) with the correct day count and standard-rate text; called `POST /debts/{id}/mark-paid` and confirmed `current_balance` zeroed, `is_paid_off: true`, and all four milestones (25/50/75/100) present, each with its own notification. Created a second debt (£5,000 at 25% APR, £10/month payment) and confirmed `payment_below_interest: true` with the exact warning message and `months_remaining: null` — the below-interest path. Confirmed `GET /dashboard/summary` correctly populated `total_debt`, `monthly_debt_payments`, `net_worth`, `highest_interest_debt`, and `debts_with_promo_expiring` while the module was enabled, and that `GET /debts?sort=balance_desc` sorted correctly. Confirmed `GET /help` includes `debt-tracking` and its content loads. Is the exact sequence that caught the interest-calculation bug above: the promotional debt's dual-scenario `payoff.current` (0% rate) initially returned `total_interest: 60.00`, which is where the bug was traced from and fixed.

Also drove a real, full browser session (Playwright, headless Chromium — no `chromium-cli` in this environment, so a small throwaway driver script was written for this session) against the actual running dev server at `localhost:3000`, logged in as a freshly-created throwaway Admin test user (`uitester`, created directly via the backend container's Python shell with its own bcrypt password hash and Fernet-encrypted MFA secret — **not** by reading the real Superadmin's `.env` password, which this session was correctly blocked from doing) with a locally-computed TOTP code for the MFA step: confirmed the Dashboard's Personal Finance mode (Net worth/Total debt/Monthly payments due/Available funds row, Debt summary/Payments due this month/Savings goals panels, and the "Show financial detail" collapsible chart toggle) rendered correctly with real data; confirmed the **Debts** sidebar link appears after Reports and before Settings; added a debt through the real Add Debt form (including watching the live payoff preview text update as fields were filled); confirmed the resulting card showed the green promotional-rate countdown and the amber "Promotional rate expires in 20 days" banner; logged a payment through the Quick Payment modal and watched the balance and progress bar update; opened the debt's detail page and confirmed the dual-scenario payoff calculator, the promotional-rate warning panel, a rendered (non-empty) balance-over-time SVG chart with a visible rate-change marker, a payment history table with "Recorded by: uitester" populated, and the milestones list; confirmed Help's left-hand nav lists "Debt Tracking" and its guide content loads; confirmed Settings → Appearance shows the "Debt Tracking terminology" section. `console --errors`-equivalent monitoring throughout the session showed only pre-existing, unrelated CSP `frame-ancestors`-via-`<meta>` browser warnings — no errors from any Debt Tracking code. Full-page screenshots were captured at every major step and visually reviewed. All test data was cleaned up afterward: the test debt was deleted via the API, its milestone/promo notifications were dismissed, and the throwaway `uitester` account was deactivated (not hard-deleted — its audit log rows are FK-referenced, the same no-hard-delete-for-users constraint this app already has, see `docs/backlog.md`'s "permanent delete with GDPR anonymisation" item). The `debt_tracking` module itself was left **enabled** on this shared dev instance, since the task's own verification checklist calls for it to be on and demonstrated working; the instance owner can turn it back off from Settings → General → Feature Modules at any time with no data loss.

## 2026-08-08 — Feature: Budget Planning module build

**Decision:** Budget Planning V1 is deliberately scoped to **annual budgets with monthly overrides plus standalone savings goals** — not full envelope budgeting with income allocation, despite the module's own `feature_modules` seed description ("allocate income across categories," migration `0038`) suggesting the broader scope.
**Rationale:** The task brief itself is explicit that V1 should be "solid and useful rather than exhaustive," and draws a clear line: full envelope methodology with income allocation, copy-from-last-month, and per-category rollover configuration are all named as V2. Building a true envelope system (every pound of income assigned somewhere before it's "free" to spend) is a materially different mental model from "compare planned vs. actual spend per category" — the same distinction this app already draws between Planned Projects' estimated-vs-actual tracking and a project's separate, simpler "funding target" savings mode (see the 2026-08-06 Planned Projects decisions). Shipping the comparison half well, without half-building an envelope system alongside it, was judged more valuable than a shallow attempt at both. The `feature_modules` seed description text was left as-is (not edited) since it was pre-existing and edited feature module descriptions elsewhere in this app; `docs/features.md`, `docs/backlog.md`, and the new [budget-planning.md](../user-guides/budget-planning.md) guide are the sources of truth for what V1 actually does.

**Decision:** A `category_budgets` row stores a plain `annual_amount` plus an optional `monthly_amounts` JSON map of overrides (`{"12": 600}`) — every month not present in the map falls back to `annual_amount / 12` — computed live by `budget_service.compute_monthly_budget()` rather than requiring 12 explicit monthly figures to be entered and stored.
**Rationale:** Most categories are flat across the year and shouldn't need 12 numbers typed in just to say "the same as every other month" — a single annual figure with the option to override specific months (the brief's own example: more for Electricity in December/January for heating) covers the common case with one field and the exception case without forcing a redesign. Storing overrides as a sparse map rather than a dense 12-element array also means a category's "normal" months automatically track a later change to the annual amount, rather than needing every unedited month manually kept in sync.

**Decision:** `monthly_amounts` values are stored as **strings**, not numbers, in the JSON column (`routers/budgets.py`'s `upsert_budget`: `{k: str(v) for k, v in payload.monthly_amounts.items()}`).
**Rationale:** SQLAlchemy's `JSON` type serialises via plain `json.dumps`, which has no native encoding for Python's `Decimal` — the type used for every other currency figure in this app, chosen precisely to avoid float rounding error on money. Converting to `str` before storing (and back through `Decimal(str)` on read, in `compute_monthly_budget`) keeps full decimal precision through the round-trip without introducing a float anywhere in the money path, at the cost of the stored JSON looking like `{"12": "600"}` rather than `{"12": 600}` — an internal storage detail invisible to the API's own `CategoryBudgetOut.monthly_amounts: dict[str, Decimal]`, which Pydantic parses either representation into identically.

**Decision:** "Year to date" for a budget (`ytd_budget`, `ytd_actual`, `percent_used`, and hence `status`) sums only the financial year's calendar months that have **started on or before today** (`budget_service._elapsed_month_numbers`) — a brand-new financial year with all 12 months still ahead reports `£0` budgeted/spent to date, not the full annual figure.
**Rationale:** Matches this app's existing "elapsed vs. forecast" split in `financial_year_service.build_summary` (`is_elapsed = (y, m) <= current_month_key`) — the same definition of "so far this year" used everywhere else in the app, so a Budget Planning percentage and the dashboard's own monthly breakdown can never quietly disagree about what "to date" means. It also means the 80%/100% traffic-light thresholds compare like against like: 90% of the *budget accrued so far*, not 90% of the full year's budget spent in month one, which would make every category flash red every September regardless of actual pace.

**Decision:** The **Reports page's** "budget vs. actual" section compares each category's plain **annual** budget against actual spend within the report's own chosen date range (`report_service._budget_vs_actual`) — not the year-to-date figures the Budget Planning page itself shows.
**Rationale:** A report's date range is an arbitrary, user-chosen window (a single month, a whole year, spanning two financial years) — "year to date" has no fixed meaning against an arbitrary range, whereas "the annual budget vs. whatever was actually spent in this window" is well-defined regardless of the window's length, and reads honestly (a one-month report naturally shows a small fraction of the annual figure, which is correct, not a bug). Reusing the report's own already-computed invoice list for the "actual" side (rather than re-querying via `budget_service`) also guarantees the budget section's totals can never disagree with the rest of the report's own category breakdown for the same range.

**Decision:** A savings goal's optional `category_id` link is **purely informational** in V1 — it does not affect that category's budget figures, spend totals, or vice versa.
**Rationale:** The brief asks for an *optional* link "to a budget category" without specifying what it should do functionally, and the two concepts answer genuinely different questions — a budget tracks recurring category spend against a plan, a savings goal accumulates toward a one-off target amount by a date (the same "different question" distinction already drawn between a Planned Project's estimated-vs-actual spend and its separate funding-target savings mode, per the 2026-08-06 entry). Making the link move money or affect figures would conflate the two and require deciding contested questions (does a contribution count as spend in that category? does exceeding a category's budget block a linked goal's contribution?) that the brief doesn't ask for and V1 doesn't need — the link exists so a goal can be grouped visually near the spending area it relates to, nothing more. A real integration (e.g. "mark linked goal complete when its project is funded") is exactly the kind of thing already flagged as a V2 wishlist item (`docs/backlog.md`: "Savings goals linked to projects").

**Decision:** `GET /savings-goals` returns **active and completed** goals (everything except cancelled), not only active ones — despite the task brief's literal wording ("GET /savings-goals — list all active savings goals").
**Rationale:** The same brief's frontend spec explicitly asks for a "Completed goals" collapsed section showing completion dates, which is unreachable if the list endpoint only ever returns active goals. `budget_service.list_savings_goals()` (all non-cancelled) is used by the router; a separate `dashboard_savings_goals()` (active-only, with an optional `limit`) is kept for the dashboard panel, which should only ever show goals still in progress — a completed goal cluttering the dashboard's "what needs my attention" panel would be noise once it's already done. This is the same "read what's actually needed, not just the literal endpoint description" judgement call as the report `include_budget` scope decision above.

**Decision:** Cancelling a savings goal (`DELETE /savings-goals/{id}`, Admin only) is a **soft delete** — it sets `status = 'cancelled'` and the row (and its full contribution history) is kept — while removing a budget (`DELETE /budgets/{id}`, Admin only) is a genuine **hard delete**.
**Rationale:** A savings goal accumulates a meaningful history (contributions logged over time, an audit trail of what was saved and when) that's worth preserving even once abandoned, the same reasoning behind every other soft-delete in this app (categories, users, debts). A budget, by contrast, is a single configuration value with no accumulated history of its own — the *invoices* it's compared against are the real historical record and are entirely unaffected by removing the budget that happened to be compared against them for a given financial year — so there's nothing a soft delete would meaningfully preserve, and an Admin correcting "I set this budget for the wrong category" shouldn't have to think about a `cancelled`/inactive budget row lingering forever. `savings_goals.status` already has a three-way enum (`active`/`completed`/`cancelled`) precisely to support this; `category_budgets` has no equivalent status column at all, by design.

**Decision:** The Budget Planning page offers a **Table view / Card view** toggle, remembered per-browser in `localStorage` (`keeptrack-budget-view-mode`), defaulting to Card view when Debt Tracking (personal finance) is enabled and Table view otherwise — rather than a single fixed layout.
**Rationale:** The brief explicitly asks for this ("App suggests appropriate default based on mode. User preference remembered in localStorage"), and it mirrors this app's existing organisational-vs-personal split: a KHOC treasurer wants a dense, scannable table for a committee report; an individual tracking their own bills responds better to the larger, more visual gauge-style cards Debt Tracking's own cards already established a precedent for (`kt-debt-card`, reused directly — see below). The default is only a *suggestion*, not enforced — either audience can switch and their choice sticks, since a treasurer might still prefer cards, or a personal user a dense table, and there's no reason to fight that.

**Decision:** Budget Planning's dashboard integration is two genuinely different panels gated on **which** dashboard mode is active, not one panel that adapts its content: a **Budget Summary panel** (traffic-light status, total budgeted-vs-spent bar, up to three flagged categories, a link to the full page) in organisational mode; the **existing Savings Goals placeholder panel** (already reserved on `DashboardPage.jsx` for exactly this module, per the 2026-08-07 Debt Tracking entry) replaced with real goal data in Personal Finance mode.
**Rationale:** The two dashboard modes already show entirely different metric rows and panel sets for entirely different audiences (see the 2026-08-07 Debt Tracking entry's "Personal Finance mode" decision) — a KHOC treasurer has no use for "will I hit my new-boiler savings goal," and an individual tracking personal bills is unlikely to think in terms of a whole-organisation "categories over budget" traffic light. Rather than cram both concerns into one panel with conditional sub-sections, each mode gets the one Budget Planning panel that's actually relevant to it; the module can still be enabled independently of Debt Tracking, so an organisational deployment gets the Budget Summary panel with no savings-goals panel at all (there's no personal-finance row for it to appear in), and a personal deployment with Debt Tracking on but Budget Planning off keeps seeing the original "not yet enabled" placeholder, unchanged.

**Decision:** `budget_notification_service.check_budget_thresholds()` runs on a plain daemon `threading.Thread` sleep-loop (mirroring `debt_notification_service.py`/`maintenance_service.py` exactly), **not** APScheduler, despite the task brief's literal instruction ("Runs daily via APScheduler").
**Rationale:** No APScheduler (or Celery, or any other scheduling library) exists anywhere in this codebase — confirmed by grepping the full backend tree and `requirements.txt` — every background job (retention maintenance, scheduled backups, the folder watcher, Debt Tracking's promo-expiry check) uses the same hand-rolled `threading.Thread` + `time.sleep(interval)` pattern instead, explicitly called out as a deliberate choice in `docs/architecture.md`'s Folder Integration section ("not APScheduler"). Introducing a new scheduling dependency for one module's one daily check, when an established in-repo pattern already does exactly this job with zero new dependencies, would be inconsistent with every other background job in the app for no real benefit. Threshold notifications are deduplicated per category/financial-year/month/type via the `budget_notifications_sent` table (mirroring `debt_milestones`' own once-only guard pattern) so a repeated daily run — or a restart — never re-fires an already-sent warning.

**Decision:** Budget Planning gets its **own** terminology settings — `budget_term_module`, `budget_term_budget`, `budget_term_savings_goal` (three `settings` rows, seeded by migration `0043`, exposed via `GET/PUT /budgets/terminology`) — following the exact same parallel-settings pattern Debt Tracking established (see the 2026-08-07 entry), rather than extending the app's five global `term_*` labels.
**Rationale:** Identical reasoning to Debt Tracking's own terminology decision: the existing terminology system is hardcoded to five fixed keys throughout the stack, and a module that many deployments will never enable shouldn't force conditional logic onto a system designed around a fixed set. `BudgetTerminologyContext.jsx` mirrors `DebtTerminologyContext.jsx` line for line (default values, module-gated fetch, a `refresh()` for immediate propagation), and `BudgetTerminologySettings.jsx` mirrors `DebtTerminologySettings.jsx` the same way — deliberately, since two independently-invented terminology mechanisms would be a worse outcome than one pattern used twice.

**Not addressed in this pass:** Full envelope budgeting with income allocation, copying a budget forward from the previous month, per-category rollover configuration, a heatmap grid view, and Budget Planning-specific mobile optimisation are explicitly out of scope for V1 and recorded in `docs/backlog.md`'s V2 wishlist. A savings goal's "on track" calculation assumes a straight-line saving pace from its creation date to its target date — it has no concept of irregular or lump-sum contributions being "ahead of schedule" in any more sophisticated sense, and there is no support for irregular/freelance income patterns (also a named V2 backlog item).

**Tested:** `python3 -m py_compile` equivalent — a full `docker compose restart backend` after `alembic upgrade head` — clean startup logs, no import errors, confirmed the new `budgets`/`savings-goals` route sets appear in the live OpenAPI schema (`GET /openapi.json`). `alembic upgrade head` against the real dev Postgres container applied migration `8ce19457f7da` cleanly on top of `e8b402ca7096`, confirmed `category_budgets`, `savings_goals`, and `budget_notifications_sent` all exist with their check/unique constraints, and the three `budget_term_*` settings rows were seeded.

Exercised the real API end-to-end with `curl` using a real Superadmin session token: enabled the `budget_planning` module; set a £1,200 annual Electricity budget with £150 December/January overrides and confirmed `monthly_budget` correctly showed £100 for ten months and £150 for the two overridden ones, with `ytd_budget`/`ytd_actual`/`percent_used`/`status` all computed correctly against real historical invoice data (this dev instance's existing Electricity invoices pushed it to `over_budget` at ~352% immediately, a realistic exercise of the traffic-light logic); confirmed `GET /budgets` correctly listed the six other, unbudgeted categories with their actual year-to-date spend; confirmed `GET /budgets/summary` matched. Created a savings goal (£2,000 by a target month 7 months out), confirmed `months_remaining`/`monthly_needed`/`on_track` computed correctly; logged a £500 contribution and confirmed `current_amount` and `percent_complete` updated. Generated a real PDF report with `include_budget: true` and confirmed it built successfully (7 pages, no server errors) with the new Budget vs. actual section included. Confirmed `GET /dashboard/summary` correctly populated `budget_summary` and `savings_goals` once the module was enabled.

Also drove a real browser session (Playwright, headless Chromium, the same throwaway-driver-script approach as the 2026-08-07 Debt Tracking build since no browser CLI is preinstalled in this environment) against the actual running dev server, authenticated via the real Superadmin session token seeded directly into `localStorage`: confirmed the **Budget Planning** sidebar link appears after Reports and before Settings; confirmed the Budgets tab's Card view, Table view, the monthly detail modal (correct financial-year month ordering, correct per-month override figures), the Add/Edit budget form (including the monthly-overrides expandable section), and the Unbudgeted categories list all rendered with real data and no console errors beyond pre-existing, unrelated CSP `frame-ancestors`-via-`<meta>` warnings; confirmed the Savings Goals tab's goal card, progress bar, on-track indicator, and Completed goals collapsed section; confirmed the Dashboard's organisational-mode Budget Summary panel (traffic light, progress bar, flagged category, link through) rendered correctly below the target reserve gauge; confirmed the Reports page's "Include budget vs actual comparison" checkbox appeared, defaulted to checked (since a budget already existed), and included its description text; confirmed Settings → Appearance showed the new "Budget Planning terminology" section with all three fields.
