One real UI bug was caught and fixed during this pass: the monthly detail modal's default `420px` modal width truncated the month table's Status column — fixed by adding an optional `className` prop to the shared `Modal` component and a `kt-budget-month-modal { max-width: 640px }` override, re-verified with a follow-up screenshot. Full-page screenshots were captured and visually reviewed at every major step. The test budget, savings goal, and its contribution were deliberately **left in place** (not cleaned up) on this shared dev instance, and the `budget_planning` module was left **enabled** — the task's own verification checklist explicitly calls for exactly this data to exist and be demonstrated working; the instance owner can remove the test data or turn the module back off from Settings → General → Feature Modules at any time with no other data affected.

## 2026-08-08 — Polish: layout and spacing fixes, pass 1

**Decision:** The Folder Integration input/output cards were restructured to three visually-divided rows inside the existing `kt-settings-list` container (a header row with the toggle, a body row with the fields and a left-aligned Save button, and a full-width footer row containing only the Test connection button) rather than inventing new card-level CSS.
**Rationale:** `kt-settings-list`'s own `gap: 1px` + border-colour background already renders a subtle hairline between stacked `kt-settings-row` children — exactly the "clear section header with a subtle divider below" the task asked for — for free, and reusing it keeps the input and output cards byte-for-byte identical in structure (same classes, same row order), which is what "identical spacing, padding, and visual structure" actually requires. Test connection was pulled out of the row it previously shared with Save into its own full-width footer row so it's consistently positioned at the bottom of both cards, per the brief.

**Decision:** The Folder Integration "Folder status" panel was renamed **Folder Activity**, wrapped in its own bordered card (`kt-folder-activity-card`) separate from the two connection cards above it, and its recent-activity table got a `kt-compact-table` modifier (smaller padding/font) plus a first-column max-width + ellipsis truncation (with a `title` attribute for the full filename) rather than changing the shared `kt-users-table` this table borrows its base styling from.
**Rationale:** `kt-users-table` is reused by the Users settings table and needed to stay untouched. A long watched-folder filename (these are real uploaded invoice filenames, often 40+ characters) was pushing the Status/Time columns off the visible width entirely on a narrow viewport rather than just wrapping — truncating it with a `title` tooltip keeps the log genuinely compact without hiding data a user can't get to another way (the "View full log" modal still shows it in full).

**Decision:** The dashboard panel-to-panel gap was unified to **20px** everywhere — `.kt-dashboard-panel`'s `margin-bottom` was raised from 14px to 20px, and `.kt-panels-row` (which previously had no `margin-bottom` at all) now also carries `margin-bottom: 20px`, matching its own internal grid `gap`.
**Rationale:** Found while reviewing panel spacing per the task brief: `.kt-panels-row` had zero bottom margin, so on the Personal Finance dashboard the Debt/Payments/Savings-goals row sat flush against the "Financial year at a glance" panel below it with no gap at all, while every other panel-to-panel transition on the page used the 14px `margin-bottom` from `.kt-dashboard-panel`. Raising both to a single shared 20px (matching the grid's own inter-column gap, so a row of panels and a stack of single panels read as the same rhythm) fixes the zero-gap case without introducing a second spacing constant. Panel padding, border-radius, and title typography were already identical across every panel via the shared `.kt-dashboard-panel`/`.kt-panel-title` classes — no other divergence was found. The intentional non-stretched (`align-items: start`) row height for `.kt-panels-row`, documented in an existing comment in `dashboard.css`, was left as-is: it exists specifically so a taller Planned Projects card (funding-target progress bar) doesn't force Upcoming Invoices/Recent Activity to match its height, which is a deliberate prior decision, not an inconsistency to "fix."

**Decision:** The invoice table's columns were given explicit widths via `<col>`/class pairs (`kt-invoices-col-*`) and `table-layout: fixed`, scoped to a new `kt-invoices-list-table` modifier class rather than applying `table-layout: fixed` to the shared `kt-invoices-table` class directly. The Signed and Status columns were also reordered to appear in that sequence (previously Status came before Signed), matching the task brief's explicit column ordering.
**Rationale:** `kt-invoices-table` is reused as-is by seven other tables across the app (Contributions, Reconciliation, Projects, Debts, Search results, Forecast breakdown, Reports, Import history) with entirely different column shapes — forcing `table-layout: fixed` on the shared class would have broken all of them, since fixed layout requires every column to have a defined width or it falls back to equal-share distribution. Scoping it to a modifier class used only by `InvoicesPage.jsx` keeps every other table's auto layout untouched.

**Decision:** On mobile (≤720px, this app's established breakpoint), the invoice table's fixed columns (Date/Amount/Category) shrink further and cell padding tightens, rather than only hiding the Signed/Status columns and leaving the desktop widths in place.
**Rationale:** Caught by an actual mobile-viewport screenshot during verification: hiding Signed/Status alone still left the desktop fixed widths (Date 130px + Amount 110px + Category 170px = 410px) consuming almost the entire 480px viewport, crushing the flexible Supplier column down to ~2 visible characters. Shrinking the fixed columns and reducing padding/font-size on mobile leaves Supplier a usable ~150px+ while keeping every other column's content legible.

**Decision:** The header's icon-to-wordmark gap (`.kt-brand-logo-group`) was reduced from 8px to 5px; the wordmark-to-instance-name divider spacing (`.kt-brand-instance`) was left unchanged after review.
**Rationale:** The task brief only flagged the icon/wordmark pairing as feeling disconnected; the divider spacing was reviewed in both a full-page and a cropped screenshot at desktop and mobile widths and already read as comfortable rather than excessive, so it wasn't a real defect to fix. The logo unit's click-to-dashboard behaviour was unchanged (still a single `<Link to="/">` wrapping both the icon and wordmark) and was re-verified end-to-end with a scripted click from a non-dashboard page.

**Not addressed in this pass:** The invoice table brief's own column list also named an "Actions" column with per-row buttons — no such column exists in the current table (rows navigate to the invoice detail page on click instead), so nothing was added for it; this is a discrepancy between the task brief's assumption and the app's actual current design, not a gap introduced by this pass.

**Tested:** `vite build` after every change (clean, no errors). Verified all four fixes with a real, driven browser session (Playwright, headless Chromium, the same throwaway-account-and-driver-script approach used in prior sessions per this log — a deactivated-not-deleted `uitester2` Admin account, since this app has no hard delete for users once they have audit log rows) against the actual running dev server (`docker compose`), in both light and dark mode and at a 480px mobile viewport, across the Dashboard, Settings → Data → Folder Integration, and Invoices pages. Two real bugs were caught and fixed during this verification, both described above: the invoice Date column truncating dates at its initial 100px/92px widths, and the mobile invoice table crushing the Supplier column. Confirmed no new browser console errors (only pre-existing, unrelated CSP `frame-ancestors`-via-`<meta>` warnings and an unrelated 403 on the throwaway test account's un-set avatar). Full-page and cropped screenshots were visually reviewed at each step. The throwaway `uitester2` account was deactivated (not deleted) afterward.

## 2026-08-08 — Polish: report footer and welcome page copy improvements

**Decision:** The PDF report footer (`services/pdf_report_service.py`) was rebuilt around a custom `_NumberedCanvas` (subclassing ReportLab's `Canvas`, buffering each page via the standard "two-pass" `showPage`/`save` recipe) rather than the previous `onFirstPage`/`onLaterPages` callback pair, so it could show a real "Page X of Y" — the total page count isn't knowable until the whole document has been laid out, which a per-page callback alone can't provide.
**Rationale:** The previous footer was `canvas.drawString(..., "Keep Track")` — hardcoded, with no `site_name`, and `f"Page {doc.page}"` with no total. It was also applied via `onFirstPage=_footer`, so the cover page got a footer too, when the brief (and the cover's own distinct branding) calls for no footer there. All three were real, verifiable bugs, not just missing polish.

**Decision:** The footer's page numbering (X and Y) counts and labels only the footer-bearing pages — the cover (always exactly page 1, given this document's fixed story structure) is excluded from both the count and the numbering, so the first page after the cover reads "Page 1 of N", not "Page 2 of N+1".
**Rationale:** An unnumbered title/cover page is the standard convention this kind of report already half-implements (the cover's distinct branding, no footer). Counting it in the total while never displaying its number would leave a reader wondering why the first visible page number is "2" — starting the visible sequence at "1" for the first page that actually shows a number reads as correct rather than off-by-one.

**Decision:** The footer's identity text is `f"{site_name} — Keep Track"` when a custom instance name is set, or plain `"Keep Track"` otherwise — reusing the exact same `site_name != "Keep Track"` check already used by `Header.jsx`'s `showSiteName` and `WelcomeOverlay.jsx`'s greeting, rather than a new/different condition.
**Rationale:** `settings_service.get_site_name()` always returns a real string (defaulting to the literal `"Keep Track"` when unset), so a naive "is it set" check would show `"Keep Track — Keep Track"` on every unconfigured instance. The existing frontend convention for "is this actually customised" already solved this exact problem; reusing it keeps "what counts as a real instance name" consistent everywhere it's checked, backend included.

**Decision:** The report title (`routers/reports.py`'s `generate_report` — `f"{site_name} {type_label} (...)"`) and the downloaded filename (`download_report`'s `Content-Disposition: filename=f"{report.title}.pdf"`) were left unchanged.
**Rationale:** Both already include the instance name today — the title is built from `site_name` whenever the caller doesn't supply a custom one, and the download filename is derived directly from that title. Verified live (below) rather than assumed: with `site_name` set, a generated report's title read `"Stayton Road - KHOC Historical Report (...)"` and downloaded as `"Stayton Road - KHOC Historical Report (...).pdf"`. The task brief's own filename example (`KHOC_Keep_Track_Report_2026.pdf`) is a different literal format (underscored slug vs. the app's existing space-separated title style) but the substantive requirement — the instance name is present in the filename — was already met, so no change was made rather than reformatting an already-correct, already-tested filename convention for cosmetic reasons alone.

**Decision:** `WelcomeOverlay.jsx`'s two hardcoded quick-start card descriptions ("Add an invoice or bill to get started", "Log your monthly income or contributions") were rewritten to use the same terminology variables their own titles already used (`` `Add your first ${expenseSingular} to get started` ``, `` `Log your ${incomeLower} to get started` ``) — everything else on the page (greeting instance name, card titles, the main welcome paragraph) was already terminology-driven and needed no change.
**Rationale:** The titles were already correct (`Upload your first ${expenseSingular}`, `Record ${incomeLower}`), which made the two description lines underneath them an easy-to-miss inconsistency — a personal deployment configured with "Bills"/"Income" would have read "Upload your first bill" directly above "Add an invoice or bill to get started," mixing its own configured vocabulary with the organisational defaults it was specifically not supposed to see. This was the one real gap found in this fix; everything else the task asked to check (dynamic terminology, instance-name greeting fallback, the "View your dashboard"/"Run a report" cards staying unchanged) was already implemented correctly from an earlier task.

**Decision:** The main welcome message was reworded from "...and understand your financial position at a glance. Everything is in one place, updated in real time." to "...and stay on top of your finances — all in one place," closely following the task brief's own suggested replacement copy.
**Rationale:** Not a functional bug (the old copy was already terminology-driven and jargon-free), but the brief explicitly asked for warmer, simpler phrasing here, and its suggested rewrite was a direct improvement over the more clinical "understand your financial position" — adopted near-verbatim rather than inventing a third variant.

**Not addressed in this pass:** No change was made to the dashboard/report quick-start cards' own copy ("View your dashboard" / "See your financial position at a glance", "Run a report" / "Generate a summary of your finances") — the brief explicitly calls both "unchanged, always relevant."

**Tested:** `python3 -m py_compile` on `services/pdf_report_service.py` and `routers/reports.py` (clean); `docker compose restart backend` (uvicorn runs without `--reload` in this dev setup, confirmed via the Dockerfile — a restart, not just a file save, was required to pick up the change) with clean startup logs. `vite build` clean for the frontend change. Generated real report PDFs by calling `report_service.build_report_data` + `pdf_report_service.generate_report_pdf` directly against this dev instance's real data (bypassing the API/auth layer only for speed — same real code path `routers/reports.py` calls), then inspected every page's extracted text and rendered two pages to PNG with PyMuPDF (already a backend dependency): confirmed with `site_name = "Stayton Road - KHOC"` that the cover page (page 1) has no footer at all, every subsequent page reads "Stayton Road - KHOC — Keep Track" with the horizontal rule and correct right-aligned "Page N of 7" (verified N runs 1 through 7 across all 7 non-cover pages, including a table-heavy page and the final Notes page), and that resetting `site_name` back to the literal default `"Keep Track"` correctly collapses the footer to plain "Keep Track" with no double-naming. Verified the welcome page end-to-end with a real, driven browser session (Playwright, headless Chromium, the same reactivated-then-redeactivated `uitester2` throwaway Admin account from the previous polish pass — this app has no hard delete for users with audit log rows) against the real running dev server, screenshotting in both light and dark mode (set via the same `keeptrack-theme` `localStorage` key `ThemeContext.jsx` itself reads, since the welcome overlay's full-viewport backdrop is deliberately unclickable and blocks the header's own theme toggle) for three real configurations: the dev instance's actual organisational settings (`site_name = "Stayton Road - KHOC"`, `Invoices`/`Contributions`) — confirmed "Welcome to Keep Track — Stayton Road - KHOC" and both fixed quick-start descriptions read naturally; `site_name` reset to the default (no custom name) — confirmed the greeting correctly fell back to plain "Welcome to Keep Track"; and a personal-finance terminology preset (`Bills`/`Income`) — confirmed "Upload your first bill" / "Add your first bill to get started" and "Record income" / "Log your income to get started" both read naturally with no leftover organisational wording, in both themes. All three of the dev instance's real settings (`site_name`, `term_expenses`, `term_income`) were restored to their original values afterward, and the throwaway test account was deactivated again once verification was complete.

## 2026-08-08 — Polish: audit log completeness verification

**Decision:** Every action type named in the task brief's checklist was checked against the actual `log_action()` call sites (found via a full-repo grep of `audit_service.log_action(`, not just a search for each literal string, since several — `budget.created`/`budget.updated`, `invoice.resigned` vs `invoice.signed` — are chosen dynamically at runtime and would be invisible to a literal-string search). The large majority were already correctly implemented: every AUTHENTICATION item except `session_timeout` and `welcome_dismissed` (both explicitly low-priority in the brief), all of USER MANAGEMENT, all of INVOICES (including the folder-integration events, which already exist under a `folder.*` prefix rather than `invoice.folder_*` — consistent with how the SYSTEM section of the brief itself names `folder.output_written`/`folder.output_failed`), all of CONTRIBUTIONS, RECONCILIATION, PROJECTS, SETTINGS, BUDGETS, DEBTS, IMPORTS, REPORTS, and SYSTEM aside from the two gaps below.

**Found and fixed — real gaps:**
- **`routers/categories.py` had zero audit logging** — create, edit, deactivate, and restore all silently skipped `audit_service.log_action` entirely (the `_admin` dependency parameter wasn't even bound to a variable). Categories drive every invoice's financial classification and every Budget Planning figure, so this was treated as a financial-data gap, not a cosmetic one. Added `category.created`/`category.edited` (with a `changed_fields` before/after diff, matching `invoice.edited`'s and `contribution.edited`'s existing shape)/`category.deactivated`/`category.restored`, and bound the previously-unused admin dependency to a real `admin` variable so `user_id` could be attributed. Verified live via `curl` against the real running dev backend (see below).
- **`backup_service.restore_from_zip()` recorded `system.restored` with no `user_id` at all.** Investigating why revealed it wasn't an oversight so much as an unfinished half of a correct instinct: by the time that audit entry is written, the database underneath the session has already been replaced by the restored dump, so the pre-restore Superadmin's numeric id has no guaranteed matching row in the *new* `users` table — attaching it as `audit_log.user_id` (a real foreign key) risks either misattributing the action to a different, unrelated user who happens to share that id in the restored database, or an outright FK violation if no row with that id exists there at all. The fix threads the acting Superadmin's *username* (captured by `routers/storage.py` before the request's own DB session is closed, since closing it early is what unblocks the restore subprocess — see the existing 2026-08-0x restore-locking decision above) through to `restore_from_zip` as a new `actor_username` parameter, recorded as plain text in the entry's description and metadata rather than as `user_id`. This preserves "who restored the system" in the audit trail without the FK risk.

**Found and fixed — low priority (per the brief):**
- `POST /auth/welcome/dismiss` now logs `user.welcome_dismissed`, only on the real true→false transition (an already-dismissed welcome staying dismissed logs nothing, matching the endpoint's existing idempotency).
- Session timeout had no server-side audit trail at all — `useSessionTimeout.js`'s inactivity path only ever called `DELETE /auth/mfa-remember` (a different action, incidentally logged as `user.mfa_remember_revoked` only when a remember token happened to exist) and then cleared local state without calling `POST /auth/logout`, so a timed-out session was frequently invisible to the audit log entirely. Added `POST /auth/session-timeout` (authenticated, logs `user.session_timeout`) and a best-effort call to it from `performInactivityLogout` alongside the existing MFA-remember revoke, using the still-valid access token in the instant before it's discarded client-side.

**Deliberately left as-is — already covered by an existing, broader event:** `invoice.project_linked` (captured in `invoice.reviewed`'s own description text when confirming with a project, and in `invoice.edited`'s `changed_fields` when linked via an edit — matches this app's existing precedent of merging `invoice.reviewed`/`invoice.confirmed` into one event rather than two for the same action, documented at the point that endpoint was built); `project.funding_target_set` (a field captured by `project.created`/`project.edited`, the same way every other project field change is — no other project field gets its own dedicated action type, and funding-target fields aren't a special case); `user.force_password_changed` (logged as `user.password_changed` with `metadata: {"forced": true}` rather than a separate action type, since it's the same fundamental action — a password changing — with one extra fact attached, not a functionally distinct event). None of these were asked to be split out for a concrete reason found during this pass, so none were changed.

**Tested:** `python3 -m py_compile` on every touched backend file — clean. `docker compose restart backend` — clean startup logs, no import errors. Exercised the real API end-to-end with `curl` using a locally-minted JWT (the app's own `create_token`, run inside the backend container for the real Superadmin account, matching the established testing pattern elsewhere in this log) against the actual running dev server: created, edited (colour change), deactivated, and restored a real throwaway category (`AuditTestCategory`) and confirmed all four new `category.*` entries appeared in `GET /logs/audit` with the correct description and `changed_fields` metadata; called the new `POST /auth/session-timeout` and confirmed a `204` and a `user.session_timeout` entry; called `POST /auth/welcome/dismiss` on an account that had already dismissed it and confirmed correctly that no new entry was logged (idempotency preserved). The test category was deactivated again afterward (this app has no hard delete for categories) rather than left active. `backup_service.restore_from_zip`'s fix was verified by code review and a compile check only — actually exercising a full database restore against this shared dev instance was judged too destructive to run as part of a routine polish-pass verification; the change is a small, narrowly-scoped parameter addition to an existing, already-tested code path (the 2026-08-0x backup/restore build's own end-to-end restore test already covered `restore_from_zip` itself).

## 2026-08-08 — Polish: permanent user delete with GDPR anonymisation

**Decision:** Permanently deleting a user (`DELETE /auth/users/{id}`, Admin only) **anonymises** every financial record they ever touched — the `users.id` foreign key on `invoices.created_by`, `contributions.recorded_by`, `monthly_reconciliations.reconciled_by`/`edited_by`, `planned_projects.created_by`/`edited_by`, `reports.generated_by`, `debts.created_by`, `debt_payments.recorded_by`, `category_budgets.created_by`, `savings_goals.created_by`, `import_batches.imported_by`, `error_log.user_id`/`resolved_by`, `settings.updated_by`, and `system_events.performed_by` is set to `NULL` — rather than deleting or cascading those records. `audit_log`/`audit_log_archive` rows for the deleted user are kept the same way (`user_id` set to `NULL`) with `'[Deleted User] '` prepended to their `description`, rather than being deleted.
**Rationale:** GDPR's right to erasure (Article 17) covers *personal data*, not an organisation's own financial and accounting records — a KHOC-style deployment has independent obligations (charity/committee accounting, HMRC) to keep an accurate history of who authorised what, even after that person has left and asked to be forgotten. Deleting the rows outright would destroy that history; cascading the delete would be actively wrong (an invoice or a reconciled month is a fact about the organisation's finances, not about the departed user). Setting the attribution to `NULL` and marking the audit trail's description keeps every number, date, and category exactly as it was — only the "who" is removed — which is the same anonymise-don't-delete precedent already used for the two service-authored audit gaps fixed in the previous entry (`backup_service.restore_from_zip`'s FK-risk workaround) and matches this app's general pattern of preferring `NULL`/soft markers over hard deletes for anything with real history (categories, savings goals, `invoices.deleted`, `contributions.deleted`, `reports.deleted`).

**Decision:** Every one of those `users.id` foreign key columns that was previously `NOT NULL` (ten of them — `invoices.created_by`, `contributions.recorded_by`, `monthly_reconciliations.reconciled_by`, `planned_projects.created_by`, `reports.generated_by`, `debts.created_by`, `debt_payments.recorded_by`, `category_budgets.created_by`, `savings_goals.created_by`, `import_batches.imported_by`) was widened to nullable by migration `0044`. The six that were already nullable (`audit_log`/`audit_log_archive.user_id`, `error_log.user_id`/`resolved_by`, `monthly_reconciliations.edited_by`, `planned_projects.edited_by`, `settings.updated_by`, `system_events.performed_by`) needed no schema change.
**Rationale:** Every one of those columns was originally `NOT NULL` on the reasonable assumption that "every invoice/contribution/etc. must have been created by *someone*" — true until permanent deletion existed. Without this migration, `DELETE /auth/users/{id}` would fail with a foreign-key violation on the very first anonymisation `UPDATE` against any table where the deleted user has a row, since Postgres enforces `NOT NULL` independently of the FK's own referential-integrity check. `services/user_deletion_service.py` centralises the full list of `(model, column)` pairs so the anonymisation logic and this migration's own `TABLE_COLUMNS` list describe the same set of columns from two different layers (ORM and schema) rather than one being derived from the other.

**Decision:** Deletion requires the target account to already be **deactivated** (`is_active = false`) — attempting to delete an active user returns `400` with `'User must be deactivated before deletion. Deactivate the user first, then delete.'` — and, once that's satisfied, a second explicit confirmation: a `confirmation_phrase` request body field that must equal `'DELETE USER'` exactly (case sensitive), returning `403` on any mismatch.
**Rationale:** Deactivation is already this app's normal, everyday, fully-reversible way to remove someone's access (`PUT /auth/users/{id}/deactivate`) — requiring it as a precondition means permanent deletion is never the first, fastest button an Admin reaches for by habit; they have to have already made the easy, reversible call before the hard, irreversible one becomes available at all. The typed confirmation phrase is the same UX pattern already established by `POST /system/reset` (`'RESET KEEP TRACK'`) and `DELETE /logs/errors/clear-all` (`'CLEAR ERROR LOG'`) for this app's other irreversible actions — deliberately reused rather than inventing a third confirmation style. Unlike the system reset's `403` (chosen because that endpoint conflates "wrong phrase" with "not authorised to even attempt this" for its Superadmin-only gate) and the error log's `400`, this endpoint's `403` was specified directly by the task brief and was kept as an explicit deliberate choice for this endpoint rather than pattern-matched from either of the other two, since both are separately defensible depnding on whether a wrong phrase reads as "bad input" (400) or "not confirmed" (403).
**Not addressed in this pass:** deleting a user's own account and deleting the Superadmin account are both rejected — reusing the existing `_reject_action_on_self_or_superadmin` helper already shared by the role-change/deactivate/reset-password endpoints — rather than writing new, endpoint-specific copy for those two checks, since the existing `'Cannot {action} your own account'` / `'Cannot {action} the Superadmin account'` messages already read correctly for "delete" with no changes needed.

**Decision:** The user's avatar and signature files are deleted via the existing `services/profile_service.remove_avatar`/`remove_signature` (only called when `user.has_avatar`/`has_signature` is true), and their `mfa_remember_tokens`/`notifications` rows are hard-deleted outright — the one place in this feature where "delete" really does mean delete, not anonymise.
**Rationale:** These are the account's actual personal data (a photo, a signature image, active "remember this device" session tokens, in-app notification text) with no financial or audit value of their own once the account is gone — unlike an invoice or a reconciliation, nothing about the organisation's records references a leftover notification or a stale MFA token, so there's nothing to preserve by anonymising them instead of deleting them outright.

**Tested:** `python3 -m py_compile` on every touched backend file (clean); `alembic upgrade head` against the real dev Postgres container applied migration `0044` cleanly on top of `0043`; `docker compose restart backend` — clean startup logs, no import errors; `vite build` — clean. Exercised the real API end-to-end with `curl` against the actual running dev server using a real Superadmin session token: self-registered and approved a real throwaway Standard user (`gdprtest_del`), logged them in for real (computed a live TOTP code from their own `mfa_secret` with `pyotp`, run inside the backend container), had them record a real contribution (`recorded_by = ` their own id) and log out — all under their own real session, not faked — confirmed `DELETE /auth/users/{id}` correctly returned `400` while the account was still active, `400` when targeting the Superadmin's own id, and `403` for both a lower-case and a wrong-case confirmation phrase once deactivated; confirmed the correct-phrase call returned `200` with the expected success message, that the user row was gone from the database entirely, that their contribution row survived with `recorded_by` now `NULL` and its financial fields (amount, group name) untouched, that all five of their real audit log entries (`user.created`, `user.login`, `user.mfa_verified`, `contribution.created`, `user.logout`) now had `user_id = NULL` and a `'[Deleted User] '`-prefixed description, and that a new `user.deleted` entry existed attributed to the Superadmin's own id. Also confirmed directly in Postgres that no `mfa_remember_tokens` or `notifications` rows remained for the deleted user. Separately drove a real, driven browser session (Playwright, headless Chromium, the same throwaway-driver-script approach as prior sessions per this log) against the actual running dev server, authenticated via the real Superadmin session token seeded into `localStorage`: registered and approved a second real throwaway Admin account (`gdprtest_ui`) via the API, then in the browser confirmed the Delete button renders disabled with a "Deactivate user before deleting" tooltip while the account is active; deactivated it via the UI's own Deactivate flow; confirmed Delete became enabled; opened the confirmation modal and confirmed its title, red warning text, the four-item "what will happen" list, and that the "Delete permanently" button stayed disabled for both an empty and a wrong-case confirmation phrase and only enabled once `DELETE USER` was typed exactly; clicked through to a real deletion and confirmed the green success message and that the row disappeared from the table with no reload needed. No new browser console errors beyond the pre-existing, unrelated CSP `frame-ancestors`-via-`<meta>` warnings and an expected 403 on the deleted account's own now-nonexistent avatar request. Both throwaway accounts used for this test were fully, permanently deleted by the test itself — there was nothing left to clean up afterward.

## 2026-08-08 — Polish: callout tooltips on notifications and status badges

**Decision:** `components/Tooltip.jsx` renders its bubble via a **React portal into `document.body`**, positioned with `position: fixed` computed from the trigger's `getBoundingClientRect()` (recomputed on show, and kept in sync with a capture-phase `scroll` listener plus a `resize` listener while open), rather than a bubble positioned relative to its own trigger in the normal DOM tree.
**Rationale:** Found, not assumed, during verification: `.kt-invoices-table th, .kt-invoices-table td { overflow: hidden }` (this table class is reused by the invoices, reconciliation, search, and several other tables per its own docs/decisions-log.md precedent) silently clipped a same-parent-positioned tooltip the instant it tried to pop outside its cell's box — a real, reproducible defect (confirmed with a driven Playwright session: the bubble's `getBoundingClientRect()` showed it rendering, but almost entirely invisible, cut off by the cell it lived in), not a hypothetical edge case, since it hit the single most common place this task asked for a badge tooltip (a table cell). `position: fixed` escapes clipping from any ancestor that doesn't itself establish a new containing block via `transform`/`filter`/`will-change` (none of this app's scrollable containers do), which is the standard, well-established fix for exactly this class of problem. The trigger keeps its own `aria-describedby` reference to the portalled bubble's `id` regardless of where in the DOM it actually renders, so the accessibility relationship is unaffected by the portal.

**Decision:** The bubble is **only mounted while visible** (conditionally rendered, not kept in the DOM and faded via CSS), with a `@keyframes` opacity animation applied on mount for a fade-in rather than a `transition` from a permanently-present `opacity: 0` state.
**Rationale:** A `position: fixed` portal has no "in place" to fade from — its coordinates are only meaningful once the trigger's real on-screen position is known, so there is nothing correct to render (or transition) before that first measurement. Mounting fresh each time also means a CSS `animation` (which auto-plays on element creation) gets the fade-in for free, without the usual "opacity:0 kept in the DOM forever" trick a transition would need.

**Decision:** Touch input is distinguished from mouse input **per-interaction**, via the trigger's own `pointerdown` event's `pointerType`, rather than a device-wide capability check (e.g. `'ontouchstart' in window` or a `matchMedia('(hover: none)')` check) done once.
**Rationale:** A touchscreen laptop or a convertible device can use either input in the same session, and a device-wide check would either (a) permanently disable the 300ms hover-delay behaviour for a device that also has a trackpad, or (b) permanently disable tap-to-show for a device that also has a touchscreen. Checking the pointer type of the interaction that's actually happening right now gets the right behaviour either way with no device sniffing.

**Decision:** A touch-triggered tap on the trigger calls `preventDefault()`/`stopPropagation()` on that tap's click (so it shows the tooltip instead of firing whatever the trigger's parent does), but a **plain mouse click is left completely alone** — it isn't intercepted even when the tooltip happens to already be showing from hover.
**Rationale:** Several of the badges this task wired up sit inside a row or card that itself navigates or opens a modal on click (`InvoicesPage.jsx`'s `<tr onClick={() => navigate(...)}>`, `BudgetPage.jsx`'s `<div className="kt-budget-row" onClick={() => onRowClick(b)}>`, and the Search Results project list's `<Link>` wrapping the whole row) — and one of this task's own badge tooltips (`Unreviewed`: "Click to open and review") explicitly assumes that click-through still works. A mouse user already gets the explanation for free on hover, with no need to also click for it, so leaving mouse clicks unintercepted preserves every one of those existing click-to-open/click-to-navigate behaviours exactly as they were; a touch user has no hover at all, so their first tap is repurposed to reveal the tooltip instead (a second tap elsewhere, or on the row's non-badge area, still reaches the original action). The one deliberate exception is the dedicated (i) info icon (`InfoTooltip`, used for notifications and dashboard banners) — see below.

**Decision:** `InfoTooltip`'s own info-icon button unconditionally stops click propagation (an explicit wrapping `onClick={(e) => e.stopPropagation()}` in `NotificationBell.jsx`, matched by the icon's own button semantics elsewhere), regardless of mouse or touch.
**Rationale:** Unlike a status badge (which already had its own pre-existing click-through meaning before this task), the (i) icon is a brand-new element introduced solely to carry a tooltip — it was never part of a notification row's "click to open" contract, so a mouse click landing on it should never be mistaken for a click on the row underneath (which would mark the notification read and navigate away before the user got to read the explanation they just clicked for).

**Decision:** Tooltip content lives in two small static lookup modules — `utils/notificationTooltips.js` (the 9 persistent notification types) and `utils/badgeTooltips.js` (invoice/reconciliation/project/debt/budget badges) — as plain strings, not templates interpolated with a specific instance's own figures (a month name, a percentage, a day count), with one exception: `promoWarningTooltip(daysRemaining, standardRate)` in `badgeTooltips.js`, which *is* a small template function.
**Rationale:** For the notification-type tooltips, the notification's own visible message text already carries the concrete numbers ("3 months still not reconciled", "Current balance (£X) is below the target reserve (£Y)") — duplicating them into the tooltip would either drift out of sync with that message over time or require threading extra context (dashboard summary fields like `reserve_months`) into `NotificationBell.jsx`, which today fetches nothing but the notification list itself. A single shared static string used identically in both the bell dropdown and the dashboard banners also guarantees the wording can never quietly diverge between the two surfaces. The debt promo-warning tooltip is the one case where the real per-debt figures (`debt.days_until_promo_ends`, `debt.standard_rate_after_promo`) are already sitting right there on the object being rendered, at zero extra fetch cost, so interpolating them is a strict improvement with no new coupling — worth the one exception to the "static string" rule.

**Decision:** Reused the shared `PERSISTENT_NOTIFICATION_TYPE` lookup already defined in `DashboardPage.jsx` (live dashboard banner `type` → persistent notification `type`, e.g. `balance_below_target` → `balance_warning`) to look up each banner's tooltip content, and fixed one stale case label found while doing so: `notificationLink()`'s switch statement checked for a `'planned_project_overdue'` type that has never existed on either the live banner or the persistent notification (the real value, confirmed against `routers/dashboard.py`, is `'project_overdue'`) — dead code that happened to be harmless only because the `default:` branch it fell through to (`notification.link`) already resolved to the same `/projects` URL.
**Rationale:** A frontend-only, one-line correction directly in the same dictionary this task needed to read correctly to wire banner tooltips up at all — not a pre-existing behavioural bug worth a separate investigation (every other case label in that switch, and every key in `PERSISTENT_NOTIFICATION_TYPE`, was already correct against the real backend `type` strings), just a stale label left over from before `project_overdue`'s current name, fixed in passing rather than left sitting next to the tooltip lookup that now depends on the same map being accurate.

**Not addressed in this pass:** Tooltip content is written with this app's default terminology ("Reconciliation", "Projects", "Debt Tracking") rather than reading through `useTerminology()`/`useDebtTerminology()`/`useBudgetTerminology()` for a deployment with renamed terms — the task brief's own example text hardcodes these same names, and making every tooltip terminology-aware would mean threading three more context hooks into `NotificationBell.jsx` (which currently uses none) for a cosmetic mismatch on personal-finance-terminology deployments, not a functional one.

**Tested:** `vite build` after every change (clean). Verified with a real, driven browser session (Playwright, headless Chromium, the same throwaway-driver-script approach as prior sessions per this log) against the actual running dev server, authenticated via a real Superadmin session token seeded into `localStorage`: confirmed the 300ms hover delay on an Invoices table badge (not yet visible at 100ms, visible at 450ms) with the correct tooltip text; confirmed `aria-describedby` on the trigger matches the portalled tooltip's own `id`; confirmed a real mouse click on a badge still navigates to the invoice detail page (click-through preserved); confirmed dark mode (`localStorage` `keeptrack-theme=dark`) renders the tooltip with the correct, slightly-lighter `#3D3D3A` background and white text per the existing `--kt-tooltip-bg`/`--kt-tooltip-text` tokens (already used by the chart tooltip, reused here rather than inventing new ones); confirmed a 390px mobile/touch viewport shows the tooltip on tap without navigating, and that a second tap elsewhere dismisses it; confirmed Escape dismisses an open tooltip; confirmed the Notification Bell's (i) icon shows its tooltip on keyboard focus (not just mouse hover) and that clicking it does not close the notification panel or mark the row read; confirmed a Reconciliation month tile's status tooltip renders with the correct text. The table-cell clipping defect described above was caught by this same verification pass (a tooltip that measured as rendering but was visually almost entirely cut off), fixed with the portal approach, and re-verified with a cropped screenshot showing the bubble fully clear of its table cell in both light and dark mode. No new browser console errors beyond the pre-existing, unrelated CSP `frame-ancestors`-via-`<meta>` warning and an expected 403 on an unset avatar request.

## 2026-08-08 — Polish: help guides content review and accuracy update

**Decision:** Reviewed all twelve guides in `user-guides/` against the actual current frontend components and backend routes (not against memory or the guides' own prior claims) — reading `SettingsNav.jsx`/`SettingsPage.jsx`, `GeneralSettings.jsx`, `AppearanceSettings.jsx`, `SecuritySettings.jsx`, `NotificationsLogsSettings.jsx`, `ReconciliationPage.jsx`, `SigningPanel.jsx`, `WelcomeOverlay.jsx`, `SetupPage.jsx`, `HeaderSearch.jsx`, `ReportsPage.jsx`, `routers/dashboard.py`, `routers/help.py`, and the `feature_modules` seed migration — rather than assuming the guides already matched the app, since several had drifted since they were first written.
**Rationale:** A help guide that's confidently wrong is worse than no guide at all — a user follows "Settings → Terminology" and there's no such place, or "Settings → Danger Zone" and gives up looking. The task brief asked for an accuracy review specifically because the app has changed significantly since these were written (new modules, renamed sections), so checking the guides against their own prose wouldn't have caught anything; checking them against the real, current UI was the only way to find genuine drift.

**Found and fixed — real inaccuracies in `settings-guide.md`:**
- Five "To get there" instructions pointed at places that don't exist or are wrong: **Terminology** ("Settings → Terminology" → actually under **Appearance**), **Target Reserve** and **Financial year** (both claimed their own top-level section → actually both live under **General**, alongside Instance name and App start date, neither of which was documented at all), **Categories** ("Settings → Categories in the sidebar" → there is no such sidebar item; it's a **Manage categories** button within General that opens a separate page), and **System reset** ("Settings → Danger Zone" → the real nav item is **Data → System Reset**, "Danger Zone" isn't a real label anywhere in the UI).
- The entire **Security** section of Settings (Session timeout, MFA remember duration, and the Signing and export toggle) had no documented home at all — Signing and export was described as being "on the Settings page" with no actual location, and the other two settings weren't mentioned anywhere in this guide.
- The **"Notification thresholds"** section described a setting ("decide how many days an invoice can sit unreviewed") that has no UI to configure it — `NotificationsLogsSettings.jsx`'s Notifications tab is a literal `<div>Coming soon.</div>` placeholder; the actual value (`unconfirmed_invoice_alert_days`) is a backend-only environment variable. Removed the section rather than leave a documented control that doesn't exist to click.
- **Users & Access** and **Storage & Backup** were reached via slightly wrong nav paths ("Settings → Users" and "Settings → Storage & Backup" with no "Data →" prefix) — corrected to match `SettingsNav.jsx`'s real category/sub-item structure.
Rewrote the guide's structure to follow the real Settings nav order (General, Appearance, Security, AI & Extraction, Users & Access, Notifications & Logs, Data) so every remaining "To get there" line was verified against `SettingsPage.jsx` while writing it, not carried over from the old structure.

**Found and fixed — real gaps in other guides:**
- `getting-started.md` didn't mention two real, current parts of first login: the **AI configuration** setup wizard step (`SetupPage.jsx`'s `'ai'` step — provider, optional API key, skippable) and the **welcome overlay** shown once after first login (`WelcomeOverlay.jsx`) — nor the **Folder Integration** module, alongside the two it did already cover (Debt Tracking, Budget Planning).
- `reviewing-and-signing.md` didn't mention that a saved signature (set from My Profile) is offered and used by default ahead of drawing one fresh each time (`SigningPanel.jsx`'s `signatureMode` defaulting to `'saved'` when `user.has_signature`), or that the signer's name is stamped onto the signed PDF automatically — both real, and both explicitly asked about in the task brief.
- `dashboard-guide.md` didn't mention the header's own **search bar** (Cmd+K/Ctrl+K, confirmed in `HeaderSearch.jsx`), the **notification bell**, or the time-of-day greeting (`Header.jsx`'s `greetingForHour`) — all present on every page, not just the dashboard.
- `reconciliation-guide.md` said a reconciled month is simply "locked" with no way to correct it — in reality an Administrator can reopen and correct one via **Edit (Admin)** (confirmed in `ReconciliationPage.jsx`'s `openAdminEdit`/`submitAdminEdit`, `models/monthly_reconciliation.py`'s `edited_by`/`edit_reason` columns), with a required reason recorded against their account. The guide also didn't explain **why** a reconciliation goes stale, only that a "Stale" state exists.
- `reports-guide.md` didn't mention the **Export CSV** button on the Previous reports table (`ReportsPage.jsx`'s `handleExportCsv`) — a real control, distinct from downloading an individual report's own PDF.
- `debt-tracking.md`'s Personal Finance dashboard section was stale in a way directly caused by a later feature: it still described the Savings goals panel as "a placeholder until Budget Planning is built," written before Budget Planning existed. Budget Planning has since been built (see the 2026-08-08 Budget Planning entry above), and the panel now shows real goal data when it's enabled — `budget-planning.md` and `dashboard-guide.md` both already said this correctly; only `debt-tracking.md`'s own copy of the same description had been left behind.

**Decision:** Every guide now ends with a **Related guides** section (a plain link list to other relevant guides), and every guide that leans on one of the five renameable labels (Invoices, Contributions, Projects, Reconciliation, Target Reserve) now has a one- or two-line note near the top that the label is configurable from Settings → Appearance and that the guide uses the default name throughout.
**Rationale:** Both were explicit, mechanical consistency requirements in the task brief, absent from most of the twelve guides beforehand (a handful already had an equivalent note or a natural "what to read next" list; most had neither in a consistent, identifiable form). Doing this uniformly means a reader who's renamed "Invoices" to "Bills" isn't confused by a guide that never explains why its own screenshots-in-words don't match their sidebar, and a reader who finishes one guide always has an obvious next place to go.

**Not addressed in this pass:** No guide content was found describing a feature that has been fully removed (every stale reference found was either a wrong location for something that still exists, or a description of a UI control that was never built) — so this pass added missing sections and corrected locations, but didn't need to delete any guide content wholesale. `docs/features.md`/`docs/database-schema.md` and other files under `docs/` were not reviewed as part of this pass — the task scope was `user-guides/` only.

**Tested:** Every "To get there" instruction in the rewritten `settings-guide.md`, and every new claim added to the other eleven guides, was checked directly against the relevant frontend component or backend route as listed above, not inferred from the guides' own prior wording. Confirmed via `grep` that all twelve guides now contain a `## Related guides` heading, that every internal Markdown link in `user-guides/` resolves to a real file in that directory or a real file under `docs/` (`storage-and-backup.md`, `decisions-log.md`), and that no guide references the now-corrected wrong paths ("Danger Zone" as a literal destination, "Settings → Terminology", "Notification thresholds") any more. Cross-checked the `routers/help.py` topic-key table against every guide's filename to confirm the in-app Help links (`HelpIconLink`'s `topic` prop, used throughout the frontend) still resolve correctly — unaffected by this pass, since no guide was renamed or moved.

## 2026-08-08 — Polish: notifications settings tab with preferences, thresholds and history

**Decision:** Ten `notif_*` on/off preferences and four `notif_*_days`/`notif_*_percent` thresholds are two conceptually distinct kinds of setting — a preference controls whether a notification is ever raised for a condition; a threshold controls when that condition is considered true in the first place — and every call site respects that distinction explicitly: the threshold is always resolved and applied first (so `notifications`/live banners reflect the *right* condition), and the preference check wraps only the actual notification-creation call (`notification_service.notify_admins`/`create_notification`), never the underlying computation. `services/settings_service.py` exposes them as two separate dict getters, `get_notification_preferences`/`get_notification_thresholds`, mirroring the existing `get_terminology` pattern.
**Rationale:** Keeping the two concerns visibly separate in the code (not just in the Settings UI's two section headings) makes each call site's intent legible on its own — `if overdue_months and prefs["notif_reconciliation_overdue"]:` reads as exactly what it does, rather than one combined boolean whose two different reasons for being true/false would need to be reverse-engineered later.

**Decision:** `notif_budget_warning_percent` is read and applied **only** inside `budget_notification_service.check_budget_thresholds()` (the background notification check) — it does not touch `budget_service.WARNING_THRESHOLD`, the separate, still-fixed-at-80% constant that drives the On Track/Warning/Over Budget status badge shown on the Budget Planning table, cards, monthly detail view, dashboard panel, and PDF report (per the 2026-08-08 Budget Planning entry above: "the same three colours are used consistently across the table, the cards, the monthly detail view, the dashboard panel, and the PDF report, so a red badge always means the same thing wherever you see it").
**Rationale:** The task brief scopes this setting narrowly — "How much of a budget must be used before **sending a warning notification**" — and it lives under a "Notification thresholds" heading, not a Budget Planning settings heading. Wiring it into the shared `WARNING_THRESHOLD` constant instead would have silently redefined what the badge means everywhere else in the app the moment an Admin changed this one notification-timing setting, breaking the "always means the same thing" guarantee that section explicitly called out and tested. Similarly, `notif_promo_rate_warning_days` **does** replace `debt_service.PROMO_WARNING_DAYS` everywhere (both the debt card/detail-page amber highlight and the notification check) — a deliberate asymmetry with the budget case, because a debt's `promo_expiring_soon` flag was never presented as an independently fixed, separately-documented threshold the way the budget traffic light was; it's the same "is this worth flagging" concept driving both the UI highlight and the notification, so keeping them in sync is the more intuitive behaviour, not a regression.

**Decision:** `services/debt_service.py`'s `debt_computed_fields()` takes `promo_warning_days` as a **required** keyword-only `int` argument (no default, no `db` session) rather than resolving the setting itself; `debt_to_out()` resolves it once (lazily, for single-debt lookups) and `routers/debts.py`'s list endpoint and `debt_service.build_dashboard_fields()`'s per-debt loop each resolve it **once before their loop** and pass the same value to every debt.
**Rationale:** Found while implementing, not assumed: `debt_computed_fields` is called once per debt in both `GET /debts` (a list comprehension) and the dashboard's debt summary — reading the setting from inside it (as first written) would have turned one settings-table query into N per request, an avoidable N+1 for no benefit, since every debt in the same request should see the same threshold anyway. Making the parameter required with no fallback default (rather than silently defaulting to the migration-seeded value) was also deliberate: a call site that forgot to resolve the real setting would otherwise silently use a possibly-stale built-in number instead of failing loudly.

**Decision:** Both `budget_notification_service.check_budget_thresholds()`'s `_record_sent` (the per-category/month/threshold dedup guard) and `debt_service.check_and_fire_milestones()`'s `DebtMilestone` row (the per-debt/percentage "already happened" guard) are written **unconditionally** once their threshold is crossed, regardless of whether the matching `notif_*` preference is on — only the `notify_admins`/`create_notification` call itself is skipped when the preference is off.
**Rationale:** Both guards exist to answer "has this specific, one-time-per-entity event already been dealt with," a factual question about the entity's own history, independent of whether anyone wanted to be told about it. If the guard row were skipped along with the notification while a preference was off, turning that preference back on later would fire a flood of backdated notifications for thresholds crossed weeks or months earlier (a category that's been over budget all along, a debt that passed 50% ages ago) — clearly wrong, and a worse experience than simply not being told about it at the time.

**Decision:** `GET /notifications/history` (and its CSV export sibling) is **site-wide** — every notification for every user in the last 30 days — not filtered to the requesting Admin's own notifications, matching the Audit Log and Error Log tables it sits alongside in the same Settings page rather than the personal, per-user `GET /notifications` the header bell already uses.
**Rationale:** The task brief's own description ("All notifications sent in the last 30 days") and its placement under a "Notification history" heading next to Preferences/Thresholds (both genuinely site-wide settings) both read as an admin audit view, not a personal inbox — and this app already has a personal inbox (the header bell). Since `notify_admins` creates one row per Admin/Superadmin, a site-wide history will show near-duplicate rows for the same event (one per recipient) rather than de-duplicating — left as-is, since each row is a real, distinct notification with its own independent read/dismissed state, not a display artefact.

**Decision:** The Notifications tab's Preferences and Thresholds sections use **deferred save** (toggles/dropdowns only update local draft state; a "Save preferences"/"Save thresholds" button — disabled until something's actually changed — persists them), unlike `FeatureModulesSettings.jsx`'s toggles, which save immediately on click.
**Rationale:** Specified directly by the task brief ("Save preferences button", "Save thresholds button"), and it suits this screen better than immediate-save anyway: a module toggle is a single, self-contained action with its own confirmation step; changing several notification preferences at once and reviewing them together before committing is a more natural flow here, especially since — unlike a module toggle — there's no individually risky action among them worth confirming one at a time.

**Not addressed in this pass:** The task brief's frontend spec says "Read Only users see the preferences and thresholds as read-only... with a note" — implemented at the component level (`NotificationSettings.jsx` renders a plain read-only view for `!isAdmin`), but `SettingsNav.jsx`'s existing access control already restricts the entire **Notifications & Logs** section (along with every other non-General section) to Admins only, so a Standard or Read Only user cannot reach this component today regardless of this change. Loosening that broader gate was out of scope for this task and not requested; the read-only branch is written defensively so the component is already correct if that gate is ever revisited. No corresponding line existed in `docs/backlog.md` for this task to remove — it was never tracked there as its own item.

**Tested:** `python3 -m py_compile` on every touched backend file (clean); `alembic upgrade head` against the real dev Postgres container applied migration `0045` cleanly on top of `0044`, seeding all 14 rows; `docker compose restart backend` — clean startup logs, no import errors; `vite build` — clean. Exercised the real API end-to-end with `curl` against the actual running dev server using a real Superadmin session token: confirmed all 14 `notif_*` rows via `GET /settings`; confirmed `GET /notifications/history` returns real, correctly-shaped data including dismissed entries; confirmed `PUT /settings/{key}` correctly rejects an out-of-range threshold (`400`, "must be one of: 70, 75, 80, 85, 90") and a non-boolean preference value (`400`, "must be 'true' or 'false'"); temporarily enabled Debt Tracking to exercise `debt_computed_fields`'s new required parameter end-to-end via `GET /debts/{id}` (confirmed `promo_expiring_soon=true` at the 30-day default, then confirmed it correctly flips to `false` after lowering `notif_promo_rate_warning_days` to 7 for the same real debt, 19 days from its actual promo end date), then restored the module to its original disabled state. Directly verified the preference gate end-to-end (not just by reading the code) by inserting a real critical `error_log` row, confirming `GET /dashboard/notifications` showed `critical_errors_detected` and a matching persistent notification was created for the Superadmin with `notif_critical_error=true`, then confirming both disappeared — with no new persistent notification row created — the moment the preference was switched to `false`; cleaned up the test error log row and its notifications afterward and restored the preference to `true`. Separately drove a real, driven browser session (Playwright, headless Chromium, the same throwaway-driver-script approach as prior sessions per this log) against the actual running dev server: toggled Balance warning off, saved, reloaded the page, and confirmed the toggle stayed off (real persistence, not local state); changed the Reconciliation overdue threshold to 7 days, saved, reloaded, and confirmed it stuck; restored both to their original values the same way; confirmed the module-gated preference/threshold rows (Promotional rate expiring, Debt milestones, Budget warning/threshold, Over budget) correctly appeared/disappeared as expected given this dev instance's actual enabled modules; confirmed **Export CSV** on the history table downloaded a real, correctly-formatted CSV; confirmed dark mode renders the whole tab correctly using the app's existing `kt-toggle`/`kt-settings-row`/`kt-severity-badge` styling, with no new components needed. No new browser console errors beyond the pre-existing, unrelated CSP `frame-ancestors`-via-`<meta>` warning and an expected 403 on an unset avatar request.

## 2026-08-08 — Polish: transaction rules for auto-categorisation

**Decision:** A transaction rule matching the extracted (or CSV-provided) supplier name always overrides AI's own category suggestion, applied in a single place — `services/ai_provider_service.py`'s `extract_invoice_data()` calls `services/rules_service.find_matching_rule()` on the result before returning it (via a private `_apply_rule_override` helper), so every caller (a normal upload, PDF bulk import, and the watched folder — all of which call `extract_invoice_data`) gets rule-based categorisation identically with no per-caller logic. CSV import is the one exception, since it has no AI step at all: `routers/imports.py`'s `import_csv` calls `rules_service.find_matching_rule()` directly, after `import_service.parse_row()` but before the row's Category-column match is used, so a rule wins there too.
**Rationale:** The task brief is explicit that rules must "always take priority" and "work even when AI is disabled" — centralising the override inside `extract_invoice_data` itself (rather than duplicating a rule-check at each of the three call sites) makes that guarantee structural rather than something three separate call sites each have to remember to do correctly, and matches this app's general preference for one obviously-correct place to put shared post-extraction logic. Whenever a rule fires, a line — `"Category set by rule: [rule name]"` — is appended to the invoice's `notes` field (there is no dedicated column for this), which is how `ReviewCard.jsx` recovers which rule applied (via a regex match against `invoice.notes`) to show the "Set by rule" badge next to the Category field; the badge disappears client-side the moment the category is changed away from what the rule set, without rewriting the stored notes text — a deliberate simplification, so a user's own edits to the free-text Notes field are never silently altered by the app.

**Decision:** Rule matching (`exact`, `contains`, `starts_with`) is case-insensitive throughout — both sides of every comparison in `rules_service._matches()` are lowercased before comparing.
**Rationale:** Supplier names are extracted by AI (or typed by hand on a CSV/manual entry) and are never guaranteed consistent casing invoice-to-invoice ("BT Broadband Ltd" vs. "bt broadband ltd" vs. "BT BROADBAND") — a case-sensitive rule would silently fail to match a supplier it was obviously written for, which is a worse failure mode than the rare false-positive risk of case-insensitive matching.

**Decision:** `rules_service.get_suggested_rules()` looks only at invoices whose category was changed via `PUT /invoices/{id}` *after* creation — detected by scanning `audit_log` for `action_type='invoice.edited'` rows against the `invoices` table whose `changed_fields` metadata includes `category_id` (the same metadata `routers/invoices.py`'s `update_invoice` already writes for every edit) — groups the resulting invoices by supplier, and only suggests a category once at least `MIN_SUGGESTION_OCCURRENCES` (2) invoices from that supplier agree on the same final category. Suppliers already covered by an existing active rule are filtered out before the suggestion list is built.
**Rationale:** A category a user picked *manually, after correcting AI's own guess* is a much stronger signal of "this is genuinely how this supplier should be categorised" than the category AI (or a CSV's Category column) assigned on the first pass — reusing the existing audit trail rather than adding a new tracking mechanism keeps this free of any new schema. Requiring at least two agreeing corrections (not one) avoids suggesting a rule off a single one-off recategorisation, which is as likely to be a mistake being fixed as a genuine pattern; the word "usually" in the suggestion copy ("supplier X is usually categorised as Y") only holds once there's more than one data point. Suggestions are capped at 10 and re-computed on every `GET /rules/suggestions` call rather than cached or persisted — the underlying audit log is small enough (90-day active window) that this is cheap, and it means a newly-created rule or a freshly corrected invoice is reflected immediately with no invalidation logic needed.

**Decision:** Rules are ordered by `priority` (integer, default 0, higher runs first) then by `id` for ties, and `find_matching_rule()` returns the first active rule (in that order) whose `match_value` matches — not the most specific one, and not every match combined.
**Rationale:** This is the same "explicit ordering beats implicit specificity-detection" approach the task brief asked for directly ("higher priority rules run first"). Auto-detecting "specificity" (e.g. preferring an `exact` match over a `contains` match, or a longer `match_value` over a shorter one) would be a reasonable-sounding heuristic but an implicit one a user can't see or reason about when two rules conflict; an explicit priority number is visible in the rules table, directly editable, and behaves predictably once understood — matching this app's Categories/Feature Modules pattern of preferring an explicit, inspectable setting over inferred behaviour wherever the two would otherwise disagree.

**Decision:** `DELETE /rules/{id}` is a soft delete via the existing `active` flag (no separate `deleted` column) — functionally identical to `POST /rules/{id}/toggle` turning a rule off, differing only in the audit action logged (`rule.deleted` vs. `rule.disabled`) — and `GET /rules` always returns every rule, active or not, for the Status column to display.
**Rationale:** Matches `DELETE /categories/{id}` (`routers/categories.py`'s `deactivate_category`), the existing precedent in this codebase for a "delete" action on a small reference table with no dependent-row cascade concerns: soft delete via a boolean the table already needs anyway, rather than adding a second flag whose only job would be distinguishing "user clicked Delete" from "user clicked Disable." The task brief's column list for `transaction_rules` (Part 1) does not include a `deleted` column, so this also avoids inventing schema beyond what was specified.

**Tested:** `python3 -c "import ast; ast.parse(...)"` on every touched/added backend file (clean). `alembic upgrade head` against the real dev Postgres container applied migration `0046` cleanly on top of `0045`. `docker compose restart backend` — clean startup logs, no import errors. Exercised the real API end-to-end with `curl` against the running dev server using a real Superadmin session token: created a real `BT` → Broadband (`contains`) rule via `POST /rules`; confirmed `POST /rules/test` correctly matched "BT Broadband Ltd" (returning the rule and its category) and correctly reported no match for "Corona Energy"; ran a real CSV import (`POST /imports/csv`) for a "BT Business" row whose own Category column said "Water," and confirmed the created invoice was categorised as Broadband instead with `notes` reading `"...\n\nCategory set by rule: BT Broadband"` — proving the rule overrides the CSV's own category text, not just AI's. Verified `GET /rules/suggestions` end-to-end: imported two invoices for a fresh supplier, manually recategorised both via `PUT /invoices/{id}` (the same audit-logged path a real user's correction takes), confirmed the suggestion appeared with `occurrence_count: 2`, created a rule from it, and confirmed the suggestion then disappeared (already covered by an active rule). Exercised `POST /rules/{id}/toggle` and `DELETE /rules/{id}` and confirmed `GET /rules` reflected active/inactive status correctly afterwards; confirmed `rule.created`/`rule.deleted` audit log entries were written correctly. Cleaned up all test invoices (soft-deleted) and the test rule (hard-deleted directly in the dev DB, since no such endpoint exists by design) afterwards, leaving only the real `BT` → Broadband rule requested by the task brief. Confirmed via Vite's dev transform endpoint that `RulesPage.jsx` and `rules.css` compile with no syntax errors, then drove a real headless-Chromium session (Playwright) against the actual running dev server: logged in as Superadmin, screenshotted `Settings → General` (confirmed the new Transaction Rules section renders correctly below Feature Modules with a working "Manage rules" link) and the Transaction Rules page itself (rules table, priority/match type/match value/category swatch/status/actions all correct); used the on-page Test tool for both a matching and non-matching supplier and confirmed the correct result text rendered each time; opened the Add rule form and the Edit modal (pre-filled correctly) and confirmed both match the app's existing `kt-modal`/`kt-field` styling. The `ReviewCard.jsx` badge logic (`RULE_NOTE_RE` regex extracting the rule name from `invoice.notes`) was verified directly against the real string the backend produced during the CSV import test above (`"Test invoice for rule verification\n\nCategory set by rule: BT Broadband"`), confirming the regex correctly extracts "BT Broadband" — full browser verification of the badge itself was not possible in this pass since AI extraction requires a real provider API key (this dev instance has only a placeholder key, per existing memory) and the badge is only reachable via the AI-driven upload/watched-folder paths, not the CSV path exercised here.

## 2026-08-08 — Polish: enhanced cash flow chart with net flow bars and rich tooltips

**Decision:** `services/financial_year_service.py`'s per-month breakdown loop (previously inline inside `build_summary`) is extracted into `_build_monthly_breakdown()`, which now also computes four new fields per month — `net_cashflow` (income minus spend), `cumulative_balance` (a running total starting from the FY's own `opening_balance`, accumulated from the FY's actual start date, not just the visible/`effective_start` date, so the first *visible* month's balance already reflects any hidden earlier activity), and `income_breakdown`/`spend_breakdown` (top 3 contribution groups / invoice categories that month, for the chart's rich tooltip). A new `monthly_breakdown_for_fy()` wraps the same helper for an arbitrary financial year, and a new `GET /dashboard/monthly-breakdown?period=current|previous` endpoint (`routers/dashboard.py`) exposes it standalone.
**Rationale:** `build_summary` is called on every dashboard load and already had all the pieces (invoice/contribution totals, category names) the new fields needed — bolting the calculation onto the existing loop, rather than a second pass over the same data, keeps the extra cost to a single dict build per month. A standalone endpoint (rather than accepting a `financial_year_id` param on `/dashboard/summary` itself) exists because the rest of that payload — metric cards, notifications, planned projects, debt/budget fields — only ever makes sense for the *current* FY; letting the chart request a different FY's figures without dragging the whole page's data model into "which FY am I describing right now" avoided a much larger, riskier change for a feature that only touches one chart.

**Decision:** `get_previous_financial_year()` finds the prior FY by shifting `today` back exactly one year (`date(today.year - 1, today.month, min(today.day, 28))`, clamped so Feb 29 never overflows into March) and running it through the existing `get_or_create_financial_year()` lookup — not by decrementing the FY id or parsing the label.
**Rationale:** FYs are keyed by a computed label (`fy_bounds_for`), not a sequential relationship to each other, and the app already lazily creates a FY row the first time anything asks for it (see the 2026-08-04 dashboard build entry) — reusing that same lazy-create path for "one year back" is consistent with how every other FY lookup in the app already works, and sidesteps having to reconstruct FY boundaries by hand for a start month that's configurable per deployment.

**Decision:** The net cash flow bars use their own independent Y-scale — a fixed zero baseline at the chart's vertical midpoint, magnitude scaled against the largest `|net_cashflow|` across the visible months — completely separate from the pound-value scale the four lines (income/spend/forecast/running balance) share.
**Rationale:** A literal shared scale would mean a bar's height is set by how big income/spend happen to be that year, not by how good or bad that specific month was — in an org with a large opening balance and modest month-to-month swings, every bar would end up a sliver at the very bottom of a shared axis, defeating the "immediate visual clarity on which months were positive and which were negative" goal from the task brief. A dedicated scale, and a fixed midline rather than one anchored to the lines' own zero (which may sit at the very bottom of the chart if the running balance never goes negative), guarantees both surplus (up) and deficit (down) bars always have equal, legible room regardless of the data shape. The lines' own scale was separately generalised to support a negative domain (previously assumed everything was ≥0), since the new optional running-balance line can genuinely go negative for an org running a real deficit.

**Decision:** The cumulative running balance line is off by default (toggled on via the legend), while the three original lines stay on.
**Rationale:** Matches the task brief's explicit instruction, and avoids changing the chart's default appearance for existing users — the balance line's absolute values (which include the FY's opening balance) are typically a different order of magnitude from income/spend/forecast, so showing it by default would immediately rescale the Y-axis and make the three lines everyone already relies on harder to read at a glance. It's one click away for anyone who wants the "am I trending up or down" view.

**Decision:** On mobile (≤640px), the net cash flow bars and the cumulative balance line drop out entirely (not just visually de-emphasised), the legend renders after the chart instead of before it, and a tap sets the hovered month instead of a mouse hover — with the previous `display: none` on the mobile tooltip removed, since tap is now the primary way to read a month's figures on that breakpoint rather than something the tooltip actively hid.
**Rationale:** A diverging bar layer and a fourth line both need real pixel width to stay legible, which a phone-width chart doesn't have — rather than shrinking everything until it's unreadable, the mobile view keeps the three lines the task brief calls out as the minimum ("just income vs spend lines") legible instead. Touch devices have no hover state, so the previous version simply hid the tooltip below 520px; that meant the richest content on the chart (the breakdown-by-source/category tooltip) was invisible exactly where users are most likely to want a quick glance. Detecting touch via `matchMedia('(hover: none)')` rather than screen width means a touch laptop or a desktop with only a touchscreen still gets tap behaviour, and a mouse-and-keyboard tablet still gets hover — matching capability, not device class.

**Decision:** Legend items (Income, Actual spend, Forecast spend, Surplus month, Deficit month, Running balance) now toggle that series' visibility on click, replacing their previous behaviour of navigating to the series' detail page (Contributions / filtered Invoices / Forecast breakdown). Clicking directly on a line *in the chart body* still navigates, unchanged.
**Rationale:** The task brief is explicit that legend items should "show/hide that element", which is a different action to what the legend previously did — keeping both behaviours on the same click target isn't possible, so the drill-through action moved to the chart body itself (which already computed "nearest series by Y-distance" for its own click handler) rather than being dropped. The panel subtitle text was updated to describe both affordances so neither is undiscoverable.

## 2026-08-09 — Polish: complete PDF report redesign with improved charts and visual hierarchy

**Decision:** Report charts (`services/pdf_report_service.py`) are now rendered with matplotlib and embedded as PNG images, replacing ReportLab's native `VerticalBarChart`/`HorizontalBarChart` graphics entirely — with `matplotlib.rcParams['font.family']`/`font.sans-serif`/`font.size` pinned once at import time, before any figure is created. `matplotlib==3.11.1` was added to `backend/requirements.txt`.
**Rationale:** The task brief's own font-consistency instruction ("all chart axes, labels, and titles use this font setting") only has teeth if every chart goes through the same rendering path — ReportLab's native chart graphics can't have their font set precisely enough to visually match the Helvetica body text, which was the root cause of the "font mismatch" complaint. Converting the forecast chart too (not just the two new current-year charts and the year-on-year chart the brief itemises) was a deliberate extension beyond the brief's Parts 1–9 list, since leaving one chart on the old renderer would have reintroduced the exact inconsistency this task set out to fix.

**Decision:** The "current financial year" for the Part 4 charts is the most recent window in `data["annual_totals"]` (i.e. the FY containing the report's own `date_to`), not literally today's calendar FY.
**Rationale:** `pdf_report_service.py` has no DB session — it only ever receives the pre-built `data` dict — and `report_service.build_report_data()` already computes exactly this window's per-category totals as the last entry in `annual_totals`. Recomputing "today's FY" independently would need a new DB query plumbed through a service that is deliberately DB-free by design, for a value report_service already has ready to use; for a report whose date range extends through the present (the normal case), this window *is* today's FY.
