**Decision:** Margins were changed from 2cm to 27mm (`PAGE_MARGIN = 2.7 * cm`) to match the brief's explicit `CONTENT_WIDTH = A4 width - 54mm` spec, even though the brief itself described 27mm as "consistent with current" — the actual prior value was 20mm.
**Rationale:** The brief's content-width arithmetic (210mm − 54mm = 156mm) only works at 27mm margins; treating the explicit number as authoritative over the brief's incorrect aside about the starting point avoids shipping a report whose printable width doesn't match its own spec.

**Decision:** Section headings (`_SectionHeading` flowable) achieve literal PDF letter-spacing via `PDFTextObject.setCharSpace()` (through `canvas.beginText()`/`drawText()`), not `Canvas.setCharSpace()` — the latter doesn't exist; the char-space operator is only exposed on a text object in ReportLab's API. "Small caps" is approximated as plain uppercase text, since ReportLab has no true small-caps rendering. The heading's text is never auto-uppercased by the class itself — callers pass already-cased text — because some headings embed a financial year label (e.g. "FY2025/26 (Sep–Aug)") whose "Sep–Aug" casing must survive.
**Rationale:** True per-character letter-spacing needed the lower-level text-object API rather than the usual `drawString()`; discovered by a runtime `AttributeError` during testing and fixed by moving to `beginText()`. Auto-uppercasing at the flowable level was ruled out after the first render showed a current-year heading rendering as "CURRENT YEAR — FY2025/26 (SEP–AUG)", which loses the FY label's established formatting.

**Decision:** The two per-status colour-coded tables (Project summary, Budget vs. actual) use wider Status columns (23–25% of content width) than an initial pass used (10–14%), taking width from the money columns.
**Rationale:** Bold status text ("Over budget", "In progress") wrapped mid-word at the narrower widths in the first rendered test PDF — verified by generating a real report with synthetic data and rendering every page to a PNG for visual review, not just by reading the layout code.

**Decision:** The logo on the cover page is redrawn natively with ReportLab shapes (`Rect`/`Circle`/`Path`), copying the exact geometry from `frontend/src/components/Logo.jsx`'s inline SVG (3 ascending bars in `#2D6B9F`, a `#1D9E75` tick badge), rather than rasterising or embedding an image asset.
**Rationale:** Keeps the cover page fully vector (crisp at any print resolution) and dependency-free — no asset file to keep in sync with the frontend component if the logo ever changes. SVG's top-left-origin coordinates were flipped (`48 - y`) to ReportLab's bottom-left origin by hand for each shape.

**Decision:** The cover's "Generated by" line now uses `user.display_name or user.username` (`routers/reports.py`), instead of always `user.username`; `generate_report_pdf()`'s parameter name (`generated_by_username`) was left unchanged since the identical name is also a public `ReportOut` API field (`models/schemas.py`) and a same-named parameter on the unrelated `export_pdf_service.py` (used by CSV/table exports) — renaming it would have implied a wider contract change that wasn't asked for.
**Rationale:** The brief calls for "[display_name or username]" specifically; changing only the value passed at the one call site that needed it kept the change minimal and avoided touching the sibling export-PDF code path or the API schema.

**Not addressed in this pass:** The brief's "Body text: 10pt, leading 15pt, mid-grey (#5F5E5A)" instruction was applied to prose paragraphs only (AI narrative, trends, looking-ahead, empty-state notices) — table cell text stays dark (`#2C2C2A`) for data-table legibility, since the brief's phrasing reads as describing running prose rather than tabular data.

**Tested:** `python3 -m py_compile` and `python3 -m pyflakes` on the rewritten file (clean, no unused imports/names). Built an isolated venv (Python 3.14, matching this machine; the app itself targets the Dockerfile's Python 3.12) with `reportlab==4.2.5`, `matplotlib==3.11.1`, and `pymupdf` for rendering, since no backend venv exists locally (dev normally runs via `docker-compose`) and this service has no DB dependency of its own. Generated two full synthetic reports directly against `generate_report_pdf()` (bypassing the DB/`report_service` layer entirely, since the function only needs the documented `data` dict shape): a 10-page "combined" report with 7 categories, 3 FY windows, budget/project/funding data, actual-vs-forecast months, and a positive net position; and a second 6-page "historical" edge-case report with 1 FY window (to exercise the year-on-year fallback message), a negative net position, no site name override, `None` funding/budget data, and an empty planned-projects list — confirming no crash and correct guard behaviour throughout. Rendered every page of both PDFs to PNG via PyMuPDF and visually reviewed each one, which caught and fixed two real bugs before sign-off: the forecast chart's legend overlapping its first bar (fixed with explicit Y-axis headroom) and the Status columns wrapping bold text mid-word (fixed by widening them). Confirmed: cover page shows the ReportLab-drawn logo with no redundant text and a "Generated by [name] on [date]" line with no time component; the Summary page shows the three colour-coded metric cards and the light-blue key-insights box; the Current Year page shows two full-width horizontal bar charts (blue spend-by-category, green monthly-average) sharing matplotlib's pinned Helvetica font; the Year on Year page shows the slope chart with visibly crossing lines (Maintenance rising, Electricity falling) and correctly falls back to the "not enough historical data" message at 1 FY window; every table (Overview, Project summary, Budget vs. actual, Funding position) has the dark navy header row; and the footer (`[site] — Keep Track` / `Page X of Y`) renders correctly on every non-cover page while the cover itself has none.

**Tested:** `python3 -c "import ast; ast.parse(...)"` on every touched backend file (clean). `docker compose restart backend` — clean startup, no import errors. Exercised `GET /dashboard/summary` and the new `GET /dashboard/monthly-breakdown?period=current|previous` end-to-end via `curl` against the real running dev server with a genuine Superadmin session token: confirmed every month in `monthly_breakdown` carries correct `net_cashflow`, `cumulative_balance`, `income_breakdown`, and `spend_breakdown` values matching the underlying invoice/contribution data by hand (e.g. Aug 2026: income £693.50 against three real contribution groups, spend £5,381.56 against three real categories, `net_cashflow` −£4,688.06, `cumulative_balance` £28,106.02); confirmed `period=previous` correctly resolves and lazily creates the prior FY row, and correctly returns an empty `monthly_breakdown` in this dev instance specifically because its configured `app_start_date` post-dates the whole of the previous FY — the same `effective_start` rule that already hides pre-tracking months from the current-year chart, applied consistently. Frontend changes verified with a real headless-Chromium (Playwright) session against the actual running dev server, logged in as Superadmin: screenshotted the desktop chart and confirmed green (surplus) and red (deficit) bars render behind the lines; hovered a month and confirmed the rich tooltip shows income with its top sources, expenses with its top categories, a signed Net line, and the running Balance; clicked the "Running balance" legend item and confirmed the grey dashed cumulative line appears and the axis rescales to fit it; clicked "Actual spend" and confirmed the green line and its area both disappear while the legend label dims; clicked "Last year" and confirmed the chart correctly shows its empty state for this dev instance's data (see above) and "This year" correctly switches back; resized to a 375px mobile viewport and confirmed the bars and running-balance line are absent, only income/spend/forecast lines remain, and the legend renders below the chart rather than above it. `console --errors` after each step showed only one pre-existing, unrelated `403` on `/debts/terminology` (a Debt Tracking permission check, not touched by this task) and a benign CSP `frame-ancestors`-in-meta-tag warning already present before this change.

## 2026-08-10 — Fix: stale-reconciliation grammar and false-positive staleness

**Decision:** The stale-reconciliation dashboard notification (`routers/dashboard.py`'s `get_notifications`) now interpolates a count-agreeing verb — `"needs"` for a count of 1, `"need"` otherwise — instead of always saying "need", and the underlying count query (`stale_reconciliation_count`) is now scoped to the current financial year (`MonthlyReconciliation.financial_year_id == current_fy.id`) rather than every financial year ever recorded.
**Rationale:** "1 reconciled month need review" is a grammatical error the task brief called out directly. The FY scoping fixes a real false-positive source found while investigating: a reconciliation left stale in a financial year nobody is actively working in any more (e.g. closed out a year ago) would otherwise keep counting toward — and surfacing on — every dashboard load indefinitely, with no way for that count to ever naturally go to zero short of an Admin manually revisiting old history. Both the live banner and the persistent notification-centre copy share this one message string, so the single fix covers both surfaces the brief asked about.

**Decision:** `services/reconciliation_service.py`'s `mark_reconciliation_stale()` gained an `is_historical: bool = False` keyword-only parameter that makes the whole call a no-op when true, and every call site that stems from an `Invoice` (`routers/invoices.py`'s `update_invoice`/`confirm_invoice`/`delete_invoice`) now passes `is_historical=invoice.is_historical`. The CSV import path (`routers/imports.py`'s `import_csv`) had its per-month `mark_reconciliation_stale` loop removed outright (every CSV-imported invoice is unconditionally historical, so the loop was guaranteed to no-op under the new rule), and the watched-folder import (`services/folder_watcher_service.py`) had its `mark_reconciliation_stale` call removed from the `if is_historical:` branch, keeping only the `get_or_create_financial_year()` side effect that branch also relied on.
**Rationale:** This was the concrete cause of the "false positive even when nothing genuinely needs attention" bug in the task brief's part 4: a CSV or watched-folder import backfilling old invoices — dated before the app's own `app_start_date`, by definition — was flagging already-reconciled months as changed, even though the Admin who reconciled that month worked from real bank records that never included (and never should include) that historical data. Centralising the guard inside `mark_reconciliation_stale()` itself, rather than only fixing the two obviously-wrong call sites, means any future caller inherits the same correct behaviour by default instead of needing to remember this rule independently. Contributions have no `is_historical` concept (the model has no such column), so their three call sites in `routers/contributions.py` were left passing the parameter's default of `False`, unchanged.

**Decision:** A new `POST /reconciliation/{id}/review` endpoint (Admin-only, `require_admin`) clears `is_stale`/`stale_reason`/`stale_since` on a reconciliation without touching `actual_balance`, `discrepancy`, `edited_by`, `edited_at`, or `edit_reason` — distinct from the existing `PUT /reconciliation/{id}` path, which only clears staleness as a side effect of an Admin actually re-entering a corrected `actual_balance` (and requires `edit_reason` when they do). The frontend (`ReconciliationPage.jsx`) surfaces this as a "Mark as reviewed" button next to the existing "Edit now" button whenever a reconciliation is stale, calling it via a new `reconciliationApi.review()` in `utils/api.js`.
**Rationale:** The task brief asked for a way to clear a false-positive stale flag "without requiring the Admin to re-enter the actual balance" — the existing edit path structurally couldn't do that, since its whole `is_stale = False` side effect only fires inside the `payload.actual_balance is not None` branch, which itself demands an `edit_reason`. A separate endpoint keeps that existing correction flow's audit semantics (`reconciliation.edited`, tracked via `edited_by`/`edited_at`/`edit_reason`) intact and unambiguous — a "we checked, it's fine" review is not the same event as "we found and corrected an error," so it gets its own `reconciliation.reviewed` audit action instead of being folded into `edited_by`, which would otherwise misrepresent an unchanged reconciliation as having been corrected.

**Not addressed in this pass:** `calculated_balance_for_month()` sums contributions/invoices cumulatively from the financial year's start through the target month (not just within that single month), which means a new invoice dated in an earlier month can, mathematically, also shift the calculated balance of every later already-reconciled month in the same FY — but every `mark_reconciliation_stale()` call site only ever flags the one month matching the changed record's own date. This is a false-*negative* gap (a later month that's actually stale but never gets flagged), the opposite of what this task's brief asked to fix, and changing it would mean re-flagging potentially many months per single edit — judged out of scope here and left as-is.

**Tested:** `python3 -c "import ast; ast.parse(...)"` on every touched backend file (clean — `routers/dashboard.py`, `routers/reconciliation.py`, `routers/invoices.py`, `routers/imports.py`, `services/reconciliation_service.py`, `services/folder_watcher_service.py`). `npx vite build` on the frontend (clean production build, no new warnings beyond the pre-existing chunk-size and `%VITE_API_BASE_URL%` ones). A full runtime backend import/smoke test wasn't possible in this environment — no working Python venv with the backend's dependencies exists here (`psycopg2-binary` fails to build from source on this machine's Python 3.14 with no local Postgres `pg_config`), and there is no existing `backend/tests/` suite to run instead — so this pass relied on careful manual re-reading of every diff (call-site signatures, unused imports removed, `is_historical` plumbed correctly end to end) rather than an executed test. Flagged here explicitly rather than claiming an end-to-end verification that didn't happen.

## 2026-08-10 — Fix: duplicate financial year creation after system reset (UniqueViolation)

**Decision:** `services/financial_year_service.py`'s `get_or_create_financial_year()` now looks up the existing row by label AND start_date AND end_date together (not label alone), and its create branch wraps the `INSERT` in `try`/`except IntegrityError`: on conflict it rolls back and re-fetches by label — the column the table's actual `UNIQUE` constraint lives on — returning whatever row won the race instead of raising. If no row is found even after that re-fetch (meaning the `IntegrityError` wasn't actually this race), the exception is re-raised rather than swallowed.
**Rationale:** Root cause, confirmed by reproducing it against a real SQLite engine (see Tested below): the previous check-then-insert had no protection against two callers finding the table empty and both trying to `INSERT` the same label — the second commit hit the `label` `UNIQUE` constraint and raised `IntegrityError`, surfacing to whichever request hit it as an unhandled 500. This function is called from many places that can genuinely run concurrently — a single dashboard load fans out to `/dashboard/summary`, `/dashboard/notifications`, and (when enabled) budget/debt dashboard fields, each opening its own request-scoped session, plus `budget_notification_service`'s daily background-thread check — and a system reset guarantees the table starts empty, which is exactly the condition needed for several of those callers to race each other the moment the dashboard (or setup wizard's own completion step) is next loaded. Matching on the full label+date-range triple on the read side (rather than label alone) is an extra correctness guard against a stale row surviving with a label that no longer matches its own computed bounds (e.g. if `financial_year_start_month` changed between when a row was created and now — see the file's own top-of-module note that a new start month produces a new label rather than rewriting old rows); recovering by label alone on the conflict path is deliberate, since label is the only column the database itself enforces uniqueness on.

**Investigated, no change needed:** `system_reset_service.py`'s `perform_reset()` already deletes every table with a `financial_year_id` foreign key (`BudgetNotificationSent`, `SavingsGoal`, `CategoryBudget`, `MonthlyReconciliation`, `Contribution`, `Invoice`, `PlannedProject`) before deleting `FinancialYear` itself, all inside one transaction committed once at the end — confirmed by reading the deletion order directly (this was the subject of the prior "system reset deletion order includes all tables" fix). There is no APScheduler (or any other job queue) in this codebase to "pause" — `budget_notification_service` and `debt_notification_service` are plain `threading.Thread` loops that `time.sleep()` between fixed intervals (24 hours) with one same-thread call at app startup, not per-reset; `perform_reset()` itself runs synchronously inside the reset request handler, so there's no window where a background thread's write can interleave with reset's own uncommitted deletes. The actual trigger is simpler and more common than a reset/scheduler race specifically: any two of the ordinary concurrent callers listed above landing on the empty post-reset table at once. Pausing one specific background thread around reset would only have closed one of several interleavings that can produce this crash; fixing the shared function itself closes all of them, including the everyday case of two browser tabs or a page's own parallel `fetch` calls.

**Tested:** `python3 -m py_compile services/financial_year_service.py` (clean). Reproduced the exact bug and verified the fix against a real database engine — no full backend venv exists in this environment (per the previous entry's note about `psycopg2-binary`), so a throwaway venv with only `sqlalchemy` installed (no compiled dependencies needed) was used against SQLite, whose `UNIQUE` constraint and `IntegrityError` behaviour match Postgres for this purpose. Defined a minimal `FinancialYear` table (label `UNIQUE`, matching the real schema) and drove two independent `Session` objects through the actual race window — both issuing their SELECT and finding nothing, exactly as two concurrent requests would against an empty post-reset table — before letting each attempt its `INSERT`: confirmed the *old* create logic raises `IntegrityError: UNIQUE constraint failed: financial_years.label` on the second session's commit, reproducing the reported crash exactly; confirmed the *new* logic instead returns the first session's row with no exception; and separately confirmed a single caller against a still-empty table (the fresh-install / post-reset-with-only-one-caller case) still creates a new row correctly. Did not run the actual FastAPI app, the real Postgres-backed reset endpoint, or the frontend setup wizard end-to-end, since no working backend environment is available here — noted explicitly rather than claimed.
